sci|1|l1-1|k-2|A Push Makes It Go — A push moves a thing away from you.
sci|1|l1-2|k-2|A Pull Makes It Come — A pull moves a thing toward you.
sci|1|l1-3|k-2|Making It Stop — A push or a pull can stop a moving thing.
sci|1|l1-4|k-2|Big Push, Little Push — A bigger push makes a bigger change.
sci|1|l1-5|k-2|Changing Direction — A push or a pull can change which way a thing goes.
sci|1|l1-6|k-2|Ball Meets Ball — Things that touch can push each other.
sci|1|l1-7|k-2|Ramps and Heavy Boxes — A push can solve a problem, like moving a heavy box.
sci|1|l2-1|k-2|Hard, Soft, Rough, Smooth — You can describe a material by how it looks and feels.
sci|1|l2-2|k-2|Does It Bend? Does It Soak? — Some materials bend and some soak up water.
sci|1|l2-3|k-2|Sorting Things — We can sort things into groups by their properties.
sci|1|l2-4|k-2|A Raincoat and a Window — We pick a material because of its properties.
sci|1|l2-5|k-2|Solids and Liquids — A solid keeps its shape. A liquid takes the shape of its cup.
sci|1|l2-6|k-2|Small Pieces, Big Things — A big thing can be built from small pieces and taken apart again.
sci|1|l2-7|k-2|Heat It, Cool It — Heating and cooling change things. Some changes go back. Some do not.
sci|10|l22-1|6-8|Everything Is Made of Atoms — All matter is built from atoms, which are far too small to see but real enough to measure.
sci|10|l22-2|6-8|Molecules and Extended Structures — Atoms bond together either in small fixed groups called molecules or in huge repeating patterns called extended structures.
sci|10|l22-3|6-8|Pure Substances and Mixtures — A pure substance has one kind of particle throughout, while a mixture holds several kinds that can be separated without a chemical change.
sci|10|l22-4|6-8|Solids, Liquids and Gases — The three common states of matter differ in how tightly particles are held and how freely they move.
sci|10|l22-5|6-8|Temperature and Thermal Energy — Temperature measures how fast particles are moving on average, while thermal energy is the total motion energy of all the particles in an object.
sci|10|l22-6|6-8|Changes of State — Melting, boiling, freezing and condensing are the same particles gaining or losing enough energy to change how they move.
sci|10|l22-7|6-8|Energy In, Energy Out — While a substance is changing state, added energy goes into breaking particles loose, so the temperature holds steady until the change is done.
sci|10|l22-8|6-8|Density: How Tightly Packed — Density is mass divided by volume, and it explains why some things float and others sink.
sci|10|l22-9|6-8|The Periodic Table — The periodic table arranges every element by the number of protons in its atoms, and elements in the same column behave in similar ways.
sci|10|l22-10|6-8|The Limits of the Microscope — A light microscope cannot show anything much smaller than the wavelength of light, so atoms need other tools.
sci|10|l22-11|6-8|Evidence That Atoms Are Real — Brownian motion, explained by Einstein and measured by Perrin, showed that molecules are real objects whose size can be calculated.
sci|10|l23-1|6-8|Physical Changes — In a physical change the substance looks different but is still made of the same molecules.
sci|10|l23-2|6-8|Signs of a Chemical Change — In a chemical change atoms rearrange to form new substances, and clues like gas, color change, heat or a new solid can tell you it happened.
sci|10|l23-3|6-8|What Fire Really Is — Fire is combustion: a fuel combines rapidly with oxygen, forming new gases and releasing energy as heat and light.
sci|10|l23-4|6-8|Reactants and Products — A chemical equation lists the reactants that go in and the products that come out, and every atom on one side appears on the other.
sci|10|l23-5|6-8|Mass Is Conserved — Because atoms are only rearranged in a reaction, the total mass of the products always equals the total mass of the reactants.
sci|10|l23-6|6-8|Reactions Around You — Everyday reactions mostly fit three patterns: synthesis joins substances, decomposition breaks one apart, and combustion combines a fuel with oxygen.
sci|10|l23-7|6-8|Reactions That Give Off Energy — An exothermic reaction releases thermal energy to its surroundings, so things around it get warmer.
sci|10|l23-8|6-8|Reactions That Take In Energy — An endothermic process absorbs thermal energy from its surroundings, so things around it get colder.
sci|10|l23-9|6-8|Design a Warmer or a Cooler — Engineers choose a reaction, then test and adjust amounts and materials to meet criteria within constraints.
sci|10|l23-10|6-8|Safety in the Lab — Because reactions make new substances and move energy, lab safety means protecting eyes and skin, using small amounts, and never mixing unknowns.
sci|10|l23-11|6-8|Stopping a Fire — Every way of putting out a fire removes one side of the fire triangle: the fuel, the oxygen or the heat.
sci|11|l24-1|6-8|Pushes, Pulls and Net Force — A force is a push or a pull with a size and a direction, and only the total, or net, force changes how something moves.
sci|11|l24-2|6-8|Motion Keeps Going — Newton's first law: with no net force, a resting object stays at rest and a moving object keeps moving at the same speed in a straight line.
sci|11|l24-3|6-8|Force, Mass and Acceleration — Newton's second law: a net force makes an object accelerate, and the acceleration is bigger with more force and smaller with more mass.
sci|11|l24-4|6-8|Every Push Pushes Back — Newton's third law: forces come in pairs, so when one object pushes on another, the second pushes back equally hard in the opposite direction.
sci|11|l24-5|6-8|Why a Sudden Stop Hurts — Stopping a moving object takes force, and the faster it stops, the bigger the force must be.
sci|11|l24-6|6-8|Helmets, Belts and Crumple Zones — Safety gear is designed to make a collision last longer and spread the force out, so the force on any one part of the body is smaller.
sci|11|l24-7|6-8|Planning a Fair Test — A fair test changes only one variable at a time, keeps everything else the same, and repeats trials so the result can be trusted.
sci|11|l24-8|6-8|Weight Is a Force — Weight is the force of gravity on an object's mass, so mass stays the same everywhere but weight changes from planet to planet.
sci|11|l24-9|6-8|Mass, Distance and Orbits — The pull of gravity gets stronger with more mass and weaker with more distance, and that pull is what keeps moons and planets in their orbits.
sci|11|l24-10|6-8|Electric Charge at a Distance — Electric forces come from charge; like charges push apart, opposite charges pull together, and the force acts without touching.
sci|11|l24-11|6-8|Magnets, Fields and Electromagnets — Magnets push and pull through space using fields, and an electric current makes a magnetic field too, which lets us build electromagnets we can switch on and off.
sci|11|l25-1|6-8|Kinetic Energy Grows with Speed — Kinetic energy is the energy of motion; it grows with mass and grows even faster with speed, because speed is squared.
sci|11|l25-2|6-8|Stored Energy: Height, Springs and Fields — Potential energy is stored energy that depends on position, such as height above the ground, a stretched spring or charges held apart.
sci|11|l25-3|6-8|The Roller Coaster Trade — Energy is never created or destroyed; on a coaster, height turns into speed and back again, and friction slowly turns some of it into heat.
sci|11|l25-4|6-8|Temperature Is Not Heat — Temperature measures how fast particles move on average, while thermal energy is the total motion energy of all the particles in an object.
sci|11|l25-5|6-8|Heat Flows Hot to Cold — Heat always flows from a warmer object to a cooler one until they reach the same temperature, and friction is one way to make it.
sci|11|l25-6|6-8|Conduction Through Touching Things — Conduction is heat moving through a material by particles bumping their neighbors; metals conduct well, and foam, wool and air conduct poorly.
sci|11|l25-7|6-8|Convection Moves the Fluid Itself — Convection carries heat by moving warm liquid or gas from place to place, driving everything from a boiling pot to the lake breeze in Chicago.
sci|11|l25-8|6-8|Radiation Needs No Matter — Radiation carries heat as invisible infrared light, which needs no material to travel through and can cross empty space.
sci|11|l25-9|6-8|Designing a Drink Keeper — To keep a drink hot or cold, a design must slow conduction, convection and radiation at the same time, and a fair test shows which design works best.
sci|11|l25-10|6-8|Calories Are Energy — Food stores chemical energy that your body releases to move, grow and stay warm, and the Calorie on a label is a unit of that energy.
sci|11|l25-11|6-8|Fuels and Their Trade-offs — Fuels store chemical energy that we release as heat and motion, and every fuel choice involves trade-offs in cost, convenience and effects on the environment.
sci|12|l26-1|6-8|Energy Moves, the Stuff Stays — A wave carries energy from place to place, but the material it travels through only moves back and forth.
sci|12|l26-2|6-8|Amplitude, Wavelength, Frequency — Amplitude tells how much energy a wave carries, wavelength is the distance between repeats, and frequency is how many repeats pass each second.
sci|12|l26-3|6-8|Reading the Wave Bench — Wave speed equals frequency times wavelength, so measuring any two of them tells you the third.
sci|12|l26-4|6-8|Sound Needs Something to Push — Sound is a mechanical wave: it travels only by pushing through a medium, so it cannot cross empty space.
sci|12|l26-5|6-8|How Fast Sound Travels — Sound moves at about 343 meters per second in air, faster in water, and faster still in steel, because stiffer, denser media pass pushes along more quickly.
sci|12|l26-6|6-8|How the Ear Hears — The ear turns air pressure waves into vibrations of the eardrum, then into nerve signals inside the cochlea.
sci|12|l26-7|6-8|Light Needs No Medium — Light is a wave that can cross empty space and travels about 300,000 kilometers per second, far faster than sound.
sci|12|l26-8|6-8|Bounce, Soak, Pass Through — When light meets a material it can reflect, be absorbed, or be transmitted, and the mix decides what we see.
sci|12|l26-9|6-8|Refraction: Light Bends — Light changes speed when it enters a new material, and that change of speed bends its path.
sci|12|l26-10|6-8|The Eye Is a Camera — The eye's cornea and lens refract light to form an image on the retina, where cells turn light into nerve signals.
sci|12|l26-11|6-8|Glasses Fix the Focus — Nearsighted and farsighted eyes focus light in the wrong place, and a lens of the right shape moves the focus onto the retina.
sci|12|l27-1|6-8|What a Lens Does — A convex lens refracts parallel light rays to a focal point, and objects placed near it appear magnified.
sci|12|l27-2|6-8|Inside a Microscope — A compound microscope uses two lenses in a row, so the magnification of one is multiplied by the magnification of the other.
sci|12|l27-3|6-8|Telescopes: Lenses vs Mirrors — Early telescopes used lenses; large modern telescopes use curved mirrors, which can be built far bigger and gather far more light.
sci|12|l27-4|6-8|Light Beyond the Rainbow — Visible light is only a small slice of a much wider family of electromagnetic waves, discovered one piece at a time.
sci|12|l27-5|6-8|Waves We Use Every Day — Different parts of the spectrum do different jobs, from radio for broadcasting to X-rays for seeing bones, based on what each wavelength passes through or is absorbed by.
sci|12|l27-6|6-8|Seeing in Other Light — Telescopes that detect infrared, radio or X-rays reveal objects and events that visible light cannot show.
sci|12|l27-7|6-8|Analog and Digital — An analog signal copies a wave's shape directly, while a digital signal turns it into numbers, which resist noise and copy perfectly.
sci|12|l27-8|6-8|How a Phone Sends a Picture — A phone turns a picture into millions of numbers, sends them as bits over radio waves and cables, and the receiving phone rebuilds the image from the numbers.
sci|12|l27-9|6-8|Light in a Glass Thread — Optical fiber traps light inside a hair-thin strand of glass by total internal reflection and carries digital signals across oceans.
sci|12|l27-10|6-8|Sonar and the Bat — Sonar and echolocation send out sound and time the echo, using the speed of sound to turn delay into distance.
sci|12|l27-11|6-8|Radar and the Weather Map — Radar sends radio waves and times the echo, and the tiny frequency shift of a moving target reveals its speed, which is how weather radar tracks storms.
sci|13|l28-1|6-8|Every Living Thing Is Made of Cells — All living things are made of one or more cells, and every cell comes from an earlier cell.
sci|13|l28-2|6-8|Through the Microscope — A microscope bends light through lenses to make tiny things look large enough to study.
sci|13|l28-3|6-8|One Cell or Many — Some living things are a single cell that does everything; others are many cells that share the work.
sci|13|l28-4|6-8|The Membrane and the Nucleus — The cell membrane controls what enters and leaves, and the nucleus holds the instructions.
sci|13|l28-5|6-8|Mitochondria: Where Fuel Becomes Energy — Mitochondria break down food using oxygen to release energy the cell can use.
sci|13|l28-6|6-8|Plant Cells: Chloroplasts and Walls — Plant cells have chloroplasts that capture light and a stiff cell wall that gives them shape.
sci|13|l28-7|6-8|Cells, Tissues, Organs, Systems — Cells group into tissues, tissues build organs, and organs work together as systems.
sci|13|l28-8|6-8|Digestion and Blood Delivery — The digestive system breaks food into small pieces, and the circulatory system carries them to every cell.
sci|13|l28-9|6-8|Breathing In, Breathing Out — The respiratory system brings oxygen into the blood and removes carbon dioxide from it.
sci|13|l28-10|6-8|Muscles and Bones — The skeletal system gives the body its frame, and the muscular system moves it by pulling on bones.
sci|13|l28-11|6-8|Nerves and Control — The nervous system senses the world, carries signals, and tells the other systems what to do.
sci|13|l28-12|6-8|One Body, Many Systems — No body system works alone; each depends on the others to keep every cell alive.
sci|13|l29-1|6-8|Making Food From Light — Plants use light energy to combine carbon dioxide and water into sugar, releasing oxygen.
sci|13|l29-2|6-8|Getting Energy Out of Food — Cellular respiration breaks sugar down with oxygen to release energy, the reverse of photosynthesis.
sci|13|l29-3|6-8|The Willow and the Mint — Careful experiments showed that plants build themselves from air and water, and that they restore the air animals use up.
sci|13|l29-4|6-8|Sensory Receptors — Sensory receptors are cells that turn light, sound, chemicals, pressure, or heat into nerve signals.
sci|13|l29-5|6-8|Signals to the Brain — Nerve signals travel along neurons to the brain, which processes them and sends back commands.
sci|13|l29-6|6-8|Memory and Learning — Experiences change the connections between neurons, and those changes are memories that guide behavior.
sci|13|l29-7|6-8|Courtship and Display — Many animals use signals, songs, and dances to attract a mate, which increases their chances of having offspring.
sci|13|l29-8|6-8|Flowers and Pollinators — Flowers are structures that attract animals to carry pollen, and their shapes match the animals that visit them.
sci|13|l29-9|6-8|Seeds on the Move — Plants have structures that move seeds away from the parent so the young plants are not crowded out.
sci|13|l29-10|6-8|Caring for the Young — Some animals make many offspring and give little care; others make few and protect them, and both strategies can work.
sci|13|l29-11|6-8|Genes and Environment — How an organism grows depends on both the genes it inherits and the conditions it lives in.
sci|13|l29-12|6-8|Testing What Helps Growth — A fair test changes one factor at a time and compares against a control to find out what really affects growth.
sci|14|l30-1|6-8|Counting a Population — A population is all the members of one species in one place, and it can only grow as far as its resources allow.
sci|14|l30-2|6-8|Competition and Predation — Living things compete for the same resources, and predators and prey keep each other's numbers in check.
sci|14|l30-3|6-8|Partners and Parasites — Species can help each other, use each other, or harm each other, and each kind of relationship shapes an ecosystem.
sci|14|l30-4|6-8|Sunlight Into Sugar — Plants, algae and some bacteria use light energy to build sugar from carbon dioxide and water, and that sugar feeds almost every living thing.
sci|14|l30-5|6-8|Food Webs and the Ten Percent Rule — Energy flows one way through a food web, and only a small share passes from each level to the next.
sci|14|l30-6|6-8|Decomposers Close the Loop — Energy flows through an ecosystem and is lost as heat, but matter cycles, and decomposers are what keep it cycling.
sci|14|l30-7|6-8|Why Variety Matters — Biodiversity, the variety of living things in a place, makes an ecosystem stronger and gives people services they cannot easily replace.
sci|14|l30-8|6-8|When an Invader Arrives — A species carried to a new place can spread fast if nothing there eats it or competes with it, and it can push out native species.
sci|14|l30-9|6-8|Drought, Fire and Recovery — Disturbances like drought and fire can knock an ecosystem down, and its resilience decides how well it bounces back.
sci|14|l30-10|6-8|Define the Problem — Before designing a fix, engineers and ecologists state exactly what must be protected, what limits the solution, and how success will be measured.
sci|14|l30-11|6-8|Compare, Test and Choose — Good ecosystem solutions come from comparing several ideas against the criteria, testing them on a small scale, and weighing trade-offs.
sci|14|l31-1|6-8|Chromosomes and DNA — Every cell carries its instructions as DNA, packed into chromosomes, and each species has its own set.
sci|14|l31-2|6-8|Genes Build Proteins — A gene is a stretch of DNA that tells a cell how to build one protein, and proteins do the work that gives an organism its traits.
sci|14|l31-3|6-8|Dominant and Recessive — Each organism carries two copies of most genes, and a dominant version can hide a recessive one for a generation.
sci|14|l31-4|6-8|Two Ways to Reproduce — Asexual reproduction copies one parent exactly, while sexual reproduction mixes genes from two parents and creates variation.
sci|14|l31-5|6-8|The Punnett Square — A Punnett square lists every possible combination of alleles from two parents and shows how likely each one is.
sci|14|l31-6|6-8|Chance, Not Certainty — Genetic ratios are probabilities; they describe many offspring on average, not what any single one must be.
sci|14|l31-7|6-8|What a Mutation Is — A mutation is a change in the DNA sequence, and it can come from copying mistakes or from damage by chemicals or radiation.
sci|14|l31-8|6-8|Harmful, Helpful, Neutral — Whether a mutation is harmful, helpful or neutral depends on what it does to a protein and on the environment the organism lives in.
sci|14|l31-9|6-8|Selective Breeding — For thousands of years people have chosen which plants and animals reproduce, and that choice slowly reshapes a species.
sci|14|l31-10|6-8|Genetic Engineering — Genetic engineering moves or edits genes directly, which is faster and more precise than breeding and raises new questions.
sci|14|l31-11|6-8|Reading the Whole Book — The Human Genome Project read the entire sequence of human DNA, giving scientists a map for finding genes linked to health and disease.
sci|15|l33-1|6-8|Three Kinds of Rock — Every rock forms in one of three ways, and each way leaves clues you can see.
sci|15|l33-2|6-8|The Rock Cycle Never Ends — Rock is always being broken down, moved, remade and melted, so any rock can become any other kind.
sci|15|l33-3|6-8|Inside the Earth — Earth has layers, and the thin, cracked outer shell rides on hot rock that slowly flows.
sci|15|l33-4|6-8|Where Plates Meet — Most earthquakes, volcanoes and mountains happen where plates pull apart, push together or slide past each other.
sci|15|l33-5|6-8|Sun and Gravity Run the Water Cycle — Sunlight lifts water into the air and gravity brings it back down, over and over.
sci|15|l33-6|6-8|Where Earth's Water Is — Almost all of Earth's water is salty ocean, and most of the fresh water is frozen or underground.
sci|15|l33-7|6-8|The Ocean Moves Heat — Ocean currents carry heat around the planet and shape the weather and climate of land far away.
sci|15|l33-8|6-8|Air Masses and Fronts — Weather changes when big bodies of air with different temperatures and moisture push against each other.
sci|15|l33-9|6-8|Weather Versus Climate — Weather is what the air does today; climate is the pattern of weather over many years in a place.
sci|15|l33-10|6-8|Forecasting Storms and Floods — Forecasts work because scientists measure the atmosphere everywhere, then run the physics forward with computers.
sci|15|l33-11|6-8|Earthquakes in the Heartland — Illinois has no plate edge, but old buried faults can still shake the state, so scientists map the risk instead of predicting the day.
sci|15|l34-1|6-8|Why Seasons Happen — Earth's tilt, not its distance from the sun, makes the seasons.
sci|15|l34-2|6-8|The Moon's Changing Face — The moon's phases are the changing view we get of its sunlit half as it orbits Earth about every 29.5 days.
sci|15|l34-3|6-8|Eclipses and Why They Are Rare — Eclipses happen when the sun, Earth and moon line up exactly, which the moon's tilted orbit allows only a few times a year.
sci|15|l34-4|6-8|Gravity Holds the Orbits — The same gravity that drops an apple keeps the moon and planets curving around instead of flying off in straight lines.
sci|15|l34-5|6-8|How Big Is the Solar System? — The planets are tiny and impossibly far apart; distance, not size, is the hard thing to picture.
sci|15|l34-6|6-8|The Telescope as a Time Machine — Because light takes time to travel, looking far out into space means looking back into the past.
sci|15|l34-7|6-8|Layers Tell Time — In undisturbed rock layers the bottom is oldest and the top is youngest, so the layers are a calendar.
sci|15|l34-8|6-8|Fossils as Clocks — Fossils show which living things came before which, and some fossils pin a layer to a narrow slice of time.
sci|15|l34-9|6-8|Counting Years With Atoms — Radioactive atoms decay at a fixed rate, so measuring how many are left gives a rock's age in years.
sci|15|l34-10|6-8|The Geologic Time Scale — Earth's 4.5-billion-year history is divided into named chunks marked by big changes in life and rock.
sci|15|l35-1|6-8|Where Minerals Come From — Useful minerals collect only where certain geologic processes happened, so they are found in some places and not others.
sci|15|l35-2|6-8|Fresh Water, Unevenly Shared — Fresh water is scarce and unevenly spread, so where a place sits in the water cycle decides how much it has.
sci|15|l35-3|6-8|Energy From the Ground and the Sky — Every energy source has a cost and a benefit, and where it comes from depends on geology and geography.

sci|15|l35-5|6-8|Building for the Worst Day — People cannot stop hazards, but they can reduce harm by building, planning and warning based on the evidence.
sci|15|l35-6|6-8|Footprints on the Land — Farming, cities and mining have remade most of Illinois's land, and the changes ripple into water and wildlife.
sci|15|l35-7|6-8|Clearing the Air and Water — Air and water pollution were measured, traced to their sources and cut by laws and engineering, which shows that impacts can be reversed.
sci|15|l35-8|6-8|More People, More Demand — Human impact grows with both how many people there are and how much each person uses.
sci|15|l35-9|6-8|The Greenhouse Effect — Certain gases in the air trap heat leaving Earth, which keeps the planet warm enough to live on and warms it more as they increase.
sci|15|l35-10|6-8|The Evidence for a Warming World — Many independent measurements, from thermometers to ice to sea level, all point the same way: Earth is warming, and the added carbon dioxide is the main cause.
sci|15|l35-11|6-8|Designing a Lighter Footprint — Reducing human impact means measuring it, designing solutions that cut it and monitoring to see whether they work.
sci|16|l36-1|9-10|Water Is Not Just Wet — Water's lopsided charge lets it stick to itself and dissolve almost everything a cell needs.
sci|16|l36-2|9-10|Carbon Builds the Big Molecules — Carbon's four bonds let cells build long chains, and four families of these chains make up almost everything alive.
sci|16|l36-3|9-10|Proteins and the Code That Makes Them — Proteins do the cell's work, and the order of their amino acids is written in DNA.
sci|16|l36-4|9-10|Enzymes Make It Fast — Enzymes are proteins that speed up specific reactions by lowering the energy needed to start them.
sci|16|l36-5|9-10|Every Living Thing Is Made of Cells — Cell theory took two centuries of microscopes and argument: all living things are made of cells, and every cell comes from another cell.

sci|16|l36-7|9-10|Division of Labor Inside a Cell — Each organelle does one kind of job, and the kind of cell decides which organelles it has the most of.
sci|16|l36-8|9-10|Making the Invisible Visible — A microscope magnifies, a stain adds contrast, and resolution, not magnification, sets what you can actually see.
sci|16|l36-9|9-10|The Gatekeeper Membrane — The cell membrane is a two-layer sheet of phospholipids studded with proteins, and it lets some things through and not others.
sci|16|l36-10|9-10|Diffusion and Osmosis — Molecules spread from where they are crowded to where they are not, and when water does this across a membrane, cells swell or shrink.
sci|16|l36-11|9-10|Moving Things Uphill — Cells spend energy to pump molecules against their gradients and to swallow or release whole packages.
sci|16|l36-12|9-10|A Steady Inside in a Changing World — Homeostasis is the constant work of keeping conditions inside the body within narrow limits while the outside changes.
sci|16|l36-13|9-10|Feedback Loops — Negative feedback reverses a change to hold a set point; positive feedback amplifies a change to push a process to its finish.
sci|16|l37-1|9-10|Where a Tree's Weight Comes From — Plants build almost all their mass from carbon dioxide taken from the air, not from soil.
sci|16|l37-2|9-10|Inside the Chloroplast — Photosynthesis happens in two stages: light energy is captured first, then that energy is used to build sugar from carbon dioxide.
sci|16|l37-3|9-10|What Slows a Plant Down — Photosynthesis runs only as fast as its scarcest input allows: light, carbon dioxide, water or the right temperature.
sci|16|l37-4|9-10|ATP, the Cell's Spending Money — Cells do not use glucose directly; they convert its energy into ATP, a small molecule that powers work in tiny, controlled payments.
sci|16|l37-5|9-10|Cellular Respiration — Cellular respiration takes glucose apart in stages, using oxygen, and captures most of the released energy as ATP inside mitochondria.
sci|16|l37-6|9-10|Energy Without Oxygen — When oxygen runs out, cells fall back on fermentation, which keeps glycolysis going but yields far less ATP.
sci|16|l37-7|9-10|The Carbon Loop — Photosynthesis and respiration pass the same carbon atoms back and forth, and the whole planet breathes in a yearly rhythm.
sci|16|l37-8|9-10|The Cell Cycle — A cell grows, copies its DNA, checks its work, and only then divides, in a cycle that takes about a day for many human cells.
sci|16|l37-9|9-10|Mitosis, Step by Step — Mitosis sorts two identical sets of chromosomes into two nuclei, and cytokinesis then splits the cell in two.
sci|16|l37-10|9-10|When Division Goes Wrong — Cancer is the result of damage to the genes that control the cell cycle, so cells divide without limit and spread.
sci|16|l37-11|9-10|Stem Cells and Specialization — All your cells carry the same DNA; they differ because they read different genes, and stem cells are the ones that have not yet chosen.
sci|16|l37-12|9-10|Systems Working Together — Cells form tissues, tissues form organs, organs form systems, and no system does its job alone.
sci|17|l38-1|9-10|A Twisted Ladder — DNA is a double helix: two backbones with paired bases inside, and the pairing rule lets it store information.
sci|17|l38-2|9-10|Copying the Code — Before a cell divides, it unzips its DNA and builds a new partner strand for each half, following the pairing rule.
sci|17|l38-3|9-10|Writing the Message — A gene is copied into a strand of messenger RNA that can leave the nucleus and carry the instructions to the cell's protein-building machines.
sci|17|l38-4|9-10|Reading the Message — A ribosome reads mRNA three bases at a time, and each three-base codon tells it which amino acid to add to a growing protein.
sci|17|l38-5|9-10|When the Code Changes — A mutation changes the sequence of bases; its effect can be nothing, a small change, or a broken protein, depending on where and how it happens.
sci|17|l38-6|9-10|Shuffling the Deck — Meiosis makes sex cells with half the usual chromosomes, and crossing over and independent assortment mix the genes so every cell is different.
sci|17|l38-7|9-10|Mendel's Peas — Gregor Mendel showed that traits are carried by paired factors, one from each parent, and that some versions hide others.
sci|17|l38-8|9-10|Beyond Simple Dominance — Many traits do not follow a simple dominant-recessive pattern: alleles can blend, both can show, or a gene can ride on the X chromosome.
sci|17|l38-9|9-10|Reading a Family Tree — A pedigree charts a trait across generations, and its pattern reveals whether an allele is dominant, recessive or sex-linked.
sci|17|l38-10|9-10|Sequencing a Genome — Scientists can now read the full order of bases in an organism's DNA, and doing so for humans changed medicine and how we see ourselves.
sci|17|l38-11|9-10|Editing With CRISPR — CRISPR lets scientists cut DNA at a chosen spot and change it, which brings real cures and hard questions about how far to go.
sci|18|l40-1|9-10|How Populations Grow — A population grows when births plus arrivals outnumber deaths plus departures, and it can grow very fast when nothing holds it back.
sci|18|l40-2|9-10|Limiting Factors and Carrying Capacity — Every environment can support only so many of a species, and the resources or dangers that set that limit are called limiting factors.
sci|18|l40-3|9-10|Reading a Growth Curve — A logistic growth curve is S-shaped: fast growth at first, then slowing as the population nears carrying capacity.
sci|18|l40-4|9-10|Producers, Consumers, Decomposers — Nearly all energy in an ecosystem enters through producers that capture sunlight, passes to consumers that eat, and is released by decomposers that break the dead apart.
sci|18|l40-5|9-10|The Ten Percent Rule — Only about ten percent of the energy at one trophic level is stored in the bodies of the next level up, which is why food chains are short and top predators are rare.
sci|18|l40-6|9-10|Food Webs, Not Chains — Real ecosystems are food webs in which most species eat and are eaten by several others, so a change in one population ripples through many.
sci|18|l40-7|9-10|The Water Cycle — Water moves in an endless cycle among ocean, air, land and living things, driven by the sun and gravity, and every organism depends on that flow.
sci|18|l40-8|9-10|The Carbon Cycle — Carbon atoms cycle between air, living things, soil, ocean and rock; burning fossil fuels adds carbon to the air faster than the cycle removes it.
sci|18|l40-9|9-10|The Nitrogen Cycle — Nitrogen is plentiful in the air but useless to most life until bacteria fix it into usable forms, and human fertilizer now adds as much as nature does.
sci|18|l40-10|9-10|Competition, Predation, Symbiosis — Species interact by competing for resources, by eating one another, and by living closely together in ways that help, harm or barely affect their partners.
sci|18|l40-11|9-10|Succession After Disturbance — After a disturbance, a community rebuilds in a roughly predictable sequence called succession, from hardy pioneers toward a longer-lasting mix of species.
sci|18|l40-12|9-10|Keystones and the Prairie Fire — Some species and some disturbances hold a whole community in shape; remove a keystone species or stop the prairie fires and the community changes completely.
sci|18|l41-1|9-10|Three Kinds of Diversity — Biodiversity is the variety of life at three levels: the genes within a species, the species within a community, and the ecosystems across a region.
sci|18|l41-2|9-10|Why Diversity Matters — Diverse ecosystems are more stable, more productive and more useful to people, and each species lost is a possible tool, food or medicine gone for good.
sci|18|l41-3|9-10|Measuring Life in a Place — Ecologists estimate biodiversity by sampling: counting in small plots, trapping and releasing, listening, and increasingly by reading DNA left in soil and water.
sci|18|l41-4|9-10|Habitat Loss and Fragmentation — The largest cause of extinction today is habitat loss, and breaking habitat into small pieces harms species even when some habitat remains.
sci|18|l41-5|9-10|Invasive Species — A species carried to a new region can spread explosively when it leaves its predators and diseases behind, crowding out natives and costing billions.
sci|18|l41-6|9-10|Pollution and Overharvesting — Pollution can poison ecosystems even at low doses that build up through food webs, and taking a species faster than it reproduces can drive it to collapse.
sci|18|l41-7|9-10|A Changing Climate — As the climate warms, species shift their ranges and timing; those that cannot move or adjust fast enough decline, and the effects pile onto the other threats.
sci|18|l41-8|9-10|Safety in Numbers — Many animals live in groups because groups improve the odds of finding food, spotting danger and raising young, and those odds are measurable.
sci|18|l41-9|9-10|The Costs of the Crowd — Group living also brings competition, disease and attention from predators, so a species lives in groups only when the benefits outweigh the costs.
sci|18|l41-10|9-10|Conservation Biology — Conservation biology uses population ecology and genetics to keep species from going extinct, with tools that range from protected areas to laws to captive breeding.
sci|18|l41-11|9-10|Restoring the Prairie at Nachusa — Restoration rebuilds a lost ecosystem step by step, with seed, fire and grazing, and treats each step as an experiment whose results are measured.
sci|18|l41-12|9-10|Designing and Judging a Solution — An environmental solution is engineered like any other: define the problem, set criteria and constraints, compare options with evidence, and weigh the trade-offs honestly.
sci|19|l42-1|9-10|Three Particles, One Atom — Every atom is built from protons and neutrons in a tiny nucleus, with electrons around it, and the number of protons decides which element it is.
sci|19|l42-2|9-10|Isotopes: Same Element, Different Mass — Atoms of one element can have different numbers of neutrons; these versions are isotopes, and some of them are unstable.
sci|19|l42-3|9-10|Electrons in Shells — Electrons fill energy levels around the nucleus in order, and the electrons in the outermost level, the valence electrons, control how an atom bonds.
sci|19|l42-4|9-10|Reading the Table — The periodic table lists elements in order of atomic number, and its rows and columns show repeating patterns that come from electron shells.
sci|19|l42-5|9-10|Why Reactivity Rises and Falls — Reactivity follows the table: metals react more going down a group, nonmetals react more going up, and the noble gases sit out.
sci|19|l42-6|9-10|Size and Ionization Energy — Atoms get larger going down a group and smaller across a period, and the energy needed to remove an electron follows the opposite trend.
sci|19|l42-7|9-10|Ionic Bonds: Give and Take — When a metal gives electrons to a nonmetal, the resulting charged ions attract each other and lock into a crystal; that attraction is an ionic bond.
sci|19|l42-8|9-10|Covalent Bonds: Sharing — Two nonmetals reach full shells by sharing electron pairs; the shared pair is a covalent bond, and it builds molecules.
sci|19|l42-9|9-10|Metallic Bonds: A Sea of Electrons — In a metal, the atoms' outer electrons roam freely through the whole solid, which explains why metals conduct, bend and shine.
sci|19|l42-10|9-10|Naming What You Make — Chemical names and formulas follow simple rules that tell you which atoms are present and in what ratio.
sci|19|l42-11|9-10|Why Salt Dissolves and Diamond Is Hard — A material's properties come from how its particles are bonded and arranged: the same element, bonded differently, can be soft or the hardest substance known.
sci|19|l43-1|9-10|Atoms Are Rearranged, Not Destroyed — In a chemical reaction, atoms break old bonds and form new ones, but no atom is created or destroyed, so mass is conserved.
sci|19|l43-2|9-10|Balancing an Equation — Balancing an equation means adjusting the numbers in front of formulas until every element has the same count on both sides.
sci|19|l43-3|9-10|Five Kinds of Reactions — Most reactions fit a few patterns: synthesis, decomposition, single replacement, double replacement and combustion.
sci|19|l43-4|9-10|Collisions and Reaction Rate — Reactions happen when particles collide hard enough and in the right way, so anything that makes effective collisions more frequent speeds up a reaction.
sci|19|l43-5|9-10|Energy Stored in Bonds — Breaking bonds takes energy and forming bonds releases it; a reaction is exothermic if it releases more than it absorbs, endothermic if it absorbs more.
sci|19|l43-6|9-10|Equilibrium: Reactions That Run Both Ways — Many reactions can run forward and backward; at equilibrium both directions happen at the same rate, and changing conditions shifts the balance.
sci|19|l43-7|9-10|Radioactive Decay and Half-Life — An unstable nucleus changes by emitting radiation, and each radioactive isotope has a fixed half-life, the time for half its atoms to decay.
sci|19|l43-8|9-10|Carbon Dating — Living things constantly take in carbon-14; after death it decays with a 5,730-year half-life, so the amount left tells how long ago the organism died.
sci|19|l43-9|9-10|Fission and Fusion — Splitting a heavy nucleus or joining light ones releases enormous energy because a small amount of mass turns into energy.
sci|19|l43-10|9-10|How a Reactor Works — A nuclear power plant uses a controlled fission chain reaction to make heat, which boils water to spin a turbine, just as a coal plant does with fire.
sci|19|l43-11|9-10|Benefits, Risks and Trade-offs — Nuclear power provides steady, low-carbon electricity, but it produces long-lived waste and carries rare but serious accident risks; weighing these is a decision about evidence and values.
sci|2|l3-1|k-2|The Drum Skin Shakes — A sound comes from something that shakes back and forth very fast.
sci|2|l3-2|k-2|Rubber Bands and Throats — Many things vibrate to make sound, even your own throat.
sci|2|l3-3|k-2|Sound Moves Things — Sound can make other things vibrate too, like rice on a drum.
sci|2|l3-4|k-2|Loud and Soft — A big wiggle makes a loud sound; a small wiggle makes a soft sound.
sci|2|l3-5|k-2|High and Low — Fast wiggles make high sounds; slow wiggles make low sounds.
sci|2|l3-6|k-2|Through Air, Water and Wood — Sound can travel through air, through water and through solid things.
sci|2|l3-7|k-2|The String Telephone — A tight string can carry a voice from one cup to another.
sci|2|l3-8|k-2|Sending a Message — People use sounds like bells and whistles to send messages far away.
sci|2|l4-1|k-2|The Dark Room — You need light to see anything; in a truly dark room you see nothing.
sci|2|l4-2|k-2|Where Light Comes From — The sun, fire, and light bulbs make light; most things only bounce it.
sci|2|l4-3|k-2|Light Goes Through Glass — Some materials let light through, some let a little through, and some block it.
sci|2|l4-4|k-2|Shadows — A shadow is the dark spot where something has blocked the light.
sci|2|l4-5|k-2|Mirrors Bounce Light — A mirror bounces light back so you can see yourself or send a beam.
sci|2|l4-6|k-2|Blink a Message — Light can carry a message, like a flashlight blink or a lighthouse beam.
sci|2|l4-7|k-2|The Telescope Collects Light — A telescope gathers lots of light so we can see faint, far things like the moon.
sci|20|l44-1|9-10|Position, Speed and Velocity — Speed tells how fast something moves; velocity adds the direction, and both are measured against a chosen starting point.
sci|20|l44-2|9-10|Reading Motion Graphs — On a position-time graph the slope is velocity; on a velocity-time graph the slope is acceleration and the area is distance.
sci|20|l44-3|9-10|Acceleration and Free Fall — Acceleration is any change in velocity; near Earth's surface, falling objects gain about 9.8 meters per second of speed each second.
sci|20|l44-4|9-10|Inertia and Balanced Forces — An object keeps its velocity unless an unbalanced force acts on it; this is Newton's first law, and it explains why the dummy kept moving.
sci|20|l44-5|9-10|Force Equals Mass Times Acceleration — Newton's second law, F = ma, says the net force on an object equals its mass times its acceleration, measured in newtons.
sci|20|l44-6|9-10|Forces Come in Pairs — Newton's third law says that whenever one object pushes on another, the second pushes back on the first with equal force in the opposite direction.
sci|20|l44-7|9-10|Momentum and Impulse — Momentum is mass times velocity; a force acting over time, called impulse, changes it.
sci|20|l44-8|9-10|Collisions and Conservation — In any collision with no outside force, the total momentum of the objects before equals the total momentum after.
sci|20|l44-9|9-10|Engineering a Safer Crash — Belts, airbags and crumple zones cannot change a crash's momentum, so they lengthen the stopping time to lower the force on people.
sci|20|l44-10|9-10|Gravity's Law — Every mass pulls on every other with a force that grows with the masses and shrinks with the square of the distance between them.
sci|20|l44-11|9-10|The Electric Force — Charged objects push or pull on each other with a force that follows an inverse-square law, like gravity, but can repel as well as attract.
sci|20|l44-12|9-10|Fields, Orbits and Satellites — A field describes the force an object would feel at each point in space; an orbit is a fall that keeps missing the ground.
sci|20|l45-1|9-10|Energy of Motion — Kinetic energy is one half the mass times the speed squared, so doubling speed gives four times the energy.
sci|20|l45-2|9-10|Stored Energy — Potential energy is energy stored by position or arrangement, such as height in a gravitational field or a stretched spring.
sci|20|l45-3|9-10|Heat and Temperature — Thermal energy is the total kinetic energy of an object's moving particles, while temperature measures how fast they move on average.
sci|20|l45-4|9-10|Energy Is Conserved — Energy changes form and moves between objects, but the total energy of a closed system never changes.
sci|20|l45-5|9-10|Why Heat Flows One Way — Heat flows from hot to cold on its own and never the reverse, and every energy conversion spreads some energy out as waste heat.
sci|20|l45-6|9-10|Where Our Energy Comes From — Every energy resource is a way of capturing concentrated energy, and each carries its own costs, limits and trade-offs.
sci|20|l45-7|9-10|What a Wave Is — A wave carries energy without carrying matter, and its speed equals its frequency times its wavelength.
sci|20|l45-8|9-10|Light Across the Spectrum — Visible light is one small slice of the electromagnetic spectrum, waves of electric and magnetic fields that all travel at the speed of light in space.
sci|20|l45-9|9-10|Light in Packets — Light also behaves as a stream of particles called photons, each carrying an energy set by its frequency.
sci|20|l45-10|9-10|Electricity from Motion — A changing magnetic field pushes current through a wire, which is how every generator turns motion into electricity.
sci|20|l45-11|9-10|Waves That Carry Information — Any wave can carry a message if you vary it in a pattern, and digital signals of ones and zeros travel farther and cleaner than analog ones.
sci|21|l46-1|9-10|Barcodes in Starlight — Spread starlight into a spectrum and its dark lines tell you which elements the star holds.
sci|21|l46-2|9-10|Bigger Eyes on the Sky — Each larger telescope gathered more light and revealed objects no one had seen, and each one changed what people believed about the sky.
sci|21|l46-3|9-10|A Ruler Made of Blinking Stars — Cepheid variables blink on a rhythm tied to their true brightness, so comparing true to apparent brightness gives their distance.
sci|21|l46-4|9-10|The Sun's Furnace — The sun shines because hydrogen nuclei fuse into helium in its core, turning a little mass into a great deal of energy.
sci|21|l46-5|9-10|How a Star Lives and Dies — A star's mass decides how fast it burns its fuel and whether it ends as a white dwarf or in a supernova.
sci|21|l46-6|9-10|Where Your Atoms Came From — Hydrogen and helium came from the early universe; nearly every heavier atom in your body was made inside stars.
sci|21|l46-7|9-10|Stretched Light and Fleeing Galaxies — Galaxies' spectra are shifted toward red, and the farther the galaxy, the bigger the shift: the universe is expanding.
sci|21|l46-8|9-10|The Glow Left Over — A faint microwave glow from every direction is the cooled light of the hot early universe, predicted before it was found.
sci|21|l46-9|9-10|How Old Is Everything — Independent evidence gives the universe an age of about 13.8 billion years and the sun and Earth about 4.6 billion.
sci|21|l46-10|9-10|A Cloud Becomes a Solar System — The sun and planets formed together from a collapsing, spinning cloud of gas and dust about 4.6 billion years ago.
sci|21|l46-11|9-10|Kepler's Three Rules — Planets move on ellipses, speed up when nearer the sun, and take longer to orbit the farther out they are.
sci|21|l46-12|9-10|Clocks in the Rocks — Radioactive atoms decay at steady, known rates, so the ratio of parent to daughter atoms in a rock tells its age.
sci|21|l47-1|9-10|Evidence From Two Shores — Matching coastlines, rocks, fossils and glacial scratches on separate continents are best explained by the continents once being joined.
sci|21|l47-2|9-10|The Ocean Floor Speaks — New crust forms at mid-ocean ridges and spreads outward, recorded in magnetic stripes and in seafloor that gets older away from the ridge.
sci|21|l47-3|9-10|Where Plates Meet — Earthquakes, volcanoes and mountains cluster along plate boundaries where plates spread apart, collide or slide past each other.
sci|21|l47-4|9-10|Layers Seen by Earthquake Waves — Seismic waves bending and stopping inside Earth reveal a solid crust and mantle, a liquid outer core and a solid inner core.
sci|21|l47-5|9-10|Heat Moving Rock — Heat from Earth's formation and from radioactive decay drives slow convection in the mantle, and that motion moves the plates.
sci|21|l47-6|9-10|The Rock Cycle at Scale — Plate motion, heat, water and time move rock through igneous, sedimentary and metamorphic forms over and over.
sci|21|l47-7|9-10|Water on the Move — Solar energy lifts water from the oceans and gravity brings it back, moving heat and shaping land as it goes.
sci|21|l47-8|9-10|Carbon and the Blanket of Air — Carbon cycles among air, water, rock and life; the carbon dioxide in the air traps heat and keeps Earth warm enough for liquid water.
sci|21|l47-9|9-10|Feedbacks Between Systems — Earth's systems push back on each other; some feedbacks amplify a change and some dampen it.
sci|21|l47-10|9-10|Bubbles in Ancient Ice — Ice cores hold layers of old snow and trapped air that record temperature and carbon dioxide for hundreds of thousands of years.
sci|21|l47-11|9-10|Models and Projections — Climate models apply the laws of physics to the whole planet, are tested against the past, and give ranges rather than exact forecasts.
sci|21|l47-12|9-10|How the Great Lakes Were Made — Ice sheets a kilometer thick scraped out the Great Lakes basins and flattened Illinois, then melted back about 14,000 years ago.
sci|21|l48-1|9-10|Renewable or Not — A resource is sustainable only if people use it no faster than nature replaces it; some resources are replaced in years, some never.
sci|21|l48-2|9-10|Water Under the Prairie — Groundwater is a shared, slowly refilled store; pumping faster than recharge draws it down for everyone.
sci|21|l48-3|9-10|Powering Illinois — Every energy source has costs and benefits; Illinois's mix of nuclear, gas, wind, solar and coal reflects a series of trade-offs.
sci|21|l48-4|9-10|Hazard, Exposure and Risk — Risk depends on the hazard, on who and what is in its path, and on how well they can withstand it; people can change the last two.
sci|21|l48-5|9-10|Too Much Water, Too Little — Floods and droughts are natural, but levees, paving, drainage and land use change how much damage they do.
sci|21|l48-6|9-10|Heat and Violent Storms — Warnings, shelter and social connection decide whether heat waves and tornadoes become tragedies.
sci|21|l48-7|9-10|From Prairie to Cornfield — Converting nearly all of Illinois's prairie to farmland fed millions and also removed the deep roots that built and held the soil.
