sci|21|l48-9|9-10|The Air We Share — Pollution problems have been solved before when the science was clear and the substitutes existed; carbon dioxide is harder because it comes from nearly everything.
sci|21|l48-10|9-10|Cost, Benefit and Constraint — Engineers judge a solution by its benefits, its costs and the constraints it must satisfy, and no real solution wins on every count.
sci|21|l48-11|9-10|Mitigation and Adaptation — Mitigation reduces the cause of a change; adaptation reduces its harm; a sensible plan does both.
sci|21|l48-12|9-10|Illinois in Fifty Years — Illinois's food, water and energy futures are linked, and choices made in fields, cities and statehouses now will shape all three.
sci|22|l49-1|11-12|A Tiny, Dense Nucleus — Rutherford's scattering experiment showed that an atom's positive charge and nearly all its mass sit in a tiny central nucleus.
sci|22|l49-2|11-12|Light in Lines — Each element emits and absorbs light only at certain wavelengths, which means its electrons can hold only certain energies.
sci|22|l49-3|11-12|Orbitals, Not Orbits — Electrons do not circle the nucleus on paths; they occupy orbitals, regions where they are likely to be found, described by the quantum model.
sci|22|l49-4|11-12|Mendeleev's Empty Squares — Mendeleev arranged the elements by mass and repeating properties, left gaps for unknown elements, and predicted their properties correctly.
sci|22|l49-5|11-12|Size and the Pull of the Nucleus — Atomic radius grows down a group and shrinks across a period, and ionization energy does the opposite, because of shells and nuclear pull.
sci|22|l49-6|11-12|Who Pulls Hardest on Electrons — Electronegativity, an atom's pull on shared electrons, rises across a period and falls down a group, and it predicts what kind of bond will form.
sci|22|l49-7|11-12|Ionic Bonds and Crystals — When a metal gives electrons to a nonmetal, the ions formed attract in every direction and build a rigid crystal lattice.
sci|22|l49-8|11-12|Sharing Pairs: Covalent Bonds — Nonmetals share electron pairs to complete their outer shells, and Lewis structures map which pairs are shared and which are not.
sci|22|l49-9|11-12|A Sea of Electrons — In metals, valence electrons move freely among a lattice of positive ions, which explains why metals conduct, shine and bend.
sci|22|l49-10|11-12|Molecules Have Shapes — Electron pairs around a central atom spread as far apart as they can, so counting pairs predicts a molecule's shape and bond angles.
sci|22|l49-11|11-12|Polar or Not — A bond is polar when electronegativities differ, but a molecule is polar only if its shape leaves those pulls unbalanced.
sci|22|l49-12|11-12|Forces Between Molecules — Weak attractions between molecules, especially hydrogen bonds, decide boiling points, and water's are unusually strong.
sci|22|l50-1|11-12|Sorting Reactions — Chemical reactions rearrange atoms without creating or destroying them, and most fall into a few recognizable patterns.
sci|22|l50-2|11-12|Electrons on the Move: Redox — In a redox reaction one substance loses electrons and another gains them, and this transfer powers rusting, burning and batteries.
sci|22|l50-3|11-12|Energy In, Energy Out — Breaking bonds costs energy and forming bonds releases it, so the difference decides whether a reaction gives off heat or absorbs it.
sci|22|l50-4|11-12|Collisions That Count — Reactions happen when particles collide with enough energy and the right orientation, so anything that increases effective collisions speeds the reaction up.
sci|22|l50-5|11-12|The Hill Every Reaction Must Climb — Reactions need a minimum energy, the activation energy, and a catalyst speeds a reaction by offering a lower path over that hill.
sci|22|l50-6|11-12|Step by Step: Mechanisms — Most reactions happen in a series of elementary steps, and the slowest step sets the overall rate.
sci|22|l50-7|11-12|Reactions That Run Both Ways — In a closed system, a reversible reaction reaches a dynamic equilibrium where forward and reverse rates are equal and concentrations stop changing.
sci|22|l50-8|11-12|Pushing an Equilibrium — When an equilibrium is disturbed, it shifts in the direction that partly undoes the change, a rule known as Le Châtelier's principle.
sci|22|l50-9|11-12|Haber's Bargain — The Haber process balances yield against speed with moderate temperature, high pressure, an iron catalyst and removal of ammonia as it forms.
sci|22|l50-10|11-12|Redox in a Wire: Batteries — A battery separates the two halves of a redox reaction and forces the electrons to travel through a wire, where they can do work.
sci|22|l50-11|11-12|Rust and How to Stop It — Corrosion is an unwanted electrochemical cell on a metal's surface, and it can be slowed by coatings, alloys or a more reactive sacrificial metal.
sci|22|l50-12|11-12|Forcing a Reaction Backward — Electrolysis uses an outside current to drive a redox reaction that would not happen on its own, which is how we make aluminum and split water.
sci|23|l51-1|11-12|The Mole — A mole is a fixed, enormous number of particles, chosen so that the mass of one mole in grams equals the atomic mass on the periodic table.
sci|23|l51-2|11-12|Molar Mass of a Compound — The molar mass of a compound is the sum of the molar masses of the atoms in its formula, and it tells you what fraction of the mass each element supplies.
sci|23|l51-3|11-12|Grams, Moles and Particles — Molar mass converts between grams and moles, and Avogadro's number converts between moles and particles, so any one of the three lets you find the other two.
sci|23|l51-4|11-12|Balancing Equations — Atoms are never created or destroyed in a chemical reaction, so a balanced equation must show the same number of each kind of atom on both sides.
sci|23|l51-5|11-12|Mole Ratios — The coefficients of a balanced equation give mole ratios, and those ratios, combined with molar masses, let you predict the mass of any product from the mass of any reactant.
sci|23|l51-6|11-12|Limiting Reactant and Yield — The reactant that runs out first limits how much product can form, and the percent yield compares what a reaction actually made with that maximum.
sci|23|l51-7|11-12|Pressure, Volume and Temperature — For a fixed amount of gas, pressure and volume are inversely related, while volume and pressure each rise in proportion to absolute temperature.
sci|23|l51-8|11-12|The Ideal Gas Law — PV equals nRT ties pressure, volume, moles and temperature into one equation, and it lets stoichiometry predict the volume of a gas.
sci|23|l51-9|11-12|Molecules in Motion — Gas laws follow from a model of tiny, fast, constantly colliding molecules, and real gases stray from that model only when pushed close together or cooled toward liquefying.
sci|23|l51-10|11-12|Sizing the Airbag — An airbag's charge is designed by working backward from the bag's volume through the ideal gas law and the mole ratio to a mass of solid.
sci|23|l51-11|11-12|Fast Reactions and Safety — How fast a reaction runs depends on temperature, concentration, surface area and catalysts, and the same chemistry that saves lives in a crash demands care in the lab.
sci|23|l52-1|11-12|Dissolving and Solubility — A substance dissolves when the attractions between solvent and solute particles are strong enough to pull the solute apart, and temperature and pressure change how much can dissolve.
sci|23|l52-2|11-12|Molarity and Dilution — Molarity, moles of solute per liter of solution, lets chemists measure out exact numbers of dissolved particles by volume.
sci|23|l52-3|11-12|Salt on the Roads — Dissolved particles lower a solvent's freezing point and raise its boiling point by an amount that depends only on how many particles are dissolved, not what they are.
sci|23|l52-4|11-12|What Makes an Acid — An acid gives up a hydrogen ion and a base accepts one, and the strength of an acid is how completely it does so in water.
sci|23|l52-5|11-12|The pH Scale — pH measures hydrogen ion concentration on a logarithmic scale, so each step of one pH unit means a tenfold change in acidity.
sci|23|l52-6|11-12|Buffers — A buffer is a mixture of a weak acid and its partner base that soaks up added acid or base, holding pH nearly steady.
sci|23|l52-7|11-12|Titration — In a titration, a solution of known concentration is added to one of unknown concentration until they exactly neutralize, and the volume used reveals the unknown.
sci|23|l52-8|11-12|Measuring Heat — The heat a reaction releases or absorbs, its enthalpy change, can be measured by tracking the temperature change of a known mass of water.
sci|23|l52-9|11-12|Hess's Law — Because enthalpy depends only on the starting and ending substances, the heat of a reaction can be found by adding the heats of steps that lead to the same result.
sci|23|l52-10|11-12|Entropy and Free Energy — Whether a reaction goes forward on its own depends on both the heat it releases and the disorder it creates, combined in the free energy change.
sci|23|l52-11|11-12|The Ocean's Changing pH — Carbon dioxide dissolving in seawater forms carbonic acid, and the ocean's surface pH has dropped measurably as atmospheric carbon dioxide has risen.
sci|23|l52-12|11-12|Cleaning the Rain — Acid rain was reduced by removing sulfur dioxide from smoke through neutralization chemistry and by rules that capped total emissions.
sci|24|l53-1|11-12|Scalars and Vectors — Some quantities need only a size, but displacement, velocity, acceleration and force also need a direction, and that direction changes the math.
sci|24|l53-2|11-12|Reading Motion Graphs — On a position-time graph the slope is velocity; on a velocity-time graph the slope is acceleration and the area under the line is displacement.
sci|24|l53-3|11-12|Equations of Constant Acceleration — When acceleration is constant, four equations connect displacement, initial velocity, final velocity, acceleration and time, and any three known values give the rest.
sci|24|l53-4|11-12|Projectiles — A thrown object moves at constant horizontal velocity while accelerating downward at g, and the two motions happen at the same time without affecting each other.
sci|24|l53-5|11-12|Circular Motion — An object moving in a circle at constant speed is still accelerating toward the center, and some real force must supply that centripetal acceleration.
sci|24|l53-6|11-12|Inertia and the First Law — An object keeps its velocity, whether zero or not, unless a net force acts on it; forces change motion rather than cause it.
sci|24|l53-7|11-12|Force, Mass and the Second Law — Net force equals mass times acceleration: the same push accelerates a small mass more than a large one, and acceleration points the same way as the net force.
sci|24|l53-8|11-12|Action, Reaction and Free-Body Diagrams — Forces come in pairs that act on different objects, and a free-body diagram that shows only the forces on one object is the tool for keeping that straight.
sci|24|l53-9|11-12|Friction — Friction is a force between touching surfaces that opposes sliding, roughly proportional to the normal force, and static friction can be larger than kinetic friction.
sci|24|l53-10|11-12|Forces on an Incline — On a slope, gravity splits into a component along the surface that drives sliding and a component into the surface that sets the normal force and friction.
sci|24|l53-11|11-12|The Physics of a Crash — In a collision the body keeps moving until a force stops it, and stretching the stopping distance and time is what a seat belt, air bag and crumple zone do to lower that force.
sci|24|l54-1|11-12|Momentum and Impulse — Momentum is mass times velocity, and a force acting over a time interval changes momentum by an amount called impulse.
sci|24|l54-2|11-12|Conservation of Momentum — When no outside force acts on a system, its total momentum stays the same, no matter how its parts push on one another.
sci|24|l54-3|11-12|Collisions in Two Dimensions — Momentum is conserved separately along each axis, and whether kinetic energy is also conserved decides if a collision is elastic or inelastic.
sci|24|l54-4|11-12|Center of Mass — Every object or system has a center of mass that moves as if all the mass were there and all external forces acted on it, even while the parts tumble.
sci|24|l54-5|11-12|Torque and Balance — Torque is the turning effect of a force, equal to force times lever arm, and an object in rotational equilibrium has zero net torque as well as zero net force.
sci|24|l54-6|11-12|Angular Momentum — Spinning objects carry angular momentum, which stays constant when no outside torque acts, so pulling mass inward makes a spin faster.
sci|24|l54-7|11-12|Universal Gravitation — Every mass attracts every other with a force proportional to the product of the masses and inversely proportional to the square of the distance between them.
sci|24|l54-8|11-12|Kepler's Laws — Planets move on ellipses with the Sun at one focus, sweep out equal areas in equal times, and have periods whose squares scale with the cubes of their orbital sizes.
sci|24|l54-9|11-12|Orbits and Satellites — A satellite stays in orbit because its sideways speed is just enough that, as it falls toward Earth, the surface curves away beneath it.
sci|24|l54-10|11-12|Weightless on the Space Station — Astronauts on the International Space Station float not because gravity is absent but because they and the station are falling around Earth together.
sci|25|l55-1|11-12|What Counts as Work — In physics, work is done only when a force moves something along the direction of the force.
sci|25|l55-2|11-12|Kinetic and Potential Energy — Energy of motion depends on mass and the square of speed; stored gravitational energy depends on mass, gravity and height.
sci|25|l55-3|11-12|Power Is Energy per Second — Power measures how fast energy is transferred, in joules per second, called watts.
sci|25|l55-4|11-12|Energy Is Never Lost, Only Moved — In a closed system the total energy stays constant; energy bar charts let you track where it goes.
sci|25|l55-5|11-12|Springs and Stored Energy — A spring's force grows in proportion to how far it is stretched, and the energy it stores grows with the square of that stretch.
sci|25|l55-6|11-12|Back and Forth: Oscillation — A mass on a spring or a pendulum swings back and forth because a restoring force keeps trading potential energy for kinetic energy.
sci|25|l55-7|11-12|Temperature Is Not Heat — Temperature measures the average energy of jiggling molecules; heat is energy flowing from a hotter object to a colder one.
sci|25|l55-8|11-12|Specific Heat and Lake Michigan — Different materials need different amounts of energy to warm by one degree, and water needs far more than most.
sci|25|l55-9|11-12|The Laws of Thermodynamics — The first law says energy is conserved when heat and work are both counted; the second says heat flows on its own only from hot to cold.
sci|25|l55-10|11-12|Why No Engine Is Perfect — A heat engine must dump some heat to a cold place, so its efficiency is limited by the temperatures it runs between.
sci|25|l55-11|11-12|Entropy and the Arrow of Time — Entropy measures how many ways energy can be spread out, and it rises because spread-out arrangements vastly outnumber concentrated ones.
sci|25|l55-12|11-12|From Power Plant to Wall Socket — Most electricity comes from spinning generators driven by steam, water or wind, and the grid must balance supply and demand every second.
sci|25|l56-1|11-12|Superposition and Interference — When two waves overlap, their displacements simply add, producing bigger waves where they agree and cancellation where they disagree.
sci|25|l56-2|11-12|Standing Waves and Resonance — A wave reflecting back on itself can lock into a standing pattern, and every object has natural frequencies at which small pushes build large motion.
sci|25|l56-3|11-12|The Doppler Shift — A wave source moving toward you squeezes its waves to a higher pitch or bluer color; moving away, it stretches them lower and redder.
sci|25|l56-4|11-12|Reflection and Mirrors — Light reflects with the angle out equal to the angle in, and curved mirrors use that rule to focus light or spread it.
sci|25|l56-5|11-12|Refraction and Snell's Law — Light bends when it changes speed at a boundary, and the amount of bending follows a precise rule tied to each material's index of refraction.
sci|25|l56-6|11-12|Lenses, the Telescope and the Microscope — A lens refracts light to a focus, and combining two lenses lets you magnify faraway or tiny objects.
sci|25|l56-7|11-12|Diffraction: Light Bends Around Corners — Waves spread out when they pass through an opening or around an edge, and the spreading grows as the opening shrinks toward the wavelength.
sci|25|l56-8|11-12|The Photoelectric Puzzle — Light knocks electrons out of metal in a way only particles of light, photons, can explain, so light behaves as both wave and particle.
sci|25|l56-9|11-12|Charge and Electric Fields — Electric charge comes in two kinds that attract or repel with a force that weakens with the square of distance, and a field describes that force at every point.
sci|25|l56-10|11-12|Voltage, Current and Ohm's Law — Voltage is energy per unit charge, current is charge flowing per second, and in many materials current is proportional to voltage.
sci|25|l56-11|11-12|Series and Parallel Circuits — In a series circuit the same current passes through every part; in a parallel circuit every branch gets the full voltage.
sci|25|l56-12|11-12|Magnets, Induction and the Turntable — Moving charges make magnetic fields, and a changing magnetic field pushes charges, which is how generators, motors and a record player's cartridge work.
sci|26|l57-1|11-12|Inputs, Outputs and Stocks — A system is a set of connected parts, and you can predict it by tracking what flows in, what flows out and what builds up.
sci|26|l57-2|11-12|Feedback Loops — Feedback loops either push a change further or pull it back, and telling the two apart is the key to predicting how a system will behave.
sci|26|l57-3|11-12|Biomes and Biodiversity — Climate sorts the world into biomes, and within each one the variety of life is both a product of the system and the thing that keeps it stable.
sci|26|l57-4|11-12|Energy Flows, Matter Cycles — Energy passes through an ecosystem once and is lost as heat, while matter is recycled again and again.
sci|26|l57-5|11-12|Carbon and Nitrogen Cycles — Carbon and nitrogen move through air, water, soil and living things in loops, and human activity has added new, fast flows to both.
sci|26|l57-6|11-12|Soil, the Slow Resource — Soil is a living layer that takes centuries to build and can be lost in a decade, and its condition sets the limit on what land can produce.
sci|26|l57-7|11-12|Water and the Ogallala — Fresh water is a small fraction of Earth's water, and groundwater drawn faster than it refills is being mined, not harvested.
sci|26|l57-8|11-12|Minerals and Mining — Every phone, wire and building depends on minerals dug from the ground, and the cost of getting them is paid in energy, land and water.
sci|26|l57-9|11-12|Fossil Fuels and Renewables — Every energy source has real advantages and real costs, and choosing between them means weighing reliability, price, land, pollution and carbon together.
sci|26|l57-10|11-12|Agriculture as a System — Modern farming produces far more food per acre than ever before by adding energy, water and chemicals to the system, and each input carries an output somewhere else.
sci|26|l57-11|11-12|Tracing the Nitrogen Downstream — Nutrients that leave Midwestern fields travel through a chain of waterways to the Gulf, where they trigger the algae bloom and oxygen loss that create the dead zone.
sci|26|l57-12|11-12|Closing the Loop — The tools that reduce nutrient loss, from cover crops to rebuilt wetlands, work by putting roots, cover and slow water back into a system that lost them.
sci|26|l58-1|11-12|When Air Became a Problem — Deadly smog episodes and steady measurement forced the United States to set enforceable limits on air pollution, and the air has grown much cleaner since.
sci|26|l58-2|11-12|Rivers That Caught Fire — Water pollution comes from pipes you can point to and from land you cannot, and the law has been far more successful with the first than the second.
sci|26|l58-3|11-12|The Ozone Fix — The recovery of the ozone layer shows what it takes to solve a global environmental problem: a clear mechanism, a measurable trend, available substitutes and an agreement with teeth.
sci|26|l58-4|11-12|The Greenhouse Effect — Certain gases in the air let sunlight in but slow heat from escaping, and adding more of them warms the planet; this physics has been measured since the 1800s.
sci|26|l58-5|11-12|Lines of Evidence — Warming is documented by many independent records, from thermometers to ice cores to sea level, that all point the same direction.
sci|26|l58-6|11-12|What Models Can and Cannot Do — Climate models are physics run forward on computers; they cannot predict a given year's weather but have correctly projected the long-term warming trend.
sci|26|l58-7|11-12|Impacts in the Midwest — For Illinois, climate change means more heavy downpours, hotter summers, shifting growing seasons and stress on rivers and lakes, with both costs and a few gains.
sci|26|l58-8|11-12|Mitigation and Carbon Accounting — Reducing emissions starts with counting them honestly, source by source, and the biggest sources are electricity, transportation, industry and agriculture.
sci|26|l58-9|11-12|Adaptation — Because some warming is already locked in, communities must also adapt, redesigning buildings, farms and cities for the climate they will actually have.
sci|26|l58-10|11-12|Environmental Justice — Pollution and climate risk are not spread evenly; they fall hardest on low-income communities and communities of color, and environmental justice asks why and what to do about it.
sci|26|l58-11|11-12|Policy Tools — Governments can reduce pollution by setting rules, putting a price on it, or creating tradable permits, and each tool has strengths the others lack.
sci|26|l58-12|11-12|Costs, Benefits and Trade-offs — Every environmental decision trades one thing for another, and honest analysis puts numbers on both sides while admitting what cannot be priced.
sci|26|l58-13|11-12|Designing a Sustainable Community — A sustainable community is designed as a system, with energy, water, food, waste and transportation planned so the place can keep working for generations.
sci|27|l59-1|11-12|What a Claim Needs — A scientific claim is an answer to a question that is backed by evidence and linked to that evidence by clear reasoning.
sci|27|l59-2|11-12|Correlation Is Not Causation — Two things that rise and fall together may be linked by a hidden third factor, by chance, or by cause running the other way.
sci|27|l59-3|11-12|Samples, Bias and Controls — A conclusion is only as good as the sample it rests on, and a fair sample needs a comparison group chosen the same way.
sci|27|l59-4|11-12|Reading a Graph Honestly — A graph can tell the truth or bend it depending on its axes, its scale and the window of data it chooses to show.
sci|27|l59-5|11-12|Error and Uncertainty — Every measurement carries uncertainty, and honest science reports how large that uncertainty is instead of hiding it.
sci|27|l59-6|11-12|Significant Figures and False Precision — The digits you report should match what your instrument can actually tell you; extra digits claim a certainty you do not have.
sci|27|l59-7|11-12|Peer Review and Replication — Science trusts a result not because an expert approved it but because others can repeat it and get the same answer.
sci|27|l59-8|11-12|Judging a Science News Story — A science headline is a claim about a claim; check who did the study, on whom, how big the effect really is and who paid for it.
sci|27|l59-9|11-12|Smoking and Lung Cancer — Without any experiment on humans, many kinds of evidence together proved that smoking causes lung cancer.
sci|27|l59-10|11-12|The Ozone Layer — A prediction made from laboratory chemistry was confirmed by measurements a decade later, and the world acted on the evidence.
sci|27|l59-11|11-12|Vaccines and Autism — A tiny, flawed study created a fear that enormous, careful studies have repeatedly failed to support.
sci|27|l60-1|11-12|Asking a Testable Question — A good investigation begins with a question that names what you will change, what you will measure and how you would know the answer.
sci|27|l60-2|11-12|Variables and Controls — A fair test changes one factor on purpose, measures one outcome and holds everything else the same across a comparison group.
sci|27|l60-3|11-12|Planning the Investigation — A written plan with a procedure, sample size, repeated trials, safety steps and a pilot run turns an idea into an investigation others could repeat.
sci|27|l60-4|11-12|Collecting Data You Can Trust — Good data come from a checked instrument, a table planned in advance, units on every number and a record of everything odd that happened.
sci|27|l60-5|11-12|Finding the Pattern — Analysis means summarizing the data, plotting them and asking whether the pattern is bigger than the scatter.
sci|27|l60-6|11-12|Models and Their Limits — A model is a deliberate simplification that lets you predict and explain, and it is only as trustworthy as its record against real data.
sci|27|l60-7|11-12|Criteria and Constraints — An engineering problem is defined by measurable criteria for success and firm constraints that any solution must obey.
sci|27|l60-8|11-12|Build, Test, Fail, Improve — Designs improve through cycles of building a prototype, testing it against the criteria, studying the failures and trying again.
sci|27|l60-9|11-12|Writing a Scientific Explanation — A written explanation states the claim, presents the evidence with numbers and uncertainty, reasons from principle, and admits what it cannot rule out.
sci|27|l60-10|11-12|Presenting and Defending a Claim — Defending a claim means answering objections with evidence, separating questions of fact from questions of value, and changing your mind when the evidence demands it.
sci|27|l60-11|11-12|Your Capstone Plan — A capstone plan lays out the question, the design, the data, the analysis, the safety steps and the timeline before any measurement is made.
sci|28|l61-1|6-8|Everything That Sounds Is Moving — Every sound starts with something vibrating, moving quickly back and forth.
sci|28|l61-2|6-8|A Chain of Pushes — Sound travels as a wave: each bit of air pushes the next bit, but the air itself stays close to home.
sci|28|l61-3|6-8|No Air, No Sound — Sound needs a medium, such as air, water or a solid, to travel through; in empty space there is no sound.
sci|28|l61-4|6-8|Frequency Sets the Pitch — The faster something vibrates, the higher the pitch we hear.
sci|28|l61-5|6-8|Amplitude Sets the Loudness — A bigger vibration makes a wave with a bigger amplitude, and we hear it as louder.
sci|28|l61-6|6-8|Wavelength and the Speed of Sound — Sound moves at a set speed in each material, and its wavelength is that speed divided by the frequency.
sci|28|l61-7|6-8|From Eardrum to Brain — The ear turns air vibrations into nerve signals, and the brain turns those signals into the sounds we notice.
sci|28|l61-8|6-8|The Sounds We Can Hear — People can hear roughly 20 to 20,000 hertz, and many animals hear sounds outside that range.
sci|28|l61-9|6-8|Loud Enough to Hurt — Very loud sound, or loud sound for a long time, can damage the hair cells in your ears for good.
sci|28|l61-10|6-8|Seeing Sound on a Screen — An oscilloscope draws a sound as a line over time, so you can see its frequency and its amplitude.
sci|28|l62-1|6-8|A Beat Is a Repeating Event — A beat is an event that repeats at an even spacing in time, like a clock's tick.
sci|28|l62-2|6-8|Tempo in Beats per Minute — Tempo is the speed of the beat, measured in beats per minute (BPM).
sci|28|l62-3|6-8|Measuring Tempo Yourself — You can measure a song's tempo by counting beats for a set time and scaling up to one minute.
sci|28|l62-4|6-8|Bars and Counting to Four — Music groups beats into bars; most popular music counts four beats in each bar.
sci|28|l62-5|6-8|Sixteen Steps in a Bar — A drum machine cuts one bar of four beats into 16 equal steps, four steps for each beat.
sci|28|l62-6|6-8|Kick, Snare and Hi-Hat — Most beats are built from three roles: a low kick, a sharp snare and a quick hi-hat, each placed on certain steps.
sci|28|l62-7|6-8|Swing: Stretching Every Other Step — Swing delays every second step a little, turning a stiff, even rhythm into one that bounces.
sci|28|l62-8|6-8|Why a Steady Pulse Helps — People naturally sync their movements to a steady beat, and a predictable pulse can help the body and brain settle and focus.
sci|28|l62-9|6-8|Rhythms in Nature — Nature is full of rhythms that can be measured, and some of them change in a predictable way.
sci|28|l62-10|6-8|When a Beat Becomes a Note — A click repeated faster than about 20 times a second stops sounding like a rhythm and starts sounding like a pitch.
sci|28|l63-1|6-8|The First Machines That Kept Sound — The first recording machines turned the vibrations of sound into a line traced on a surface.
sci|28|l63-2|6-8|A Groove Is a Wave You Can Touch — A record's groove is a long spiral whose tiny side-to-side wiggles copy the shape of the sound wave.
sci|28|l63-3|6-8|Speed Matters — A record must spin at the speed it was made for; faster raises the pitch and tempo, slower lowers them.
sci|28|l63-4|6-8|Scratching — Scratching is moving a record back and forth by hand, which plays a slice of the recording forward and backward.
sci|28|l63-5|6-8|Pitch Up, Pitch Down — A pitch control changes a turntable's speed a little, so a DJ can make two records play at the same tempo.
sci|28|l63-6|6-8|Blending Two Records — Beatmatching lines up two records so their beats fall together, letting a DJ move from one song to the next without a bump.
sci|28|l63-7|6-8|Sampling: Measuring Many Times a Second — Digital recording measures a sound wave thousands of times each second and stores each measurement as a number.
sci|28|l63-8|6-8|Bit Depth: How Fine the Ruler Is — Bit depth sets how many different heights each sample can have; more bits means finer detail and less hiss.
sci|28|l63-9|6-8|The Crunch of a 1987 Sampler — A famous 1987 sampling drum machine used 12 bits at 26.04 kHz, and its limits gave its drums a gritty sound that shaped hip hop.
sci|28|l63-10|6-8|Analog and Digital Side by Side — Analog recording stores a continuous copy of the wave; digital recording stores a list of numbers, and each way has strengths.
sci|28|l64-1|6-8|Start With a Purpose — Good design starts with a clear purpose and a list of what the design must do.

sci|28|l64-9|6-8|Explain Your Beat Like a Scientist — A strong explanation makes a claim, backs it with measured evidence and uses science to explain why.

sci|5|l10-8|3-5|Magnets Push and Pull — A magnet can push or pull certain objects without touching them, and its two poles behave differently.
sci|5|l10-9|3-5|Static Electricity — Rubbing some materials together builds up static electricity, which can push or pull objects without touching them.
sci|5|l10-10|3-5|Solving a Problem With Magnets — Engineers use pushes and pulls, like the pull of a magnet, to design solutions to everyday problems.
sci|5|l11-1|3-5|Faster Means More Energy — Energy is the ability to make things happen, and a faster-moving object carries more energy.
sci|5|l11-2|3-5|Energy Passes in a Collision — When objects collide, energy moves from one object to the other, and the motion of both changes.
sci|5|l11-3|3-5|Stored Energy — Food, fuel, batteries and a marble held up high all store energy that can be released later.
sci|5|l11-4|3-5|Sound Carries Energy — Sound is energy that travels through air, water and solids as vibrations.
sci|5|l11-5|3-5|Light Carries Energy — Light carries energy from a source across space, even through empty space, and can warm what it lands on.
sci|5|l11-6|3-5|Heat Moves From Hot to Cold — Heat is energy that always moves from a warmer object to a cooler one.
sci|5|l11-7|3-5|Wires Carry Energy — Electricity moves energy through wires from where it is made to where it is used.
sci|5|l11-8|3-5|From Battery to Light and Motion — A circuit changes the stored energy in a battery into light, motion, sound or heat.
sci|5|l11-9|3-5|Designing an Energy Changer — Engineers design devices that change energy from one form to another, then test and improve them.
sci|5|l11-10|3-5|Testing and Improving — A fair test, where only one thing changes at a time, shows which design change actually helps.
sci|6|l12-1|3-5|What Counts as Matter — Matter is anything that has mass and takes up space, and that includes things you cannot see, like air.
sci|6|l12-2|3-5|Pieces Too Small to See — All matter is made of particles far too small to see, and a model helps us picture how they are arranged.
sci|6|l12-3|3-5|A Balloon on the Balance — A gas is matter: it has mass and takes up space, and a careful balance can prove it.
sci|6|l12-4|3-5|Ice Melts, Mass Stays — When a solid melts or a liquid freezes, its shape and look change, but the amount of matter does not.
sci|6|l12-5|3-5|Where the Sugar Went — When a solid dissolves, its particles spread out between the liquid's particles; the solid is still there, and the mass proves it.
sci|6|l12-6|3-5|Mixing Adds Up — When you mix two materials, the mass of the mixture equals the mass of the parts you put in.
sci|6|l12-7|3-5|When the Balance Seems Wrong — If mass seems to vanish during a change, matter has usually escaped as a gas; a closed container catches it.
sci|6|l12-8|3-5|Properties You Can Test — A property is something you can observe or measure about a material, and properties let you tell one material from another.
sci|6|l12-9|3-5|Conduct, Dissolve, Float — Whether a material carries electricity or heat, dissolves in water, or floats are properties you can test to identify it.
sci|6|l12-10|3-5|Some Mixtures Come Apart — Salt and water make a mixture, not a new substance; the salt can be taken back out unchanged.
sci|6|l12-11|3-5|Fizz Means Something New — Some mixing makes new substances with new properties; bubbles, a color change or a temperature change are clues that this has happened.
sci|6|l13-1|3-5|Waves in a Rope and a Pond — A wave is a repeating pattern of motion that moves energy from place to place while the material itself stays put.
sci|6|l13-2|3-5|Amplitude and Wavelength — Amplitude is how tall a wave is, and wavelength is the distance from one crest to the next; both can be measured and drawn.
sci|6|l13-3|3-5|Waves Move Things — Because waves carry energy, they can make objects move, from a bobbing toy boat to a fluttering sheet of paper.
sci|6|l13-4|3-5|Light Bounces Into Your Eyes — We see an object because light from a source bounces off it and enters our eyes; with no light, we see nothing.
sci|6|l13-5|3-5|Mirrors and Shadows — Light travels in straight lines, which is why mirrors show clear reflections and objects cast shadows.
sci|6|l13-6|3-5|Light Bends in Water — When light passes from air into water or glass, it slows and bends; that is why a straw looks broken and a lens can focus light.
sci|6|l13-7|3-5|Dots, Dashes and Blinks — A code turns letters into a pattern of short and long signals, so a flashlight or a wire can carry words.
sci|6|l13-8|3-5|Drum Talk — Beats on a drum can carry a message across a distance because people agree on what each pattern means.
sci|6|l13-9|3-5|Ones and Zeros — Computers send pictures, sounds and words as long strings of just two signals, on and off, called binary.
sci|6|l13-10|3-5|Clean Signals Over Long Distances — Digital signals of on and off can be copied and sent again without mistakes, which is why they carry messages so far.
sci|7|l14-1|3-5|The Same Four Stages — Every living thing is born, grows, may reproduce, and dies, and the cycle starts again with the next generation.
sci|7|l14-2|3-5|A Frog Changes Shape — A frog starts as an egg in water, lives as a tadpole with gills and a tail, and slowly changes into an air-breathing adult.
sci|7|l14-3|3-5|A Butterfly's Four Stages — A monarch passes through four stages, egg, caterpillar, chrysalis and adult, and each stage has a different job.
sci|7|l14-4|3-5|A Bean and an Oak — Plants have life cycles too: a seed sprouts, grows, makes flowers and seeds, and dies, quickly for a bean and slowly for an oak.
sci|7|l14-5|3-5|What a Trait Is — A trait is a feature of a living thing, and many traits are inherited, passed from parents to their young.
sci|7|l14-6|3-5|Why Brothers and Sisters Differ — Offspring get traits from both parents, so brothers and sisters are alike in many ways but never exactly the same.
sci|7|l14-7|3-5|The World Shapes Traits Too — Some traits are changed by the environment, by food, sunlight, weather and experience, not only by what parents pass on.
sci|7|l14-8|3-5|Traits That Help Find Food — Some traits give an individual an advantage in finding food, so that individual is more likely to survive and reproduce.
sci|7|l14-9|3-5|Traits That Help Find a Mate — Some traits help an animal attract a mate, so that animal is more likely to reproduce and pass those traits on.
sci|7|l14-10|3-5|Comparing Life Spans — Life cycles share the same stages, but the length of a life and of each stage varies enormously from one kind of living thing to another.
sci|7|l15-1|3-5|A Plant's Outside Parts — Roots, stems, leaves, flowers and thorns are external structures, and each one does a job that helps the plant survive, grow and reproduce.
sci|7|l15-2|3-5|An Animal's Outside Parts — Shells, fur, feathers, claws, beaks and skin are external structures that protect animals and help them move, eat and stay warm.
sci|7|l15-3|3-5|Parts on the Inside — Internal structures, the heart, lungs, stomach, bones and brain, work together to keep an animal alive and growing.
sci|7|l15-4|3-5|How the Eye Uses Light — We see because light bounces off objects, enters the eye through the pupil, and forms an image that the eye sends to the brain.
sci|7|l15-5|3-5|Messages to the Brain — Eyes, ears, skin, nose and tongue send information to the brain through nerves, and the brain uses it to decide what the body does next.
sci|7|l15-6|3-5|The Brain Decides — The brain uses information from the senses, plus memory, to respond quickly and to learn from what happened before.
sci|7|l15-7|3-5|Living in a Group — Living in a group helps some animals get food, stay safe and raise young better than they could alone.
sci|7|l15-8|3-5|When the Pond Dries Up — When an environment changes, some living things survive in place, some move to a new place, and some die.
sci|7|l15-9|3-5|How a Fossil Forms — A fossil is the preserved remains or trace of a living thing from long ago, usually formed when it was buried quickly in mud or sand.
sci|7|l15-10|3-5|Inside the Mazon Creek Concretions — Mazon Creek fossils near Braidwood, Illinois, are unusually complete because soft-bodied animals were buried fast in mud that hardened around them.
sci|7|l15-11|3-5|What Fossils Tell Us — By comparing fossils with living things and with the rocks around them, scientists learn what lived long ago and how the environment has changed.
sci|8|l16-1|3-5|The Old Idea About Soil — For a long time people thought plants ate soil, but a careful test showed that idea was wrong.
sci|8|l16-2|3-5|The Missing Weight — The willow gained about 164 pounds while the soil lost only two ounces, so the tree was not made of soil.
sci|8|l16-3|3-5|Air Is Real Stuff — Air is made of invisible gases that have weight, and plants take one of those gases in through their leaves.
sci|8|l16-4|3-5|Leaves Catch Light — Leaves are green because they hold a substance that catches the energy in sunlight.
sci|8|l16-5|3-5|Water, Air and Light Together — Plants use light energy to turn water and carbon dioxide into sugar, and they give off oxygen.
sci|8|l16-6|3-5|Sugar Put Away for Later — Plants store extra sugar as starch in roots, seeds and stems, and that stored food is what people and animals eat.
sci|8|l16-7|3-5|Animals Eat to Get Energy — Animals cannot make food from light, so they get energy by eating plants or other animals that ate plants.
sci|8|l16-8|3-5|Food Travels Through the Body — The body breaks food into tiny pieces, moves them into the blood, and carries them to every part that needs them.
sci|8|l16-9|3-5|Growing and Repairing — Food gives more than energy; it supplies the materials the body uses to grow and to fix itself.
sci|8|l16-10|3-5|Five Kinds of Food — A healthy meal has fruits, vegetables, grains, protein and dairy, with fruits and vegetables filling half the plate.
sci|8|l16-11|3-5|Energy In, Energy Out — The energy you eat should match the energy you use, and the sun is behind all of it.
sci|8|l17-1|3-5|Makers and Takers — Producers make their own food from sunlight, and consumers get food by eating producers or other consumers.
sci|8|l17-2|3-5|Food Chains — A food chain shows energy passing from the sun to a producer to consumers, and most energy is lost at each step.
sci|8|l17-3|3-5|Food Webs — In a real habitat many food chains cross and connect into a food web, so most animals have more than one food.
sci|8|l17-4|3-5|A Pond — In a pond, algae and water plants are the producers, and energy moves through insects, tadpoles, fish and herons.
sci|8|l17-5|3-5|A Tallgrass Prairie — Illinois was once mostly prairie, a grassland where deep-rooted grasses feed insects, birds, small mammals and their hunters.
sci|8|l17-6|3-5|An Oak Forest — In an oak forest, acorns and leaves feed a web of squirrels, mice, deer, owls and the fungi that recycle the fallen leaves.
sci|8|l17-7|3-5|The Work of Decomposers — Decomposers such as fungi, bacteria and earthworms return the matter in dead things to the soil, water and air.
sci|8|l17-8|3-5|The Cycle of Matter — Matter moves in a loop among plants, animals, decomposers, air, water and soil, so the same bits are used again and again.
sci|8|l17-9|3-5|A Healthy Ecosystem — An ecosystem is all the living things in a place plus the nonliving parts they need, and a healthy one has many kinds of living things in balance.
sci|8|l17-10|3-5|When a Link Is Removed — Taking one living thing out of a food web can change the numbers of many others in ways that are hard to predict.
sci|8|l17-11|3-5|People Protecting a Habitat — When people find what is breaking a web and fix it, living things can come back, as the bald eagle did.
sci|9|l18-1|3-5|What Weather Is — Weather is what the air is doing right now, and we measure it with tools instead of guessing.
sci|9|l18-2|3-5|Rain, Snow and the Gauge — Precipitation is any water that falls from clouds, and a rain gauge measures how much.
sci|9|l18-3|3-5|Where the Wind Comes From — Wind is moving air, and we measure both its direction and its speed.
sci|9|l18-4|3-5|Seasons in the Numbers — Weather data collected over a year shows a repeating pattern called the seasons.
sci|9|l18-5|3-5|Climate Is the Long Story — Climate is the usual weather of a place over many years; weather is what happens today.
sci|9|l18-6|3-5|Climates Around the World — Different places on Earth have very different climates because of sunlight, oceans, mountains and lakes.
sci|9|l18-7|3-5|Tornado Watch, Tornado Warning — A tornado is a spinning column of air from a storm cloud, and Illinois gets dozens of them each year.
sci|9|l18-8|3-5|Floods and Blizzards — Too much water or snow at once can be as dangerous as a tornado, and both have simple safety rules.
sci|9|l18-9|3-5|Building to Stay Safe — People cannot stop severe weather, but good design and planning can reduce the harm it causes.
sci|9|l18-10|3-5|Reading the Sky — Long before radar, people forecast weather by watching clouds, wind and the direction storms travel.
sci|9|l18-11|3-5|Tools That See Far — Modern forecasts combine radar, satellites, weather balloons and computers to predict days ahead.
sci|9|l19-1|3-5|Layers Like Pages — Rock forms in layers, with the oldest at the bottom, so a cliff is a stack of Earth's history.
sci|9|l19-2|3-5|Fossils in the Rock — Fossils are traces of living things preserved in rock, and they show that Illinois was once a warm swamp.
sci|9|l19-3|3-5|The Canyon's Story — The Grand Canyon's mile-deep walls show layer after layer that a river slowly cut through.
sci|9|l19-4|3-5|Weathering Breaks Rock — Weathering is the breaking of rock into smaller pieces by water, ice, plants and temperature changes.
sci|9|l19-5|3-5|Erosion Carries It Away — Erosion is the moving of rock pieces and soil by water, ice, wind or gravity.
sci|9|l19-6|3-5|Deposition Builds New Land — When moving water, wind or ice slows down, it drops what it carries, and that builds new land.
sci|9|l19-7|3-5|Fast Changes and Slow Ones — Some changes to Earth's surface happen in minutes, others take millions of years, and both leave evidence.
sci|9|l19-8|3-5|Rivers of Ice — A glacier is a huge mass of ice that flows slowly and scrapes, carries and drops rock as it goes.
sci|9|l19-9|3-5|What the Ice Left Behind — Melting glaciers dropped a thick blanket of rock, sand and clay that made Illinois flat and its soil rich.
sci|9|l19-10|3-5|Maps of Quakes and Volcanoes — Earthquakes and volcanoes are not scattered randomly; they line up in belts around Earth.
sci|9|l19-11|3-5|Designing for Shaking and Flooding — People cannot stop earthquakes or floods, but maps and smart design can keep more people safe.
sci|9|l20-1|3-5|Our Star, the Sun — The sun is a star like the ones at night; it looks huge and bright only because it is so much closer.
sci|9|l20-2|3-5|Why Stars Look Different — Stars look brighter or dimmer mostly because of how far away they are, and their distances are enormous.
sci|9|l20-3|3-5|Patterns in the Night Sky — Stars form patterns that stay the same night after night, so people have used them as a map.
sci|9|l20-4|3-5|Earth Spins — Earth rotates once every 24 hours, and that spin is what makes day and night.
sci|9|l20-5|3-5|The Sun's Daily Path — Because Earth spins, the sun appears to rise in the east, climb high toward the south, and set in the west.
sci|9|l20-6|3-5|Shadows Through the Day — A shadow's length and direction change through the day in a pattern that tracks the sun's path.
sci|9|l20-7|3-5|Why We Have Seasons — Earth's tilt, not its distance from the sun, causes the seasons by changing how directly sunlight hits us.
sci|9|l20-8|3-5|Stars of the Seasons — As Earth moves around the sun, the night side faces different directions, so different stars appear each season.
sci|9|l20-9|3-5|The Moon's Changing Face — The moon's phases repeat about every 29.5 days as we see different amounts of its sunlit half.
sci|9|l20-10|3-5|Down Means Toward the Center — Gravity pulls everything toward Earth's center, which is why 'down' points the same way everywhere on Earth.
sci|9|l21-1|3-5|Naming the Spheres — Scientists divide Earth into four systems: the geosphere, hydrosphere, atmosphere and biosphere.
sci|9|l21-2|3-5|Where the Spheres Meet — Earth's systems constantly interact: air moves water, water shapes rock, rock feeds plants, and living things change all three.
sci|9|l21-3|3-5|The Air Around Us — The atmosphere is a thin layer of mixed gases, mostly nitrogen and oxygen, that makes life on Earth possible.
sci|9|l21-4|3-5|A Watery World — Almost all of Earth's water is salty ocean; only a tiny share is the fresh water people can drink.
sci|9|l21-5|3-5|Water Under Your Feet — Groundwater fills the spaces in rock and soil underground and is where many towns get their drinking water.
sci|9|l21-6|3-5|The Lake Next Door — The Great Lakes hold about a fifth of the world's surface fresh water, and Chicago drinks from Lake Michigan every day.
sci|9|l21-7|3-5|Making a River Run Backward — In 1900 Chicago reversed its river to protect its drinking water, an engineering fix that moved the problem downstream.
sci|9|l21-8|3-5|Using What Earth Gives — People use Earth's resources for food, energy and materials, and that use changes the land, water and air.
sci|9|l21-9|3-5|Reduce, Reuse, Recycle — Using less, using things again, and recycling materials all save resources and cut waste.
sci|9|l21-10|3-5|Protecting Earth's Systems — People can repair some of the damage they cause, using science to protect water, land, air and living things.
med|1|l1-1|k-2|Tiny Living Things — Germs are tiny living things that are too small to see.
med|1|l1-2|k-2|Some Germs Make Us Sick — Most germs do not hurt us, but some germs can make us sick.
med|1|l1-3|k-2|Soap and Water — Washing with soap and water takes germs off your hands.
med|1|l1-4|k-2|Scrub for 20 Seconds — Scrub your hands with soap for about 20 seconds, as long as a short song.
med|1|l1-5|k-2|Cough Into Your Elbow — Cough or sneeze into a tissue or your elbow to keep germs off others.
med|1|l1-6|k-2|Brush and Bathe — Brushing teeth twice a day and taking baths keep your body clean.
med|1|l1-7|k-2|Keep Your Own Cup — When someone is sick, do not share cups, spoons or straws.
med|1|l2-1|k-2|Food Is Fuel — Food gives your body the energy it needs to move, think and grow.
med|1|l2-2|k-2|Many Kinds of Food — Eat many kinds of food: fruits, vegetables, grains, protein and milk foods.
med|1|l2-3|k-2|Water and Sometimes Foods — Water is the best drink, and sweets are sometimes foods.
med|1|l2-4|k-2|Why We Sleep — Sleep helps your body grow and rest, and helps your brain learn.
med|1|l2-5|k-2|How Much Sleep? — Children your age need about 10 hours of sleep each night.
med|1|l2-6|k-2|Move Every Day — Moving and playing every day makes your heart and muscles strong.
med|1|l2-7|k-2|Rest When You Are Tired — Resting when you are tired helps your body get ready to play again.
med|1|l2-8|k-2|Every Body Is Different — Each body is different, and that is okay.
med|10|l19-1|6-8|Jenner and Cowpox — In 1796 Edward Jenner showed that cowpox could protect a person from smallpox, the idea behind the first vaccine.
med|10|l19-2|6-8|Semmelweis and Clean Hands — In 1847 Ignaz Semmelweis showed that doctors washing their hands saved the lives of new mothers.
med|10|l19-3|6-8|Surgery Without Pain — In 1846 a public operation in Boston showed that ether could put a patient to sleep so surgery caused no pain.
med|10|l19-4|6-8|Snow's Map — John Snow mapped cholera deaths in 1854 and traced them to one water pump on Broad Street.
med|10|l19-5|6-8|The Birth of Epidemiology — Epidemiology studies who gets sick, where and when, to find causes and stop disease from spreading.
med|10|l19-6|6-8|Pasteur and Pasteurization — Louis Pasteur showed that tiny living microbes cause spoiling and disease, and that gentle heating can kill them.
med|10|l19-7|6-8|Pasteur and Rabies — In 1885 Pasteur's team gave a rabies vaccine to a bitten boy, Joseph Meister, who survived.
med|10|l19-8|6-8|Koch Finds the Germs — Robert Koch proved that specific bacteria cause specific diseases, finding the tuberculosis germ in 1882.
med|10|l19-9|6-8|Lister and Antiseptics — In 1867 Joseph Lister used carbolic acid to kill germs in wounds, and far fewer surgery patients died of infection.
med|10|l19-10|6-8|How the Big Idea Changed — Across the 1800s, evidence slowly replaced the miasma idea with germ theory, and that changed daily life.
med|10|l20-1|6-8|X-Rays Show the Inside — In 1895 Wilhelm Röntgen discovered X-rays, which let doctors see bones inside the body without surgery.
med|10|l20-2|6-8|Insulin for Diabetes — In 1921–22, Frederick Banting and Charles Best in Toronto helped discover insulin, which let people with type 1 diabetes live.
med|10|l20-3|6-8|Fleming's Discovery — In 1928 Alexander Fleming noticed that a mold made a substance, penicillin, that stopped bacteria from growing.
med|10|l20-4|6-8|Penicillin Goes to War — During World War II, scientists in Britain and the United States learned to make penicillin in huge amounts.
med|10|l20-5|6-8|When Bacteria Fight Back — Bacteria can become resistant to antibiotics, so these medicines must be used only when needed and as directed.
med|10|l20-6|6-8|Salk's Polio Vaccine — In 1955 Jonas Salk's polio vaccine was declared safe and effective, and polio cases in the U.S. soon fell sharply.
med|10|l20-7|6-8|The Shape of DNA — In 1953 scientists worked out that DNA is a double helix, which explains how living things pass on instructions.
med|10|l20-8|6-8|Clean Water and Sewers — Clean drinking water and sewers prevented huge numbers of deaths from diseases such as cholera and typhoid.
med|10|l20-9|6-8|Safe Food and Medicine — In 1906 the United States passed its first national food and drug safety laws after reports about unsafe food.
med|10|l20-10|6-8|How Life Expectancy Rose — Life expectancy in the U.S. rose by about thirty years during the 1900s, mostly because fewer children died young.
med|11|l21-1|6-8|Skin and Mucus — Skin and mucus are barriers that keep most germs out of the body before they can cause harm.
med|11|l21-2|6-8|Cells That Eat Germs — Innate immune cells respond fast to any invader, often by swallowing and destroying germs.
med|11|l21-3|6-8|Inflammation: Red, Warm, Swollen — Inflammation brings blood, fluid and defender cells to an injured or infected place, which causes redness, warmth and swelling.
med|11|l21-4|6-8|B Cells and T Cells — Lymphocytes called B cells and T cells learn to recognize one specific germ and attack it.
med|11|l21-5|6-8|Antibodies — Antibodies are Y-shaped proteins made by B cells that lock onto one antigen and mark or block a germ.
med|11|l21-6|6-8|Memory Cells — After an infection, memory cells stay in the body and let it respond faster and stronger the next time.
med|11|l21-7|6-8|How Vaccines Build Memory — A vaccine shows the immune system a harmless version or piece of a germ so it builds memory cells without the illness.
med|11|l21-8|6-8|Herd Immunity — When enough people in a community are immune, a germ has trouble spreading, which also protects people who cannot be vaccinated.
med|11|l21-9|6-8|Allergies — An allergy happens when the immune system overreacts to something harmless, like pollen or peanuts.
med|11|l21-10|6-8|When the Body Targets Itself — In an autoimmune condition, the immune system mistakes some of the body's own cells for invaders.
med|11|l21-11|6-8|How Scientists Found Out — We know about immunity because scientists observed carefully, tested ideas and changed their thinking when evidence demanded it.
med|11|l22-1|6-8|Food for a Growing Body — Teens grow fast, so they need a variety of foods that give energy, protein, calcium and other nutrients.
med|11|l22-2|6-8|Sleep: The Brain's Night Shift — Teens need about 8 to 10 hours of sleep a night, because sleep helps the brain learn and the body grow and heal.
med|11|l22-3|6-8|Moving Your Body — Young people are advised to get about 60 minutes of physical activity each day, which helps the heart, bones, mood and sleep.
med|11|l22-4|6-8|A Brain Under Construction — The teen brain is still developing, especially the prefrontal cortex, which helps with planning and self-control.
med|11|l22-5|6-8|Vaping and Nicotine — Most vapes contain nicotine, which is addictive and can affect the developing teen brain.
med|11|l22-6|6-8|Alcohol and Other Drugs — Alcohol and other drugs change how the brain works, and they carry extra risks for a brain that is still developing.
med|11|l22-7|6-8|Understanding Peer Pressure — Peer pressure is the pull to do what others your age do, and it can push toward good or risky choices.
med|11|l22-8|6-8|Refusal Skills — Refusal skills are clear, practiced ways to say no while staying calm and keeping your friendships.
med|11|l22-9|6-8|Stress and Anxiety — Stress is the body's response to a challenge, and there are skills that help calm it and ways to get help when it feels too big.
med|11|l22-10|6-8|Asking for Help — Asking for help is a strength, and trusted adults, counselors and the 988 Lifeline are there when things feel too heavy.
med|11|l22-11|6-8|Growing Up: Puberty — Puberty is a normal time of growth and change, and a parent, school nurse or doctor can answer questions about it.
med|12|l23-1|9-10|Lind's Comparison — A fair test compares similar groups that differ in only one way, the treatment.
med|12|l23-2|9-10|Random Groups — In a randomized controlled trial, chance decides who gets the treatment, so the groups start out alike.
med|12|l23-3|9-10|Placebos and Blinding — Blinding keeps expectations from changing a trial's results.
med|12|l23-4|9-10|The Placebo Effect — People often feel better after a placebo, so a real treatment must do better than a placebo to show it works.
med|12|l23-5|9-10|Why Numbers Matter — Larger trials give more reliable answers because chance plays a smaller role.
med|12|l23-6|9-10|Checked by Other Experts — Peer review means other experts check a study before it is published.
med|12|l23-7|9-10|Phases of a Drug Trial — In the United States, a new drug passes through trial phases before the FDA decides whether to approve it.
med|12|l23-8|9-10|Linked Is Not the Same as Caused — When two things happen together, one does not necessarily cause the other.
med|12|l23-9|9-10|Stronger and Weaker Evidence — Some kinds of studies give stronger evidence than others, from single stories up to reviews of many trials.
med|12|l23-10|9-10|Reviewing Many Studies — A systematic review gathers all good studies on one question and weighs them together.
med|12|l23-11|9-10|Reading a Health Headline — Ask a few careful questions before believing a health headline.
med|12|l24-1|9-10|New Cases and All Cases — Incidence counts new cases over a time; prevalence counts all current cases at one moment.
med|12|l24-2|9-10|Outbreak, Epidemic, Pandemic — An outbreak, epidemic and pandemic differ mainly in how widely a disease spreads.
med|12|l24-3|9-10|A Long Epidemic: HIV — HIV was first recognized in 1981, and research turned it from a usually fatal illness into a manageable one for many people.
med|12|l24-4|9-10|Tracing Contacts — Contact tracing finds people exposed to an infection so they can get tested, treated or stay home.
med|12|l24-5|9-10|What Framingham Found — A risk factor raises the chance of a disease; the Framingham Heart Study found several for heart disease.
med|12|l24-6|9-10|Smoking and Lung Cancer — Studies in the 1950s by Doll and Hill and others showed that smoking causes lung cancer.
med|12|l24-7|9-10|Watching Over Health — The CDC and the WHO track diseases and help communities respond, at home and around the world.
med|12|l24-8|9-10|Ending Smallpox — Vaccination and careful surveillance made smallpox the first human disease ever eradicated.
med|12|l24-9|9-10|Where We Live Matters — Social determinants, like housing, income and schools, shape health as much as medical care does.
med|12|l24-10|9-10|Public Health Choices — Public health measures protect groups, but people debate how to balance safety, freedom and cost.
med|13|l25-1|9-10|Qi and the Meridians — Traditional Chinese Medicine explains health as the smooth flow of qi through channels called meridians.
med|13|l25-2|9-10|Yin, Yang and Five Phases — TCM uses yin-yang and the five phases to describe balance and change in the body.
med|13|l25-3|9-10|TCM in China's Hospitals — In China, TCM and Western medicine are often practiced side by side, sometimes in the same hospital.
med|13|l25-4|9-10|Acupuncture and Chronic Pain — Studies suggest acupuncture may help some people with some kinds of chronic pain and nausea.
med|13|l25-5|9-10|What Sham Trials Show — In many trials, fake acupuncture works almost as well as real acupuncture, which raises questions about how it helps.
med|13|l25-6|9-10|Tai Chi for Balance — Tai chi, a slow Chinese exercise, has good evidence for improving balance and preventing falls in older adults.
med|13|l25-7|9-10|Tu Youyou and Project 523 — Tu Youyou used an ancient text as a clue and modern chemistry as a test to discover artemisinin.
med|13|l25-8|9-10|Artemisinin in the World — Artemisinin-based combination therapies are now a main treatment for malaria and have saved many lives.
med|13|l25-9|9-10|Herbs, Drugs and Contamination — Herbal products can interact with medicines or be contaminated, so tell your doctor about any herbs you take.
med|13|l25-10|9-10|Endangered Animals and Respect — Some traditional products use endangered animals, and respectful talk about TCM includes this problem and the evidence.
med|13|l26-1|9-10|The Three Doshas — Ayurveda teaches that health depends on the balance of three doshas: vata, pitta and kapha.
med|13|l26-2|9-10|Diet and Daily Routine — Ayurveda gives great weight to food, sleep and a steady daily routine.
med|13|l26-3|9-10|India's Ministry of AYUSH — India's government recognizes Ayurveda and several other traditions through its Ministry of AYUSH.
med|13|l26-4|9-10|Yoga and Low Back Pain — Yoga can help some adults with chronic low back pain a little, about as much as other exercise.
med|13|l26-5|9-10|Meditation and Stress — Meditation programs have evidence for easing stress and anxiety for some people.
med|13|l26-6|9-10|Turmeric in the Lab and in People — Curcumin from turmeric looks promising in lab studies, but proof of benefit in people is limited.
med|13|l26-7|9-10|Heavy Metals in Some Products — Some imported Ayurvedic products have been found to contain lead, mercury or arsenic.
med|13|l26-8|9-10|Unani Medicine — Unani medicine grew from Greek ideas carried forward by Arabic and Persian physicians and is practiced today in South Asia.
med|13|l26-9|9-10|Kampo in Japan — Kampo is Japan's adaptation of Chinese herbal medicine, and many Kampo formulas are used within Japan's national health system.
med|13|l26-10|9-10|Indigenous and Curandero Traditions — Indigenous healers and curanderos in the Americas treat body, mind, family and spirit together.
med|13|l26-11|9-10|Integrative Clinics and Telling Your Doctor — Integrative medicine combines proven complementary practices with standard care, and it starts with telling your doctor everything you take.
med|14|l27-1|9-10|A Parasite Carried by Mosquitoes — Malaria is caused by a tiny parasite that female Anopheles mosquitoes pass from person to person.
med|14|l27-2|9-10|Nets and Sprays — Insecticide-treated bed nets and indoor spraying stop mosquitoes from biting, which prevents many malaria cases.
med|14|l27-3|9-10|From an Ancient Text to a Nobel Prize — Artemisinin, a key malaria drug, was found by testing a plant named in an old Chinese medical text, and new vaccines now add protection.
med|14|l27-4|9-10|Tuberculosis: An Old Disease — Tuberculosis is a bacterial lung infection that spreads through the air and can be cured with months of antibiotics.
med|14|l27-5|9-10|HIV and the Treatment Revolution — HIV has no cure yet, but daily antiretroviral therapy lets people live long, healthy lives and stops the virus from spreading through sex.
med|14|l27-6|9-10|Cholera and Clean Water — Cholera spreads through water tainted by human waste, so clean water and sanitation prevent it, and oral rehydration saves lives.
med|14|l27-7|9-10|Measles and the Power of Vaccines — Measles is one of the most contagious diseases, and two doses of vaccine protect about 97 out of 100 people.
med|14|l27-8|9-10|Neglected Tropical Diseases — Neglected tropical diseases affect over a billion of the world's poorest people, and some, like Guinea worm, are close to being wiped out.
med|14|l27-9|9-10|Polio, Nearly Gone — Vaccines have cut wild polio cases by more than 99 percent since 1988, leaving only a few places where it still spreads.
med|14|l27-10|9-10|Global Health Organizations — The WHO sets health guidance for the world, while groups like Gavi and the Global Fund pay for vaccines and treatment in poorer countries.
med|14|l27-11|9-10|Poverty, Place and Disease — Where people live and how much money they have strongly shape which diseases they catch and whether they get treated.
med|14|l28-1|9-10|Heart Disease and Stroke — Heart disease and stroke, both caused mostly by damaged blood vessels, are the two leading causes of death in the world.
med|14|l28-2|9-10|Cancer — Cancer is a group of diseases in which cells grow out of control, and some cancers can be prevented or caught early.
med|14|l28-3|9-10|Diabetes — Diabetes means the body cannot use sugar in the blood well; type 2, the most common kind, is strongly linked to lifestyle and can often be delayed or prevented.
med|14|l28-4|9-10|Tobacco and Smoking — Tobacco is the leading preventable cause of death worldwide, and quitting at any age lowers the risk.
med|14|l28-5|9-10|Blood Pressure: The Quiet Risk — High blood pressure usually has no symptoms, so regular checks matter, and it can be lowered with habits and medicine.
med|14|l28-6|9-10|Food and Movement — Eating mostly whole foods and staying active lower the risk of most chronic diseases.
med|14|l28-7|9-10|What Okinawa Taught — Traditional Okinawan life combined a plant-rich, modest-calorie diet with activity and close friendships, and the benefits faded when those habits changed.
med|14|l28-8|9-10|The Mediterranean Diet — The traditional Mediterranean way of eating has been linked in studies to less heart disease, though researchers debate how strong the evidence is.
med|14|l28-9|9-10|Food Environments — The food that is nearby, cheap and advertised shapes what people eat, so healthy choices are easier in some places than others.
med|14|l28-10|9-10|Screening: Finding Disease Early — Screening tests look for disease before symptoms appear, which can save lives, but every test has trade-offs.
med|14|l28-11|9-10|Prevention or Treatment? — Preventing disease across a whole population can save more lives than treating each sick person, though both are needed.
med|14|l28-12|9-10|Culture, Food and Movement — Culture shapes how people eat and move, and healthy habits last longest when they fit a community's own traditions.
med|15|l29-1|11-12|The Beveridge Model — In the Beveridge model, the government pays for care with taxes and often runs the hospitals itself.
med|15|l29-2|11-12|The Bismarck Model — In the Bismarck model, workers and employers pay into insurance funds, and those funds pay for care.
med|15|l29-3|11-12|National Health Insurance — In national health insurance, one public plan pays the bills, while doctors and hospitals stay mostly private or independent.
med|15|l29-4|11-12|The Out-of-Pocket Model — Where there is little insurance, people pay for care themselves, and many go without or fall into debt.
med|15|l29-5|11-12|India and China — India and China, the two most populous countries, are expanding public insurance while many people still pay out of pocket.
med|15|l29-6|11-12|Insurance Through Work — Most Americans under 65 get health insurance through a job, a pattern that grew during and after World War II.
med|15|l29-7|11-12|Medicare and Medicaid — Since 1965, Medicare has covered older Americans and Medicaid has covered many people with low incomes.
med|15|l29-8|11-12|The Affordable Care Act — The Affordable Care Act of 2010 expanded coverage, and Americans still disagree about it and about what should come next.
med|15|l29-9|11-12|Costs and Spending — The United States spends more on health care per person than any other wealthy country.
med|15|l29-10|11-12|Waits, Coverage and Outcomes — Every system trades off cost, coverage, wait times and choice, and people weigh those trade-offs differently.
med|15|l30-1|11-12|Culturally Responsive Care — Culturally responsive care means learning each patient's beliefs and needs and shaping care to fit them.
med|15|l30-2|11-12|Working With Interpreters — Trained medical interpreters help patients and clinicians understand each other accurately and safely.
med|15|l30-3|11-12|Listening as a Medical Skill — Careful listening is a medical skill that helps clinicians find the problem and helps patients follow the plan.
med|15|l30-4|11-12|Who Makes the Decision? — In many cultures, health decisions are made by the family together, and good care makes room for that.
med|15|l30-5|11-12|The Spirit Catches You — Anne Fadiman's book about a Hmong family and their doctors shows how misunderstanding can harm care even when everyone means well.
med|15|l30-6|11-12|Working With Traditional Healers — The WHO encourages countries to bring safe, proven traditional medicine into health systems while protecting patients.
med|15|l30-7|11-12|Why Some People Distrust Medicine — Past harms by medical researchers help explain why some communities are slow to trust health care today.
med|15|l30-8|11-12|Health Disparities — Health disparities are gaps in health between groups, and they come from many causes that people can work to change.
med|15|l30-9|11-12|A Health Workforce for Everyone — Community health workers, diverse clinicians and patient voices help health systems serve every culture well.
med|16|l31-1|11-12|Autonomy and Beneficence — Good care respects a patient's right to choose and also works for the patient's good.
med|16|l31-2|11-12|Do No Harm, Be Fair — Non-maleficence means avoiding needless harm, and justice means sharing care and burdens fairly.
med|16|l31-3|11-12|Informed Consent — Informed consent means a patient agrees to care after understanding its purpose, risks, benefits and choices.
med|16|l31-4|11-12|The Nuremberg Code — After Nazi doctors' crimes, the 1947 Nuremberg Code said voluntary consent is essential in research.
med|16|l31-5|11-12|The Tuskegee Study — For 40 years, a U.S. government study watched Black men with syphilis without telling them or treating them.
med|16|l31-6|11-12|Henrietta Lacks and HeLa — HeLa cells helped medicine enormously, but they were taken without consent, raising questions about fairness.
med|16|l31-7|11-12|The Belmont Report — The 1979 Belmont Report set three principles for U.S. research: respect for persons, beneficence and justice.
med|16|l31-8|11-12|Organ Donation — Transplants save lives, and ethics guides how organs are given and shared.
med|16|l31-9|11-12|Sharing Scarce Care — When there is not enough care for all, ethics asks for clear, fair rules decided ahead of time.
med|16|l31-10|11-12|Genetic Testing and Privacy — Genetic tests can guide care, but genetic information needs careful privacy protection.
med|16|l32-1|11-12|Dorothea Dix and the Asylums — In the 1800s, reformers like Dorothea Dix pushed for hospitals that treated mental illness with care, not punishment.
med|16|l32-2|11-12|Leaving the Big Hospitals — In the late 1900s, care moved from large hospitals to communities, with gains and real gaps.
med|16|l32-3|11-12|Depression and Anxiety — Depression and anxiety are common, real health conditions, and they are treatable.
med|16|l32-4|11-12|Talk Therapy and CBT — Cognitive behavioral therapy helps people notice and change thought patterns, and strong evidence supports it.
med|16|l32-5|11-12|Medicine and Daily Habits — Medication can help some conditions, and sleep, movement and connection support every mind.
med|16|l32-6|11-12|Addiction and the Brain — Addiction is a treatable brain condition, not a failure of character.
med|16|l32-7|11-12|How Stigma Is Changing — Stigma keeps people from seeking help, and open talk from public figures is helping change it.
med|16|l32-8|11-12|Neurodiversity — Neurodiversity describes the natural variety of human minds, including autistic people and people with ADHD or dyslexia.
med|16|l32-9|11-12|Mindfulness From Buddhist Roots — Mindfulness began in Buddhist practice and has been studied as a support for stress, anxiety and depression.
med|16|l32-10|11-12|Mental Health Across Cultures — Cultures describe distress in different ways, and good care listens to a person's own words and beliefs.
med|16|l32-11|11-12|988 and School Supports — Help is close: 988 is available any time, and schools have trained people ready to listen.
med|17|l33-1|11-12|How mRNA Vaccines Work — An mRNA vaccine gives cells short-lived instructions to make one harmless piece of a germ, so the immune system learns to recognize it.
med|17|l33-2|11-12|CRISPR: Editing a Gene — CRISPR lets scientists cut DNA at a chosen spot, which makes it possible to change a gene far more precisely than before.
med|17|l33-3|11-12|The Ethics of Editing — Editing body cells to treat one patient is widely accepted with care, while editing embryos that pass changes to future generations remains strongly contested.
med|17|l33-4|11-12|Personalized Medicine — Personalized medicine uses a person's genes and the features of their illness to choose the treatment most likely to help them.
med|17|l33-5|11-12|AI in Diagnosis — Artificial intelligence can find patterns in medical images and records, but it needs testing, fair data and human oversight.
med|17|l33-6|11-12|Care Across Distance — Telehealth brings some care to patients by video, phone and messages, which helps many people but does not replace every in-person visit.
med|17|l33-7|11-12|When Antibiotics Stop Working — Bacteria can evolve resistance to antibiotics, so using antibiotics only when needed helps keep them working.
med|17|l33-8|11-12|A World Growing Older — People are living longer, so health systems are planning for more older adults, many of whom live with long-term conditions.
med|17|l33-9|11-12|Caring at the Bedside — Nurses, doctors, pharmacists, therapists and EMTs each do different work and need different training and a state license.
med|17|l33-10|11-12|Research, Public Health and More — Many health careers work behind the scenes or in communities, and some traditional practices, like acupuncture, are licensed by most states.
med|17|l34-1|11-12|Choosing Your Question — A strong capstone question is about something you care about, can be answered with evidence, and is narrow enough to study.
med|17|l34-2|11-12|Four Parts of a Clear Question — Clinical questions are often built from four parts: the people, the treatment, the comparison and the outcome.
med|17|l34-3|11-12|Not All Evidence Is Equal — Systematic reviews of well-designed trials usually give stronger evidence than single studies, expert opinion or personal stories.
med|17|l34-4|11-12|Trusted Places to Look — Public health agencies, research institutes and peer-reviewed journals are reliable starting points for health evidence.
med|17|l34-5|11-12|Spotting Weak Claims — Watch for red flags, such as miracle promises, hidden sellers and no named evidence, whether a claim is about a modern or a traditional treatment.
med|17|l34-6|11-12|What Makes a Test Fair — Fair tests use a comparison group, assign people by chance, and when possible keep people from knowing who got which treatment.
med|17|l34-7|11-12|Case Study: Tai Chi and Falls — Tai chi comes from Chinese martial and health traditions, and trials suggest it can reduce falls in older adults.
med|17|l34-8|11-12|Case Study: Saving Children's Lives — Deaths of children under five fell by more than half worldwide since 1990, thanks to many simple, tested public-health steps.
med|17|l34-9|11-12|Known, Unknown, Concluded — A strong conclusion separates what the evidence shows, what is still unknown, and what you conclude and why.
med|17|l34-10|11-12|Presenting With Respect — Present your findings clearly, cite every source, and speak about every tradition and every person with respect.
med|2|l3-1|k-2|Doctors and Nurses — Doctors and nurses help us stay well and help us get better when we are sick.

med|2|l3-8|k-2|Calling 911 — In an emergency, call 911 to get help fast.

med|2|l4-4|k-2|Everyone's Brain Is Different — Every brain works in its own way, and that is okay.

med|2|l4-8|k-2|Kindness Helps Everyone — Kind words and actions help everyone feel safe.
med|3|l5-1|k-2|People Everywhere Care — People all over the world have cared for their health for a very long time.

med|3|l5-3|k-2|Stretching with Yoga — In India, many people do yoga to stretch, breathe and feel calm.

med|3|l6-1|k-2|Helmets On — Wear a helmet every time you ride a bike or scooter.
med|3|l6-2|k-2|Buckle Up — Car seats and seat belts keep you safe in a car.
med|3|l6-3|k-2|Crossing the Street — Hold a grown-up's hand, stop, and look both ways before you cross.
med|3|l6-4|k-2|Sunscreen and Hats — Sunscreen, hats and shade protect your skin from the sun.

med|4|l7-1|3-5|Your Skeleton — An adult skeleton has 206 bones that hold the body up and protect the soft parts inside.
med|4|l7-2|3-5|Muscles Pull — Muscles move the body by pulling on bones, and many muscles work in pairs.
med|4|l7-3|3-5|Joints Let You Bend — Joints are the places where bones meet, and they let the body bend, twist and turn.
med|4|l7-4|3-5|The Heart Pumps — The heart is a muscle that pumps blood to every part of the body, all day and all night.
med|4|l7-5|3-5|Roads for Blood — Arteries carry blood away from the heart, veins bring it back, and tiny capillaries connect them.
med|4|l7-6|3-5|Lungs and Breathing — The lungs bring oxygen into the body and send carbon dioxide out.
med|4|l7-7|3-5|The Food Journey — The digestive system breaks food into tiny parts the body can use for energy and growth.
med|4|l7-8|3-5|Brain and Nerves — The brain is the body's control center, and nerves carry its messages to and from every part.
med|4|l7-9|3-5|Systems Work Together — When you run, your muscles, heart, lungs and brain all change together to meet the body's needs.
med|4|l7-10|3-5|Skin, the Largest Organ — Skin is the body's largest organ; it protects you, helps you feel, and helps keep you cool.
med|4|l8-1|3-5|Carbohydrates for Energy — Carbohydrates are the body's main source of energy, and whole grains give steady energy.
med|4|l8-2|3-5|Protein Builds — Protein helps the body build and repair muscles, skin and other parts.
med|4|l8-3|3-5|Fats Help Too — The body needs some fat for energy and health, and fats from plants and fish are good choices.
med|4|l8-4|3-5|Vitamins and Minerals — Vitamins and minerals are needed in small amounts, and colorful foods help you get many of them.
med|4|l8-5|3-5|Water Keeps You Going — Water is a nutrient too; it makes up much of your body and helps every system work.
med|4|l8-6|3-5|MyPlate — MyPlate shows a balanced meal: half fruits and vegetables, plus grains, protein and dairy.
med|4|l8-7|3-5|Reading a Food Label — The Nutrition Facts label tells you the serving size and what nutrients are in a packaged food.
med|4|l8-8|3-5|Sugar and Your Teeth — Germs in the mouth use sugar to make acid that can harm teeth, so brushing and fewer sugary drinks help.
med|4|l8-9|3-5|Healthy Foods Around the World — Families around the world eat many different healthy meals that give the body the same kinds of nutrients.
med|4|l8-10|3-5|Breakfast and Balance — Breakfast refuels the body for learning, and no single food is 'bad'; balance and variety over the day matter most.
med|5|l9-1|3-5|Bacteria Are Tiny Living Things — Bacteria are tiny one-celled living things; most are harmless or helpful, but a few can make us sick.
med|5|l9-2|3-5|Viruses Need a Host — Viruses are even smaller than bacteria, and they can only make more of themselves inside a living cell.
med|5|l9-3|3-5|How Germs Travel — Germs spread through the air, by touch, and in food and water, so washing hands and covering coughs help.
med|5|l9-4|3-5|White Blood Cells on Guard — The immune system uses white blood cells to find and destroy germs that get into the body.
med|5|l9-5|3-5|A Fever Is a Fight — A fever is a higher body temperature that often means the immune system is fighting germs.
med|5|l9-6|3-5|How a Vaccine Works — A vaccine teaches the immune system to know a germ, so the body is ready before the real germ arrives.
med|5|l9-7|3-5|Edward Jenner Tests an Idea — In 1796 Edward Jenner used cowpox to protect a boy from smallpox, which led to the first vaccine.
