med|5|l9-8|3-5|The End of Smallpox — Vaccines used all over the world wiped out smallpox, and in 1980 it was declared gone.
med|5|l9-9|3-5|Antibiotics Fight Bacteria — Antibiotics are medicines that kill bacteria or stop them from growing.
med|5|l9-10|3-5|Why Antibiotics Skip Viruses — Antibiotics work on bacteria but not on viruses, so they do not help a cold or the flu.
med|5|l10-1|3-5|Cleaning a Small Cut — For a small cut, wash it with clean water, cover it with a bandage and tell an adult.
med|5|l10-2|3-5|Helping a Nosebleed — For a nosebleed, sit up, lean forward and pinch the soft part of the nose.
med|5|l10-3|3-5|Cooling a Burn — For a small burn, cool it under cool running water and tell an adult; do not use ice or butter.
med|5|l10-4|3-5|When to Call 911 — Call 911 when someone is in danger right now, like when they cannot breathe, will not wake up or is badly hurt.
med|5|l10-5|3-5|What to Say on the Call — On a 911 call, say what happened and where you are, answer the questions and do not hang up until told.
med|5|l10-6|3-5|Calling Poison Control — If someone may have swallowed or touched something harmful, an adult can call Poison Control at 1-800-222-1222.
med|5|l10-7|3-5|What an Allergy Is — An allergy is when the immune system reacts strongly to something that is harmless for most people.
med|5|l10-8|3-5|Epinephrine Pens — An epinephrine pen is medicine for a severe allergic reaction, used by adults, trained helpers or the person it belongs to.
med|5|l10-9|3-5|Staying Calm Helps — Taking slow breaths helps you think clearly so you can remember the steps and get help.
med|5|l10-10|3-5|A First-Aid Kit — A first-aid kit holds supplies for small hurts, and knowing where it is helps you act fast.
med|5|l10-11|3-5|Helping a Friend Get an Adult — When a friend is hurt, one of the best ways to help is to get a trusted adult quickly.
med|6|l11-1|3-5|Hippocrates Looks Closely — Hippocrates of ancient Greece taught that illness has natural causes that doctors can watch and study.
med|6|l11-2|3-5|The Four Humors — For about 2,000 years, doctors believed in four humors, until science replaced that idea.
med|6|l11-3|3-5|A Promise to Patients — The Hippocratic Oath is an old promise that doctors will help the sick and not harm them.
med|6|l11-4|3-5|Nightingale Cleans Up — Florence Nightingale showed that clean, airy hospitals help sick people get well.
med|6|l11-5|3-5|Counting Saves Lives — Nightingale used numbers and charts to prove that clean hospitals save lives.
med|6|l11-6|3-5|Pasteur Finds Germs — Louis Pasteur showed that tiny living germs cause spoiling and many diseases.
med|6|l11-7|3-5|Clara Barton Helps — Clara Barton cared for soldiers and founded the American Red Cross in 1881.
med|6|l11-8|3-5|Hospitals, Labs and Medicines — Today doctors, nurses, labs and tested medicines work together to help people heal.
med|6|l11-9|3-5|Testing What Works — Scientists test medicines on groups of people to find out if they are safe and if they work.
med|6|l12-1|3-5|Qi, Yin and Yang — Traditional Chinese Medicine teaches that health comes from balance and the smooth flow of qi.
med|6|l12-2|3-5|Thin Needles — Acupuncture uses very thin needles, and studies show it may help some kinds of pain for some people.
med|6|l12-3|3-5|Herbs and Moving Slowly — TCM uses herbs and gentle movement like tai chi and qigong, and tai chi can help balance.
med|6|l12-4|3-5|The Science of Life — Ayurveda is an old healing tradition from India that teaches about three doshas in balance.
med|6|l12-5|3-5|Yoga and Breathing — Yoga comes from India and can help with stretching, strength and feeling calm.
med|6|l12-6|3-5|Food and Spices — Ayurveda uses food and spices for balance, and some remedies need care because they may not be tested or clean.
med|6|l12-7|3-5|Used by Millions — Traditional medicine is used in most countries in the world, so it matters to study it carefully.
med|6|l12-8|3-5|How Scientists Test — Scientists test traditional practices with fair tests, and results show some help and some do not.
med|6|l12-9|3-5|One Team for Your Health — Tell your doctor about everything you use, so all your helpers can work together safely.
med|7|l13-1|3-5|Feelings Start in the Brain — Your brain makes feelings, and feelings are signals that help you notice what is going on.
med|7|l13-2|3-5|Mental Health Is Health — Mental health is how we think, feel and get along, and it matters as much as body health.
med|7|l13-3|3-5|Naming Your Feelings — Putting a name on a feeling can help you understand it and calm it.
med|7|l13-4|3-5|What Stress Feels Like — Stress is the body's way of getting ready for a challenge, and a little is normal.
med|7|l13-5|3-5|Breathing and Moving — Slow breathing and moving your body are two simple tools that help calm stress.
med|7|l13-6|3-5|Talking and Sleeping — Talking to someone you trust and getting enough sleep both help your mind handle stress.
med|7|l13-7|3-5|Worry and Sadness Are Common — Many kids feel worried or sad, and help is available when those feelings stay a long time.
med|7|l13-8|3-5|People Who Help — Counselors, psychologists and doctors are trained to help with feelings and mental health.
med|7|l13-9|3-5|Different Minds — People's brains learn and feel in different ways, and each mind has strengths.
med|7|l13-10|3-5|Kindness and Including Others — Being kind and including others helps everyone's mental health, including your own.
med|7|l13-11|3-5|Screens and Sleep — Putting screens away before bed helps your brain rest and your mood stay steady.
med|7|l13-12|3-5|Your Calm-Down Plan — A calm-down plan lists the tools that help you, so you can use them when feelings get big.
med|7|l14-1|3-5|Growth Spurts — Children grow steadily, and sometimes the body grows quickly in a burst called a growth spurt.
med|7|l14-2|3-5|Fuel for Growing — A growing body needs more food, water and sleep to build bones and muscles.
med|7|l14-3|3-5|Every Body Has Its Own Clock — Bodies grow and change at different times, and that is normal.
med|7|l14-4|3-5|Baby Teeth and Adult Teeth — Kids have 20 baby teeth that fall out and are replaced by up to 32 adult teeth.
med|7|l14-5|3-5|Eyes and Glasses — Eyes can change as you grow, and glasses help bend light so you see clearly.
med|7|l14-6|3-5|Protecting Your Hearing — Your ears turn sound into signals, and very loud sounds over time can harm hearing.
med|7|l14-7|3-5|Muscles Grow with Use — When you use your muscles, they grow stronger over time.
med|7|l14-8|3-5|An Hour of Moving — Health experts say kids need about an hour of active play every day.
med|7|l14-9|3-5|Asking About Changes — As you grow, your body will change, and trusted adults can answer your questions.
med|8|l15-1|6-8|The Edwin Smith Papyrus — The Edwin Smith Papyrus, from about 1600 BCE, describes injuries case by case, using careful looking and touching.
med|8|l15-2|6-8|The Ebers Papyrus — The Ebers Papyrus, from about 1550 BCE, collects hundreds of remedies and mixes practical care with spells.
med|8|l15-3|6-8|What Egypt Passed On — Egypt's written, organized approach to injuries and remedies influenced later healers around the Mediterranean.
med|8|l15-4|6-8|Temples of Asclepius — Many ancient Greeks sought healing at temples of the god Asclepius, where they slept and hoped for a helpful dream.
med|8|l15-5|6-8|Hippocrates of Kos — Hippocrates and his followers taught that illness has natural causes that healers can observe and study.
med|8|l15-6|6-8|The Four Humors — Greek medicine taught that health came from a balance of four body fluids called humors.
med|8|l15-7|6-8|Why the Humors Were Replaced — New evidence about cells and germs in the 1800s showed the humors theory was wrong, so doctors set it aside.
med|8|l15-8|6-8|Galen of Pergamon — Galen, a Greek doctor in the Roman Empire in the 100s CE, studied anatomy through animal dissection and wrote many books.
med|8|l15-9|6-8|Galen's Long Shadow — Galen's books guided doctors for over 1,300 years, until Andreas Vesalius corrected many of his mistakes in 1543.
med|8|l15-10|6-8|Water, Baths and Public Health — Rome built aqueducts, baths and sewers that brought clean water to many people and carried waste away.
med|8|l16-1|6-8|The Yellow Emperor's Classic — The Huangdi Neijing, compiled over 2,000 years ago, is a founding book of Traditional Chinese Medicine that teaches qi and yin-yang.
med|8|l16-2|6-8|Five Phases and Meridians — TCM links the body to five phases and teaches that qi travels along channels called meridians.
med|8|l16-3|6-8|Reading the Pulse — TCM doctors feel the pulse at three places on each wrist and also look at the tongue to judge a patient's health.
med|8|l16-4|6-8|Acupuncture and Herbs — Acupuncture and herbal formulas are key TCM treatments; evidence supports some uses and not others.
med|8|l16-5|6-8|The Charaka Samhita — The Charaka Samhita is a classic Ayurvedic book about medicine that stresses diet, daily habits and prevention.
med|8|l16-6|6-8|Sushruta the Surgeon — The Sushruta Samhita is an ancient Indian book on surgery that describes many instruments, procedures and training methods.
med|8|l16-7|6-8|Noses and Cataracts — The Sushruta Samhita describes rebuilding a nose with skin from the face and treating cataracts by moving the clouded lens.
med|8|l16-8|6-8|The Three Doshas — Ayurveda teaches that three doshas, vata, pitta and kapha, shape each person's body and health.
med|8|l16-9|6-8|TCM in the Modern World — TCM is used today in China and around the world, often alongside modern medicine, and is studied by scientists.
med|8|l16-10|6-8|Ayurveda Today — Ayurveda is widely used in India and elsewhere today; studies test its practices, and some products have safety concerns.
med|9|l17-1|6-8|The Bimaristan — Cities in the Islamic world built large hospitals, called bimaristans, that cared for rich and poor alike.
med|9|l17-2|6-8|Great Hospitals of Three Cities — Famous hospitals in Baghdad, Damascus and Cairo became centers of care and teaching.
med|9|l17-3|6-8|Al-Razi Tells Two Diseases Apart — Al-Razi used careful observation to show that smallpox and measles are different diseases.
med|9|l17-4|6-8|Ibn Sina Writes the Canon — Ibn Sina's Canon of Medicine, finished about 1025, gathered the medical knowledge of his time into one huge work.
med|9|l17-5|6-8|A Textbook for Centuries — The Canon was used to teach doctors in Europe and Asia for several hundred years.
med|9|l17-6|6-8|Ibn al-Nafis and the Lungs — In the 1200s, Ibn al-Nafis correctly described blood passing from the heart through the lungs and back.
med|9|l17-7|6-8|From Arabic to Latin — Translators turned Arabic medical books into Latin, and this knowledge helped shape European medicine.
med|9|l17-8|6-8|Unani Medicine Today — Unani medicine, built on Greek and Arabic ideas, is still practiced today, especially in South Asia.
med|9|l17-9|6-8|Care in the Monasteries — In medieval Europe, monasteries cared for the sick, grew healing plants and copied medical books.
med|9|l17-10|6-8|The Black Death — The Black Death of 1347 to 1351 killed a large share of people in Europe and the Middle East, and it led to early quarantine rules.
med|9|l18-1|6-8|The Rule of Galen — For over a thousand years, doctors trusted Galen's books about the body, even though he had mostly studied animals.
med|9|l18-2|6-8|On the Fabric of the Human Body — In 1543, Vesalius published a detailed book of human anatomy based on what he saw himself.
med|9|l18-3|6-8|Pictures That Traveled — The printing press let accurate pictures of the body be copied exactly and shared widely.
med|9|l18-4|6-8|Harvey Does the Math — William Harvey used measurement to show that blood must travel in a loop through the body.
med|9|l18-5|6-8|The Valves in the Veins — Simple experiments with valves in the veins showed that blood in the veins flows toward the heart.
med|9|l18-6|6-8|The Missing Link — Harvey could not see how blood passed from arteries to veins, but tiny vessels found later with a microscope filled the gap.
med|9|l18-7|6-8|Paré and the Boiling Oil — When Ambroise Paré ran out of boiling oil, he found that a gentler dressing helped wounded soldiers heal better.
med|9|l18-8|6-8|Tying Off Blood Vessels — Paré used thread to tie off bleeding blood vessels during amputations instead of burning them.
med|9|l18-9|6-8|Leeuwenhoek's Little Animals — In the 1670s, Antonie van Leeuwenhoek used tiny homemade lenses to see living things too small for the eye.
med|9|l18-10|6-8|See for Yourself — Between 1500 and 1700, doctors began to trust observation and experiment more than old authority.
mth|1|l1-1|k-2|One Number for Each Thing — Say one number for each thing you count. Do not skip any. Do not count one twice.
mth|1|l1-2|k-2|The Last Number Tells How Many — The last number you say tells how many things there are in all.
mth|1|l1-3|k-2|Zero Means None — Zero means there is nothing to count. We write it as 0.
mth|1|l1-4|k-2|Counting On — You can start counting from any number. You do not have to start at one.
mth|1|l1-5|k-2|Counting by Tens — Count by tens: 10, 20, 30, up to 100. Each jump adds ten.
mth|1|l1-6|k-2|Counting by Twos and Fives — Count by twos: 2, 4, 6, 8. Count by fives: 5, 10, 15, 20.
mth|1|l1-7|k-2|More, Fewer, the Same — Match things up one to one to see which group has more, fewer, or the same.
mth|1|l1-8|k-2|Writing Numbers and Number Words — Every number has a numeral and a word. 7 is seven. 12 is twelve.
mth|1|l2-1|k-2|A Bundle of Ten — Ten ones make one ten. We can count a bundle of ten as one group.
mth|1|l2-2|k-2|Teen Numbers — A teen number is one ten and some more ones. 14 is ten and four.
mth|1|l2-3|k-2|The Ten Frame — A ten frame has ten boxes. A full frame is one ten. Extra dots are the ones.
mth|1|l2-4|k-2|Two-Digit Numbers — In a two-digit number, the first digit counts tens. The second digit counts ones.
mth|1|l2-5|k-2|Base-Ten Blocks — A long rod is one ten. A small cube is one one. Rods and cubes show any number.
mth|1|l2-6|k-2|Comparing with Symbols — Compare tens first. If tens are the same, compare ones. Use >, < or =.
mth|1|l2-7|k-2|Ten More and Ten Less — Ten more changes the tens digit up by one. Ten less changes it down by one.
mth|1|l2-8|k-2|The Hundred Chart — On a hundred chart, each step right adds one. Each step down adds ten.
mth|10|l20-1|3-5|Area Is Counting Squares — Area is the number of unit squares that cover a flat shape with no gaps and no overlaps.
mth|10|l20-2|3-5|Multiply Rows and Columns — The area of a rectangle equals its length times its width, because the rows repeat the same count.
mth|10|l20-3|3-5|Choosing a Square Unit — Pick a square unit that fits the size of the thing: small squares for small things, big squares for big spaces.
mth|10|l20-4|3-5|Perimeter Is Adding Sides — Perimeter is the total distance around a shape, found by adding the lengths of all its sides.
mth|10|l20-5|3-5|A Shortcut for Rectangles — For a rectangle, add length and width, then double: perimeter = 2 × (length + width).
mth|10|l20-6|3-5|Same Area, Different Fence — Two rectangles can have the same area but different perimeters, so you must find each one separately.
mth|10|l20-7|3-5|Split It Into Rectangles — To find the area of an L-shape or T-shape, cut it into rectangles, find each area, and add.
mth|10|l20-8|3-5|Find the Missing Side — If you know the area or perimeter and one side, work backward with division or subtraction to find the other side.
mth|10|l20-9|3-5|Volume Is Counting Cubes — Volume is the number of unit cubes that pack a solid shape, counted layer by layer.
mth|10|l20-10|3-5|The Volume Formula — For a box, volume = length × width × height, which is the same as the area of the floor times the height.
mth|10|l21-1|3-5|Inches, Feet and Yards — To change a bigger unit into a smaller one, multiply; 1 foot is 12 inches and 1 yard is 3 feet.
mth|10|l21-2|3-5|Meters, Grams and Kilograms — Metric units change by 100 or 1,000: 1 meter is 100 centimeters, 1 kilometer is 1,000 meters, 1 kilogram is 1,000 grams.
mth|10|l21-3|3-5|Cups, Quarts and Liters — Liquid volume has its own units: 1 gallon is 4 quarts, 1 quart is 2 pints, 1 pint is 2 cups, and 1 liter is 1,000 milliliters.
mth|10|l21-4|3-5|Minutes, Hours and Days — Time converts like other units: 1 hour is 60 minutes, 1 day is 24 hours, 1 week is 7 days.
mth|10|l21-5|3-5|How Much Time Passed — To find elapsed time, jump from the start time to the next easy mark, then count the hours and minutes to the end.
mth|10|l21-6|3-5|Bar Graphs With a Scale — On a bar graph, read the scale first, because each line may stand for more than 1.
mth|10|l21-7|3-5|Picture Graphs and Keys — In a picture graph, the key tells how many each picture stands for, and half a picture stands for half that amount.
mth|10|l21-8|3-5|Line Plots With Fractions — A line plot stacks an X above each measurement on a number line, so tall stacks show the most common values.
mth|10|l21-9|3-5|Build Your Own Line Plot — To make a line plot, measure, choose a number line that fits the data, and place one X for each measurement.
mth|10|l21-10|3-5|Solving Problems With Data — A graph or table gives the numbers; you still choose the operation and check that the answer makes sense.
mth|11|l22-1|3-5|Points, Lines, Rays and Segments — Geometry starts with points, and lines, rays and segments are the straight paths that connect them.
mth|11|l22-2|3-5|What an Angle Is — An angle is two rays that share an endpoint, and its size is how far open they are, not how long they are.
mth|11|l22-3|3-5|Measuring With a Protractor — Line up the vertex and one side, then read the scale that starts at 0 on that side.
mth|11|l22-4|3-5|Right, Acute and Obtuse — Compare an angle to a square corner: less than 90° is acute, exactly 90° is right, more than 90° is obtuse.
mth|11|l22-5|3-5|Adding and Splitting Angles — When a ray splits an angle into two parts, the two parts add up to the whole angle.
mth|11|l22-6|3-5|Parallel and Perpendicular — Parallel lines never meet and stay the same distance apart; perpendicular lines meet at right angles.
mth|11|l22-7|3-5|Sorting Triangles — Sort a triangle two ways: by its sides (how many are equal) and by its biggest angle (right, acute or obtuse).
mth|11|l22-8|3-5|Quadrilateral Families — Quadrilaterals are sorted by asking three questions: which sides are parallel, which sides are equal, and which angles are right angles.
mth|11|l22-9|3-5|Every Square Is a Rectangle — Shapes form a hierarchy: a shape that meets the rule for a group belongs to that group and to every bigger group above it.
mth|11|l22-10|3-5|Lines of Symmetry — A line of symmetry folds a shape onto itself so the two halves match exactly.
mth|11|l22-11|3-5|Drawing the Other Half — To finish a symmetric shape, copy each corner the same distance from the line on the other side.
mth|11|l23-1|3-5|Ordered Pairs — An ordered pair (x, y) tells you first how far to go right, then how far to go up, starting from (0, 0).
mth|11|l23-2|3-5|Plotting and Naming Points — To name a point, read straight down to the x-axis first, then straight across to the y-axis.
mth|11|l23-3|3-5|Distances Along the Grid — If two points share a coordinate, the distance between them is the difference of the other coordinates.
mth|11|l23-4|3-5|Graphing a Pattern — A pattern becomes a set of points when you pair each step number with its value.
mth|11|l23-5|3-5|Two Patterns Side by Side — When two patterns start at 0 and one adds twice as much, every value in the second is twice the matching value in the first.
mth|11|l23-6|3-5|Rules That Add — An add rule tells you the start and what to add; to find any term, apply the rule step by step or count the jumps.
mth|11|l23-7|3-5|Rules That Multiply — A multiply rule grows faster and faster, because each term is a multiple of the one before.
mth|11|l23-8|3-5|Describing a Pattern's Features — Look beyond the rule: notice whether terms are odd or even, what digit they end in, and why that must happen.
mth|11|l23-9|3-5|Maps and Coordinates — Real maps use coordinate ideas: a starting point, a direction across, a direction up, and distances you find by subtracting.
mth|11|l23-10|3-5|Shapes on the Grid — Plot the vertices, connect them, and use coordinates to find side lengths, missing corners and the perimeter.
mth|12|l24-1|6-8|What a Ratio Compares — A ratio compares two amounts by telling how many of one there are for each amount of the other.
mth|12|l24-2|6-8|Equivalent Ratios — Two ratios are equivalent when you can multiply or divide both parts of one by the same number to get the other.
mth|12|l24-3|6-8|Tape Diagrams for Ratios — A tape diagram draws each part of a ratio as equal boxes, so you can find the value of one box and then every amount.
mth|12|l24-4|6-8|Ratio Tables — A ratio table lists equivalent ratios in rows, so you can scale a ratio up or down to find a missing amount.
mth|12|l24-5|6-8|Double Number Lines — A double number line lines up two amounts that grow together, so equal jumps on one line match equal jumps on the other.
mth|12|l24-6|6-8|Finding a Unit Rate — A unit rate tells how much of one amount goes with exactly 1 of the other, and you find it by dividing.
mth|12|l24-7|6-8|Comparing Rates: the Better Deal — To compare two deals, find each one's unit rate in the same unit and then compare the numbers.
mth|12|l24-8|6-8|Using a Unit Rate to Predict — Once you know a unit rate, multiply it by any amount to predict the matching amount, or divide to go backward.
mth|12|l24-9|6-8|Converting Units With Rates — A unit conversion is a rate like 12 inches per 1 foot, so you multiply or divide by it just like any other rate.
mth|12|l24-10|6-8|Recipes, Maps and Speeds — Recipes, map scales and speeds are all ratios, so the same tools of scaling, tables and unit rates solve all of them.
mth|12|l25-1|6-8|Spotting a Proportional Table — A relationship is proportional when every pair in its table has the same ratio, so dividing y by x always gives the same number.
mth|12|l25-2|6-8|The Constant of Proportionality — In a proportional relationship y = kx, the constant k is the unit rate, and it tells you what y is when x is 1.
mth|12|l25-3|6-8|Graphs of Proportional Relationships — A proportional relationship graphs as a straight line through the origin, and its steepness is the constant k.
mth|12|l25-4|6-8|Percent Means per Hundred — A percent is a ratio with 100 as the second number, so 45% means 45 out of every 100.
mth|12|l25-5|6-8|Percent of a Number — To find a percent of a number, change the percent to a decimal or fraction and multiply.
mth|12|l25-6|6-8|Percent Increase and Decrease — Percent change compares the amount of change to the original amount, never to the new amount.
mth|12|l25-7|6-8|Tax, Tip and Discount — Tax and tip are percents added to a price, and a discount is a percent taken away, each figured from the price it applies to.
mth|12|l25-8|6-8|Simple Interest and Percent Error — Simple interest is a percent of the starting amount paid for each year, and percent error compares a mistake to the true value.
mth|12|l25-9|6-8|Scale Drawings — A scale drawing keeps every length in the same ratio to the real object, so one scale factor converts any drawn length to real life.
mth|12|l25-10|6-8|Percents in a Row — To apply two percents one after another, multiply by both multipliers in turn; the percents do not simply add.
mth|13|l26-1|6-8|Where Negative Numbers Live — A negative number tells how far below a chosen zero point something is, like a temperature, an elevation or a bank balance.
mth|13|l26-2|6-8|The Number Line in Both Directions — On a horizontal number line, positive numbers sit to the right of zero and negative numbers sit to the left, one unit apart.
mth|13|l26-3|6-8|Opposites — Two numbers are opposites when they sit the same distance from zero on opposite sides, like 7 and −7.
mth|13|l26-4|6-8|Absolute Value — The absolute value of a number is its distance from zero, so it is never negative: |−6| = 6 and |6| = 6.
mth|13|l26-5|6-8|Comparing Integers — Of two numbers on a number line, the one farther to the right is greater, so any positive beats any negative and −2 is greater than −9.
mth|13|l26-6|6-8|Ordering Rational Numbers — To order fractions, decimals and integers together, write them in one form, place them on the number line, and read from left to right.
mth|13|l26-7|6-8|Fractions, Decimals and Repeating Digits — Every rational number has a decimal form that either ends or repeats, and you find it by dividing the top of the fraction by the bottom.
mth|13|l26-8|6-8|Four Quadrants — Two number lines crossing at zero make a coordinate plane, and their signs split it into four quadrants.
mth|13|l26-9|6-8|Plotting Points — To plot (x, y), start at the origin, move x units left or right, then y units up or down, and mark the spot.
mth|13|l26-10|6-8|Distances on the Plane — Two points on the same horizontal or vertical line are as far apart as the difference of their unequal coordinates, found with absolute values.
mth|13|l26-11|6-8|Reflections Across the Axes — Changing the sign of one coordinate reflects a point across an axis: (x, y) to (−x, y) flips it over the y-axis, and (x, y) to (x, −y) flips it over the x-axis.
mth|13|l27-1|6-8|Adding With Chips — Adding integers with the same sign adds the sizes and keeps the sign; a positive chip and a negative chip cancel to zero.
mth|13|l27-2|6-8|Adding on the Number Line — To add on a number line, start at the first number and move right for a positive addend or left for a negative one.
mth|13|l27-3|6-8|Subtracting by Adding the Opposite — Subtracting a number is the same as adding its opposite: a − b = a + (−b), so 3 − (−5) = 3 + 5 = 8.
mth|13|l27-4|6-8|Change and Distance — The change from one value to another is end minus start, and the distance between two numbers is the absolute value of their difference.
mth|13|l27-5|6-8|Multiplying Signed Numbers — Multiply the absolute values, then set the sign: same signs give a positive product and different signs give a negative product.
mth|13|l27-6|6-8|Dividing Signed Numbers — Division follows the same sign rules as multiplication: same signs give a positive quotient, different signs give a negative one.
mth|13|l27-7|6-8|Properties That Still Hold — The commutative, associative and distributive properties work for negative numbers exactly as for positive ones, and they can make hard problems easy.
mth|13|l27-8|6-8|Adding and Subtracting Signed Fractions — Signed fractions and decimals follow the same sign rules as integers; get a common denominator or line up the decimal points, then apply the rules.
mth|13|l27-9|6-8|Multiplying and Dividing Signed Fractions — Multiply or divide the fractions or decimals as usual, then set the sign with the same rule as integers: same signs positive, different signs negative.
mth|13|l27-10|6-8|Multi-Step Problems — Break a signed-number problem into steps, write each step with signs, keep a running total, and check that the final sign makes sense.
mth|13|l27-11|6-8|Order of Operations With Signs — Follow the usual order, parentheses, then multiplication and division, then addition and subtraction, and treat each negative sign as part of its number.
mth|13|l28-1|6-8|Powers and Repeated Multiplication — An exponent counts how many times the base is used as a factor: 2^5 = 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 = 32.
mth|13|l28-2|6-8|Multiplying and Dividing Powers — With the same base, multiply powers by adding exponents and divide by subtracting them: 2^3 × 2^4 = 2^7 and 2^7 ÷ 2^4 = 2^3.
mth|13|l28-3|6-8|A Power of a Power — Raising a power to another power multiplies the exponents: (2^3)^2 = 2^6, and a power of a product applies to every factor: (3x)^2 = 9x^2.
mth|13|l28-4|6-8|Zero and Negative Exponents — Any nonzero base to the zero power is 1, and a negative exponent means a reciprocal: 2^−3 = 1/2^3 = 1/8.
mth|13|l28-5|6-8|Square Roots and Perfect Squares — The square root of a number is the number that, multiplied by itself, gives it: √49 = 7 because 7 × 7 = 49.
mth|13|l28-6|6-8|Cube Roots — The cube root of a number is the number used three times as a factor to make it: the cube root of 64 is 4 because 4 × 4 × 4 = 64.
mth|13|l28-7|6-8|Irrational Numbers and Where √2 Sits — A number that cannot be written as a fraction of integers, like √2 or π, is irrational, but it still has an exact spot on the number line.
mth|13|l28-8|6-8|Writing Very Large Numbers — Scientific notation writes a number as a decimal between 1 and 10 times a power of ten: 4,500,000 = 4.5 × 10^6.
mth|13|l28-9|6-8|Writing Very Small Numbers — A negative exponent on the 10 moves the decimal point left, so 0.00035 = 3.5 × 10^−4.
mth|13|l28-10|6-8|Multiplying and Dividing in Scientific Notation — Multiply or divide the coefficients, use the exponent rules on the powers of ten, then fix the answer so the coefficient is between 1 and 10.
mth|13|l28-11|6-8|Adding, Subtracting and Comparing — To add or subtract in scientific notation, make the exponents match first; to compare, look at the exponent before the coefficient.
mth|14|l29-1|6-8|What a Variable Is — A variable is a letter that stands for a number, either one unknown number or a quantity that changes.
mth|14|l29-2|6-8|Words to Expressions — Key phrases tell you the operation, and the order of the words tells you the order of the terms.
mth|14|l29-3|6-8|Expressions from Real Situations — A fixed amount becomes the constant, and an amount paid per item or per mile becomes the coefficient of the variable.
mth|14|l29-4|6-8|Order of Operations — Do grouping symbols first, then exponents, then multiplication and division left to right, then addition and subtraction left to right.
mth|14|l29-5|6-8|Substitute and Evaluate — To evaluate an expression, replace each variable with its number, then follow the order of operations.
mth|14|l29-6|6-8|Tables from Expressions — A table of values shows how an expression changes as its variable changes, one row for each input.
mth|14|l29-7|6-8|The Distributive Property — Multiplying a sum means multiplying each part: a(b + c) = ab + ac.
mth|14|l29-8|6-8|Combining Like Terms — Terms with exactly the same variable part can be added or subtracted by combining their coefficients.
mth|14|l29-9|6-8|Factoring an Expression — Factoring runs the distributive property backward: pull out the greatest common factor and write the expression as a product.
mth|14|l29-10|6-8|Perimeter and Area Expressions — Geometry formulas are expressions; write them with variables, then simplify or evaluate them.
mth|14|l29-11|6-8|Patterns and the nth Term — When a pattern grows by the same amount each step, an expression in n gives any term without counting up to it.
mth|14|l30-1|6-8|What an Equation Says — An equation states that two expressions have the same value, and a solution is a value of the variable that makes it true.
mth|14|l30-2|6-8|Adding and Subtracting to Solve — Undo addition with subtraction and subtraction with addition, doing the same thing to both sides.
mth|14|l30-3|6-8|Multiplying and Dividing to Solve — Undo multiplication with division and division with multiplication, on both sides of the equation.
mth|14|l30-4|6-8|Two-Step Equations — Undo addition or subtraction first, then undo multiplication or division, keeping both sides equal at each step.
mth|14|l30-5|6-8|Variables on Both Sides — Move all variable terms to one side and all constants to the other, then solve the two-step equation that remains.
mth|14|l30-6|6-8|Equations with Parentheses — Use the distributive property to clear the parentheses, combine like terms, then solve as before.
mth|14|l30-7|6-8|Reading Inequalities — An inequality compares two amounts, has many solutions, and is graphed as a ray on the number line.
mth|14|l30-8|6-8|Solving Inequalities — Solve an inequality like an equation, but reverse the symbol when you multiply or divide both sides by a negative number.
mth|14|l30-9|6-8|Inequalities in Context — Translate the situation into an inequality, solve it, and then state the answer in words that fit the situation.
mth|14|l30-10|6-8|Writing Equations from Stories — Name the unknown, write what the story says with that letter, solve, and answer in the story's words.
mth|14|l30-11|6-8|Checking and Making Sense — A solution is not finished until it is substituted back into the original equation and read against the situation.
mth|15|l31-1|6-8|Rise Over Run — Slope is the rise divided by the run, and a bigger slope means a steeper climb.
mth|15|l31-2|6-8|Slope Between Two Points — To find the slope between two points, subtract the y-values for the rise and the x-values for the run, in the same order.
mth|15|l31-3|6-8|Same Slope Everywhere — Every slope triangle on a straight line is similar to every other one, so the slope is the same between any two points.
mth|15|l31-4|6-8|Slope as a Rate of Change — In a real situation, the slope is the rate of change: how much y changes for each 1 unit of x.
mth|15|l31-5|6-8|The Form y = mx + b — In y = mx + b, m is the slope and b is the y-intercept, the value of y when x is 0.
mth|15|l31-6|6-8|Graphing From Slope and Intercept — To graph y = mx + b, plot the y-intercept, then use the slope as rise over run to find more points.
mth|15|l31-7|6-8|Equation From a Graph — Read the y-intercept where the line crosses the y-axis, find the slope from two clear points, then write y = mx + b.
mth|15|l31-8|6-8|Equation From a Table or Two Points — Find the slope from any two points, then substitute one point into y = mx + b to solve for b.
mth|15|l31-9|6-8|Proportional or Just Linear? — A proportional relationship is a line through the origin, y = kx; a line with a nonzero y-intercept is linear but not proportional.
mth|15|l31-10|6-8|Horizontal and Vertical Lines — A horizontal line has slope 0 and equation y = a number; a vertical line has an undefined slope and equation x = a number.
mth|15|l32-1|6-8|One Output for Each Input — A function is a rule that gives exactly one output for each input.
mth|15|l32-2|6-8|Function or Not? — A rule is not a function if any input has two different outputs; on a graph, a vertical line would hit the graph twice.
mth|15|l32-3|6-8|Four Ways to Show a Rule — The same function can be given in words, as a table of values, as an equation or as a graph, and you can move between them.
mth|15|l32-4|6-8|Linear Functions — A linear function changes by the same amount for each step in x, so its graph is a straight line and its equation is y = mx + b.
mth|15|l32-5|6-8|Nonlinear Functions — A nonlinear function does not change by the same amount each step, so its graph is a curve, not a straight line.
mth|15|l32-6|6-8|Rate of Change and Initial Value — Every linear function has a rate of change, which is its slope, and an initial value, which is its output when the input is 0.
mth|15|l32-7|6-8|Comparing Two Functions — To compare functions given in different ways, find each one's rate of change and initial value, then compare those numbers.
mth|15|l32-8|6-8|Sketching a Graph From a Story — Each part of a story becomes a segment of the graph: rising, falling or flat, steep or gentle.
mth|15|l32-9|6-8|Telling a Story From a Graph — Read a graph segment by segment: steeper means faster, flat means a pause, and falling means the amount is decreasing.
mth|15|l32-10|6-8|Describing a Function in Words — You can describe any function by saying where it is increasing or decreasing, and whether it is linear or nonlinear.
mth|15|l33-1|6-8|What a System Is — A system of equations is two equations that must both be true, and its solution is the one pair (x, y) that works in both.
mth|15|l33-2|6-8|Solving by Graphing — Graph both lines on one grid; the point where they cross is the solution, and substituting checks it.
mth|15|l33-3|6-8|Estimating and Checking — When the crossing is not on a grid corner, estimate it from the graph, then use algebra to find the exact point and check it.
mth|15|l33-4|6-8|Substitution — When one equation gives a variable by itself, substitute that expression into the other equation and solve.
mth|15|l33-5|6-8|Elimination by Adding — When a variable has opposite coefficients in the two equations, add the equations to eliminate it.
mth|15|l33-6|6-8|Elimination With Multiplying — If no coefficients match, multiply one or both equations by a number so that a variable's coefficients become equal or opposite, then eliminate.
mth|15|l33-7|6-8|One, None, or Infinitely Many — Two lines can cross once, never cross, or be the same line, giving one solution, no solution, or infinitely many.
mth|15|l33-8|6-8|Reading the Count From Slopes — Rearrange both equations into y = mx + b and compare: different m means one solution, same m with different b means none, same m and b means infinitely many.
mth|15|l33-9|6-8|Two Phone Plans — When two plans have different fixed costs and different rates, the system's solution is the break-even point where they cost the same.
mth|15|l33-10|6-8|Tickets, Coins and Mixtures — Many word problems give a count and a total; write one equation for each, then solve the system.
mth|16|l34-1|6-8|Parallelograms from Rectangles — The area of a parallelogram is base times height, because you can cut off one end and slide it over to make a rectangle.
mth|16|l34-2|6-8|Triangles Are Half — Every triangle is exactly half of a parallelogram with the same base and height, so its area is 1/2 × base × height.
mth|16|l34-3|6-8|Trapezoids Two Ways — A trapezoid's area is the average of its two parallel sides times the height, and you can prove it by cutting the shape into simpler pieces.
mth|16|l34-4|6-8|Around a Circle — The distance around any circle is π times the distance across it, and π is about 3.14.
mth|16|l34-5|6-8|Inside a Circle — Cut a circle into thin wedges and lay them in a row to see why its area is π times the radius squared.
mth|16|l34-6|6-8|Unfolding a Box — A net is a solid shape unfolded flat, and it shows every face you would need to cover or paint.
mth|16|l34-7|6-8|Surface Area of Prisms — Surface area is the total area of every face, so find each face on the net and add them all up.
mth|16|l34-8|6-8|Surface Area of Pyramids — A pyramid's surface area is its base plus the triangles on its sides, and each triangle uses the slant height.
mth|16|l34-9|6-8|Prisms and Cylinders — The volume of a prism or cylinder is the area of its base times its height, because it is a stack of identical layers.
mth|16|l34-10|6-8|Cones and Spheres — A cone holds one third of the cylinder around it, and a sphere holds two thirds, which gives the formulas 1/3 πr²h and 4/3 πr³.
mth|16|l34-11|6-8|Packaging, Paint and Pools — Read the situation to decide whether it asks for surface area, which covers the outside, or volume, which fills the inside.
mth|16|l35-1|6-8|Adding to 90 or 180 — Two angles are complementary if they add to 90 degrees and supplementary if they add to 180, and each fact lets you find a missing angle by subtracting.
mth|16|l35-2|6-8|When Two Lines Cross — When two straight lines cross, the angles opposite each other are equal and the angles next to each other add to 180.
mth|16|l35-3|6-8|Finding Unknown Angles — Write what the angles must add to as an equation, then solve it to find the unknown.
mth|16|l35-4|6-8|A Line Across Two Parallels — When a line crosses two parallel lines, it makes matching angles: corresponding and alternate interior angles are equal, and same-side interior angles add to 180.
mth|16|l35-5|6-8|Why a Triangle Adds to 180 — The three angles of any triangle add to 180 degrees, because a parallel line through the top corner lines them up along a straight line.
mth|16|l35-6|6-8|The Triangle Inequality — Three lengths make a triangle only if every two of them add to more than the third.
mth|16|l35-7|6-8|One Triangle, Many or None — Some sets of measurements build exactly one triangle, some build many different sizes, and some build none at all.
mth|16|l35-8|6-8|Slicing Solids — A cross section is the flat shape you see when you slice a solid, and it depends on the direction of the cut.
mth|16|l35-9|6-8|Reading a Scale — A scale tells how a length on a drawing matches a real length, and multiplying or dividing by that ratio converts between them.
mth|16|l35-10|6-8|Scale Factor and Area — When every length is multiplied by a scale factor, the area is multiplied by the scale factor squared.
mth|16|l36-1|6-8|Sliding: Translations — A translation slides every point of a shape the same distance in the same direction, so the image is an exact copy in a new place.
mth|16|l36-2|6-8|Flipping: Reflections — A reflection flips a shape across a line, so the image is a mirror copy the same distance from the line on the other side.
mth|16|l36-3|6-8|Turning: Rotations — A rotation turns a shape around a fixed center point by a set angle, and every point stays the same distance from that center.
mth|16|l36-4|6-8|Growing: Dilations — A dilation multiplies every distance from a center by a scale factor, changing the size of a shape but not its shape.
mth|16|l36-5|6-8|Congruent or Similar — Two shapes are congruent if slides, flips and turns can lay one exactly on the other, and similar if a dilation is also allowed.
mth|16|l36-6|6-8|Squares on a Right Triangle — In a right triangle with legs a and b and hypotenuse c, a² + b² = c², and you can prove it by fitting four copies of the triangle inside a square.
mth|16|l36-7|6-8|Finding a Missing Side — To find a leg, subtract the other leg's square from the hypotenuse's square; to find the hypotenuse, add the squares of the legs.
mth|16|l36-8|6-8|Is It a Right Triangle? — The converse of the Pythagorean theorem says that if the three sides satisfy a² + b² = c², the triangle has a right angle.
mth|16|l36-9|6-8|Distance on a Grid — To find the distance between two points on a grid, draw the right triangle between them and use the Pythagorean theorem on its legs.
mth|16|l36-10|6-8|Ladders, Screens and Diagonals — Whenever a real situation contains a right angle, the Pythagorean theorem can find a length that is hard to measure directly.
mth|17|l37-1|6-8|Statistical Questions — A statistical question expects answers that vary, and you answer it by collecting and describing data from a group.
mth|17|l37-2|6-8|Dot Plots — A dot plot stacks one dot per data value above a number line, so you can see every value and where they pile up.
mth|17|l37-3|6-8|Histograms — A histogram groups data into equal-width intervals and shows how many values fall in each one, with bars that touch.
mth|17|l37-4|6-8|The Mean — The mean is the fair-share value: add all the values and divide by how many there are.
mth|17|l37-5|6-8|The Median — The median is the middle value of the ordered data; half the values are at or below it and half at or above.
mth|17|l37-6|6-8|Mean or Median? — Use the mean when values are fairly even; use the median when a few extreme values would drag the mean away from the typical value.
mth|17|l37-7|6-8|Range and Interquartile Range — Range measures the full spread; interquartile range measures the spread of the middle half and is not thrown off by outliers.
mth|17|l37-8|6-8|Box Plots — A box plot draws the five-number summary, so each of its four pieces holds about a quarter of the data.
mth|17|l37-9|6-8|Mean Absolute Deviation — Mean absolute deviation (MAD) is the average distance of the values from the mean; a bigger MAD means more spread.
mth|17|l37-10|6-8|The Shape of a Distribution — Describe a distribution by its shape, symmetric or skewed, its peaks, clusters, gaps and outliers, before you pick a measure of center.
mth|17|l37-11|6-8|Comparing Two Groups — To compare two groups, compare their centers, then judge the difference against the spread: a gap of about 2 MADs or more matters; a gap much smaller than the MAD may not.
mth|17|l38-1|6-8|A Number From 0 to 1 — Probability is a number from 0 to 1 that tells how likely an event is: 0 means impossible, 1 means certain, and 1/2 means as likely as not.
mth|17|l38-2|6-8|Theoretical Probability — When all outcomes are equally likely, probability = number of favorable outcomes ÷ total number of outcomes.
mth|17|l38-3|6-8|Experimental Probability — Experimental probability = times the event happened ÷ number of trials; with more trials it usually settles near the theoretical probability.
mth|17|l38-4|6-8|Listing Every Outcome — For a compound event, list the sample space in an organized way; then probability is favorable outcomes over total outcomes.
mth|17|l38-5|6-8|Tree Diagrams — A tree diagram branches once for each choice; each path from start to tip is one outcome, and the number of tips is the product of the branch counts.
mth|17|l38-6|6-8|Probability of Compound Events — Find the probability of a compound event by counting favorable outcomes in the sample space, or by multiplying the probabilities of independent parts.
mth|17|l38-7|6-8|Simulating Chance — A simulation uses a random tool matched to the real probabilities, run many times, to estimate a probability that is hard to compute.
mth|17|l38-8|6-8|Random Samples — A random sample gives every member of the population an equal chance of being chosen, so the sample is likely to look like the whole.
mth|17|l38-9|6-8|From Sample to Population — Find the proportion in a random sample, then multiply by the population size to estimate the count in the whole population.
mth|17|l38-10|6-8|Comparing Two Populations — Compare random samples from two populations the way you compare two groups: look at the difference in centers relative to the spread.
mth|17|l39-1|6-8|Two Measurements Per Point — A scatter plot shows two measurements for each person or thing as one point, with one variable on each axis.
mth|17|l39-2|6-8|Reading a Scatter Plot — Read a scatter plot by asking: as one variable increases, what happens to the other, and how tightly do the points follow that trend?
mth|17|l39-3|6-8|Positive and Negative Association — A positive association means both variables rise together; a negative association means one falls as the other rises; a shapeless cloud shows no association.
mth|17|l39-4|6-8|Clusters, Outliers and Curves — Besides direction, look for clusters, outliers and whether the pattern is a line or a curve.
mth|17|l39-5|6-8|A Line by Eye — When points show a linear pattern, draw a straight line through the middle of the cloud with about as many points above as below, then use it to predict.
mth|17|l39-6|6-8|Slope of a Fitted Line — The slope of a fitted line tells how much the vertical variable changes, on average, for each 1-unit increase in the horizontal variable.
mth|17|l39-7|6-8|Intercept and Predictions — The y-intercept is the line's value when the horizontal variable is 0; it means something only if 0 is inside or near the data.
mth|17|l39-8|6-8|Two-Way Tables — A two-way table counts people by two categories at once; row and column totals let you check the count and answer how-many questions.
mth|17|l39-9|6-8|Relative Frequencies — Divide a cell by its row total (or column total) to get a relative frequency, which lets you compare groups of different sizes.
mth|17|l39-10|6-8|Association in a Table — Two categories are associated when the row percents differ noticeably between rows; similar percents mean little or no association.
mth|18|l40-1|9-10|True, False or Open — An equation is a statement that can be true or false, and a solution is a value that makes it true.
mth|18|l40-2|9-10|Keeping the Balance — Whatever you do to one side of an equation, do to the other side, and the statement stays true.
mth|18|l40-3|9-10|Multi-Step Equations with Reasons — Simplify each side first, then collect the variable on one side, and name the reason for every step.
mth|18|l40-4|9-10|Rearranging a Formula — A formula with several letters is solved the same way as any equation: undo operations until the letter you want stands alone.
mth|18|l40-5|9-10|Temperature and Other Conversions — A conversion formula can be run in either direction once you solve it for the other variable.
mth|18|l40-6|9-10|Absolute Value Equations — An absolute value equation usually has two solutions, one on each side of the center point.
mth|18|l40-7|9-10|Inequalities Show a Range — An inequality is solved like an equation, but its solution is a whole range of numbers, shown on a number line.
mth|18|l40-8|9-10|When the Sign Flips — Multiplying or dividing both sides of an inequality by a negative number reverses the inequality sign.
mth|18|l40-9|9-10|Compound Inequalities — A compound inequality joins two conditions with 'and' or 'or', and you solve every part at once.
mth|18|l40-10|9-10|Writing Equations from Situations — To model a situation, define a variable, translate the words into an equation, solve it, and say what the number means.
mth|18|l40-11|9-10|Limits Become Inequalities — Words like 'at most', 'at least' and 'no more than' signal an inequality, and the answer is a range that may need rounding.
mth|18|l40-12|9-10|Units and Reasonableness — Carry the units through your work and compare the answer to a rough estimate before you trust it.
mth|18|l41-1|9-10|Inputs and Outputs — A function is a rule that gives exactly one output for each input.
mth|18|l41-2|9-10|Reading f(3) — f(3) means the output of rule f when the input is 3; it is a question and its answer, not a product.
mth|18|l41-3|9-10|Domain and Range — The domain is every input a function allows, and the range is every output it can produce.
mth|18|l41-4|9-10|Slope as a Rate of Change — Slope is the change in output divided by the change in input, and in a story it is a rate.
mth|18|l41-5|9-10|Slope-Intercept Form — In y = mx + b, m is the rate of change and b is the starting value where the line crosses the y-axis.
mth|18|l41-6|9-10|Arithmetic Sequences as Functions — An arithmetic sequence is a linear function whose inputs are the counting numbers 1, 2, 3, and so on.
mth|18|l41-7|9-10|Key Features of a Graph — Intercepts, direction and zeros tell the story of a graph in a few numbers.
mth|18|l41-8|9-10|Building a Model from Data — When a table's outputs change by the same amount each step, a linear model fits, and you can use it to predict.
mth|18|l41-9|9-10|Piecewise and Step Functions — A piecewise function uses different rules on different parts of its domain; a step function jumps between constant values.
mth|18|l41-10|9-10|Shifting and Stretching Lines — Adding to the output slides a graph up or down, adding to the input slides it sideways, and multiplying the output changes its steepness.
mth|18|l41-11|9-10|Comparing Two Models — To compare two linear models, find where their outputs are equal, then see which is better on each side of that point.
mth|18|l42-1|9-10|What a System Is — A system of equations is two or more equations about the same variables, and its solution makes every equation true at once.
mth|18|l42-2|9-10|Solving by Graphing — Graph both equations; the point where the lines cross is the solution, and reading it from the graph gives an estimate.
mth|18|l42-3|9-10|Substitution — If one equation tells you what a variable equals, substitute that expression into the other equation and solve.
mth|18|l42-4|9-10|Elimination by Adding — Adding two equations together is legal because you add equal amounts to both sides, and it can make one variable disappear.
mth|18|l42-5|9-10|Elimination with Multiplying — Multiply one or both equations by a number first so that a pair of terms becomes opposites, then add.
mth|18|l42-6|9-10|No Solution or Infinitely Many — Parallel lines give a system no solution, and two forms of the same line give infinitely many.
mth|18|l42-7|9-10|Graphing an Inequality — A linear inequality shades a half of the plane; the line is the boundary, and a test point tells you which side.
mth|18|l42-8|9-10|Systems of Inequalities and Feasible Regions — Graph each inequality and keep only the overlap; that overlap is the feasible region where every constraint holds.
mth|18|l42-9|9-10|Mixture Problems — In a mixture problem, one equation counts the total amount and another counts the total of the ingredient.
mth|18|l42-10|9-10|Business Problems and Break-Even — A business breaks even where cost equals revenue, and ticket problems split a total count and a total value.
mth|18|l42-11|9-10|The Best Mix Within Limits — To find the best plan under several constraints, evaluate the goal at every vertex of the feasible region.
mth|19|l43-1|9-10|Multiplying and Dividing Powers — When powers share a base, multiply them by adding the exponents and divide them by subtracting the exponents.
mth|19|l43-2|9-10|Powers of Powers and the Zero Exponent — Raising a power to a power multiplies the exponents, and any nonzero base raised to the zero power equals 1.
mth|19|l43-3|9-10|Negative Exponents — A negative exponent means divide: b^(−n) equals 1/b^n, the reciprocal of the positive power.
mth|19|l43-4|9-10|Rational Exponents and Roots — An exponent of 1/n means the nth root, so 9^(1/2) = √9 = 3 and 8^(1/3) equals the cube root of 8, which is 2.
mth|19|l43-5|9-10|Doubling and Growth Factors — Exponential growth multiplies by the same factor each step, so the value after x steps is start × factor^x.
mth|19|l43-6|9-10|Percent Growth and Compound Interest — A rate of r per period gives the growth factor 1 + r, so 5% growth means multiply by 1.05 each period.
mth|19|l43-7|9-10|Decay and Half-Life — Exponential decay multiplies by a factor between 0 and 1 each step, such as 0.5 for each half-life or 0.85 for a 15% loss.
mth|19|l43-8|9-10|Geometric Sequences — A geometric sequence multiplies by the same common ratio each term, so the nth term is a(1) × r^(n − 1).
mth|19|l43-9|9-10|Building a Model From a Table — To build an exponential model from a table or story, find the starting value at x = 0 and the constant ratio between equal steps.
mth|19|l43-10|9-10|Linear or Exponential? — Linear growth adds the same amount each step and exponential growth multiplies, so exponential growth eventually outruns any linear growth.
mth|19|l44-1|9-10|Terms, Degree and Standard Form — A polynomial is a sum of terms, each a number times whole-number powers of a variable, and its degree is its highest exponent.
mth|19|l44-2|9-10|Adding and Subtracting Polynomials — Add polynomials by combining like terms; subtract by distributing the minus sign to every term of the second polynomial first.
mth|19|l44-3|9-10|Multiplying With the Area Model — To multiply polynomials, multiply every term of one by every term of the other; an area model organizes the products as boxes.
mth|19|l44-4|9-10|Special Products — Squaring a binomial gives a^2 + 2ab + b^2, and a sum times a difference gives a^2 − b^2; these identities hold for every value of the variable.
mth|19|l44-5|9-10|Factoring Out the Greatest Common Factor — Factoring reverses multiplying; always begin by pulling out the greatest common factor of all the terms.
mth|19|l44-6|9-10|Factoring x^2 + bx + c — To factor x^2 + bx + c, find two numbers that multiply to c and add to b; they are the constants in (x + p)(x + q).
mth|19|l44-7|9-10|Factoring ax^2 + bx + c — When the leading coefficient is not 1, find two numbers that multiply to a × c and add to b, then split the middle term and group.
mth|19|l44-8|9-10|Special Factoring Patterns — Recognize a^2 − b^2 as (a + b)(a − b) and a^2 ± 2ab + b^2 as (a ± b)^2 to factor in a single step.
mth|19|l44-9|9-10|Factoring by Grouping — For four-term polynomials, factor a GCF from each pair of terms; if the pairs share a binomial, factor it out.
mth|19|l44-10|9-10|The Zero-Product Property — If a product equals zero, at least one factor is zero, so a factored equation splits into simple equations.
mth|19|l45-1|9-10|Parabolas, Vertices and Symmetry — The graph of a quadratic function is a parabola, symmetric about a vertical axis through its vertex, its highest or lowest point.
mth|19|l45-2|9-10|Standard Form and the Vertex Formula — In y = ax^2 + bx + c the axis of symmetry is x = −b/(2a); substitute that x back in to find the vertex's y-value.
mth|19|l45-3|9-10|Vertex Form — Vertex form y = a(x − h)^2 + k shows the vertex (h, k) directly and describes the parabola as y = x^2 shifted and stretched.
mth|19|l45-4|9-10|Factored Form and Zeros — Factored form y = a(x − p)(x − q) shows the zeros p and q, and the vertex lies halfway between them.
mth|19|l45-5|9-10|Solving With Square Roots — When a quadratic has no x term, isolate the squared expression and take the square root of both sides, keeping both the positive and the negative root.
mth|19|l45-6|9-10|Completing the Square — Add (b/2)^2 to both sides to turn x^2 + bx into a perfect square, then solve with square roots; the same move rewrites any quadratic in vertex form.
mth|19|l45-7|9-10|The Quadratic Formula — For any equation ax^2 + bx + c = 0, x = (−b ± √(b^2 − 4ac)) / (2a); it comes from completing the square on the general equation.
mth|19|l45-8|9-10|The Discriminant — The discriminant b^2 − 4ac tells how many real solutions a quadratic has: two if positive, one if zero, none if negative.
mth|19|l45-9|9-10|Projectile Models — A thrown object's height is h = −16t^2 + vt + h0 in feet and seconds; the vertex gives the peak and a zero gives the landing time.
mth|19|l45-10|9-10|Area and Revenue Problems — Many area and number problems set up a quadratic equation; write it from the situation, solve, then reject any solution that makes no sense.
mth|19|l45-11|9-10|Linear, Exponential or Quadratic? — Constant first differences mean linear, constant ratios mean exponential, and constant second differences mean quadratic.
mth|2|l3-1|k-2|Adding Puts Groups Together — Adding puts two groups together to find how many in all.
mth|2|l3-2|k-2|Adding More to a Group — When more come, say the first number and count on.
mth|2|l3-3|k-2|Taking Away — Subtracting takes some away and finds how many are left.
mth|2|l3-4|k-2|How Many More? — Subtract to compare two groups and find how many more.
mth|2|l3-5|k-2|Parts and the Whole — A number bond shows a whole and its two parts.
mth|2|l3-6|k-2|What Equals Means — The equal sign means both sides are the same amount.
mth|2|l3-7|k-2|Find the Missing Number — A missing number can be in any spot; use parts and wholes to find it.
mth|2|l3-8|k-2|Doubles and Making Ten — Doubles and pairs that make ten are facts worth knowing by heart.
mth|2|l4-1|k-2|Count On to Add — To add a small number, start with the big number and count on.
mth|2|l4-2|k-2|Count Back to Subtract — To take away a small number, start at the whole and count back.
mth|2|l4-3|k-2|Near Doubles — If you know a double, you can find the fact right next to it.
mth|2|l4-4|k-2|Make Ten to Add — Break one number so the other becomes 10, then add the rest.
mth|2|l4-5|k-2|Think Addition to Subtract — To subtract, ask what you add to the part to reach the whole.
mth|2|l4-6|k-2|Fact Families — Three numbers make a family of two adding facts and two subtracting facts.
mth|2|l4-7|k-2|Two-Step Story Problems — Solve one step at a time, then check by a second way.
mth|20|l46-1|9-10|Points, Lines and Planes — Geometry starts with three undefined terms, and every other word is defined from them.
mth|20|l46-2|9-10|Postulates and Theorems — A postulate is accepted without proof; a theorem must be proved from postulates, definitions and earlier theorems.
mth|20|l46-3|9-10|Conjectures and Counterexamples — Inductive reasoning spots a pattern and makes a conjecture; deductive reasoning proves it; one counterexample disproves it.
mth|20|l46-4|9-10|Conditional Statements — A conditional has a hypothesis and a conclusion, and it is false only when the hypothesis is true and the conclusion is false.
mth|20|l46-5|9-10|Converse, Inverse, Contrapositive — The contrapositive always matches the original statement's truth; the converse and inverse can be different.
mth|20|l46-6|9-10|Biconditionals and Definitions — A biconditional is true when a conditional and its converse are both true, and every good definition is a biconditional.
mth|20|l46-7|9-10|Two-Column Proofs With Segments — A two-column proof lists statements on the left and a reason for each on the right, from the given to what you want to prove.
mth|20|l46-8|9-10|Angle Proofs — Angle proofs rest on two facts: a linear pair adds to 180°, and vertical angles are congruent.
mth|20|l46-9|9-10|Paragraph and Flow Proofs — Paragraph, flow and two-column proofs carry the same statements and reasons in different layouts.
mth|20|l46-10|9-10|Parallel Lines and Transversals — When a transversal crosses parallel lines, corresponding angles are congruent, and that one fact proves the other angle pairs.
mth|20|l46-11|9-10|Compass and Straightedge — A construction uses only a compass and an unmarked straightedge, and every step is backed by a postulate or theorem.
mth|20|l47-1|9-10|Translations — A translation slides every point the same distance in the same direction, and a coordinate rule describes it exactly.
mth|20|l47-2|9-10|Reflections — A reflection flips a figure across a line, and the line is the perpendicular bisector of every segment joining a point to its image.
mth|20|l47-3|9-10|Rotations — A rotation turns a figure around a fixed center through a given angle, and rotations about the origin follow simple coordinate rules.
mth|20|l47-4|9-10|Symmetry — A figure has symmetry when a reflection or a rotation maps it onto itself.
mth|20|l47-5|9-10|Congruence by Rigid Motion — Two figures are congruent when a sequence of rigid motions maps one exactly onto the other, so every matching part is equal.
mth|20|l47-6|9-10|SSS and SAS — Three pairs of congruent sides, or two sides and the included angle, are enough to force two triangles to be congruent.
mth|20|l47-7|9-10|ASA and AAS — Two angles and the included side, or two angles and any side, are enough to prove two triangles congruent.
mth|20|l47-8|9-10|Why SSA Fails — Two sides and a non-included angle can produce two different triangles, so SSA is not a congruence criterion, except for right triangles.
mth|20|l47-9|9-10|Corresponding Parts — Once two triangles are proved congruent, every pair of corresponding parts is congruent, a step called CPCTC.
mth|20|l47-10|9-10|Parallelograms — In a parallelogram, opposite sides and opposite angles are congruent, consecutive angles are supplementary, and the diagonals bisect each other.
mth|20|l47-11|9-10|Rectangles, Rhombuses and Squares — Special parallelograms add rules about their diagonals: a rectangle's are congruent, a rhombus's are perpendicular, and a square's are both.
mth|20|l48-1|9-10|The Angle Sum — The three interior angles of any triangle add to 180°, and a line parallel to one side proves it.
mth|20|l48-2|9-10|Exterior Angles — An exterior angle of a triangle equals the sum of the two remote interior angles.
mth|20|l48-3|9-10|Isosceles and Equilateral — If two sides of a triangle are congruent, the angles opposite them are congruent, and the converse is also true.
mth|20|l48-4|9-10|Perpendicular Bisectors and the Circumcenter — Every point on a segment's perpendicular bisector is equidistant from its endpoints, so the three bisectors of a triangle meet at one point equidistant from all three vertices.
mth|20|l48-5|9-10|Angle Bisectors and the Incenter — Every point on an angle's bisector is equidistant from the angle's two sides, so a triangle's three angle bisectors meet at a point equidistant from all three sides.
mth|20|l48-6|9-10|Medians and the Centroid — The three medians of a triangle meet at the centroid, which sits two thirds of the way from each vertex to the opposite midpoint.
mth|20|l48-7|9-10|Altitudes and Midsegments — An altitude is the perpendicular height from a vertex, and a midsegment is parallel to the third side and half its length.
mth|20|l48-8|9-10|The Triangle Inequality — Any two sides of a triangle must add to more than the third side, or the triangle cannot close.
mth|20|l48-9|9-10|Bigger Angle, Bigger Side — In any triangle, the longest side is opposite the largest angle, and the shortest side is opposite the smallest angle.
mth|20|l48-10|9-10|Coordinate Proofs — Placing a figure on a coordinate grid turns geometry statements into calculations with distance, midpoint and slope.
mth|20|l48-11|9-10|Centers on the Grid — On a coordinate grid, the centroid is the average of the three vertices, and the circumcenter of a right triangle is the midpoint of the hypotenuse.
mth|21|l49-1|9-10|Stretching From a Center — A dilation multiplies every distance from a center point by the same scale factor, so shapes keep their angles but change size.
mth|21|l49-2|9-10|What Similar Means — Two figures are similar when one is a dilation (plus slides, turns or flips) of the other: equal angles and proportional sides.
mth|21|l49-3|9-10|Three Shortcuts for Triangles — You can prove two triangles similar with AA, SAS or SSS without checking all six parts.
mth|21|l49-4|9-10|A Line Parallel to One Side — A line parallel to one side of a triangle cuts the other two sides into proportional pieces.
mth|21|l49-5|9-10|The Pythagorean Theorem, Proved — Dropping a perpendicular from the right angle to the hypotenuse makes three similar triangles, and their proportions prove a² + b² = c².
mth|21|l49-6|9-10|Two Triangles Worth Memorizing — In a 45-45-90 triangle the hypotenuse is leg × √2; in a 30-60-90 triangle the sides are x, x√3 and 2x.
mth|21|l49-7|9-10|Sine, Cosine and Tangent — For an acute angle in a right triangle, sine, cosine and tangent are side ratios that depend only on the angle, because all such triangles are similar.
mth|21|l49-8|9-10|Finding a Missing Side — Choose the ratio that links the side you know, the side you want and the angle, then solve the equation.
mth|21|l49-9|9-10|Finding a Missing Angle — When you know two sides, form the ratio and use the inverse function (sin⁻¹, cos⁻¹ or tan⁻¹) to find the angle.
mth|21|l49-10|9-10|Looking Up and Looking Down — The angle of elevation looks up from horizontal and the angle of depression looks down; between two points they are equal.
mth|21|l49-11|9-10|Shadows, Mirrors and Rivers — Indirect measurement uses a small triangle you can measure and a similar large one you cannot.
mth|21|l50-1|9-10|Central Angles and Arcs — A central angle has its vertex at the center, and the arc it cuts off has the same measure in degrees.
mth|21|l50-2|9-10|Angles on the Edge — An inscribed angle, with its vertex on the circle, measures half the arc it opens onto.
mth|21|l50-3|9-10|Chords, Tangents and Secants — A tangent meets a circle at one point and is perpendicular to the radius there; a perpendicular from the center bisects any chord.
mth|21|l50-4|9-10|Circumference and Area — Circumference is π times the diameter, and cutting a circle into wedges shows why its area is π times the radius squared.
mth|21|l50-5|9-10|Pieces of a Circle — An arc or a sector is a fraction of the circle, θ/360, times the whole circumference or the whole area.
mth|21|l50-6|9-10|A New Way to Measure Angles — One radian is the angle whose arc equals the radius, so a full circle is 2π radians and arc length is simply r × θ.
mth|21|l50-7|9-10|The Equation of a Circle — A circle with center (h, k) and radius r is the set of points satisfying (x − h)² + (y − k)² = r², which is the distance formula in disguise.
mth|21|l50-8|9-10|Finding the Center — Completing the square turns an expanded circle equation back into standard form and reveals the center and radius.
mth|21|l50-9|9-10|Prisms and Cylinders — The volume of a prism or cylinder is the area of its base times its height, because it is a stack of identical layers.
mth|21|l50-10|9-10|Cones and Pyramids — A cone or pyramid holds exactly one third of the cylinder or prism with the same base and height.
mth|21|l50-11|9-10|Spheres and Cavalieri's Principle — Comparing a hemisphere with a cylinder minus a cone, slice by slice, gives the sphere's volume: V = (4/3)πr³.
mth|21|l50-12|9-10|Density and Modeling With Solids — Density is an amount divided by the space it fills, and real objects can be modeled by combining simple solids.
mth|22|l51-1|9-10|Dot Plots and Shape — A dot plot stacks one dot per value on a number line, so the shape of the whole data set is visible at a glance.
mth|22|l51-2|9-10|Histograms and Bins — A histogram groups numbers into equal-width bins and draws a bar for each bin, so it works for large data sets and messy decimals.
mth|22|l51-3|9-10|Box Plots and Quartiles — A box plot draws five numbers, the minimum, first quartile, median, third quartile and maximum, and shows where the middle half of the data lives.
mth|22|l51-4|9-10|Mean Versus Median — The mean shares the total equally and the median finds the middle; one very large value pulls the mean but barely moves the median.
mth|22|l51-5|9-10|Range and Interquartile Range — The range measures the full stretch of the data and the interquartile range measures the stretch of the middle half, which extreme values cannot touch.
mth|22|l51-6|9-10|Standard Deviation, Introduced — Standard deviation measures the typical distance of the values from the mean, using every value in the data set.
mth|22|l51-7|9-10|Outliers and Their Pull — An outlier is a value far outside the pattern of the rest, and it pulls the mean and range much harder than the median and IQR.
mth|22|l51-8|9-10|Comparing Two Distributions — To compare two data sets fairly, compare their shape, their center and their spread, not just one number.
mth|22|l51-9|9-10|Choosing the Right Summary — Report the mean and standard deviation for symmetric data with no outliers; report the median and IQR when the data are skewed or have outliers.
mth|22|l51-10|9-10|Two-Way Frequency Tables — A two-way table counts people by two categories at once, with the cells giving joint counts and the margins giving totals for each category.
mth|22|l51-11|9-10|Conditional Relative Frequencies — A conditional relative frequency divides a cell by its row or column total, which lets you compare groups of different sizes and look for an association.
mth|22|l51-12|9-10|Reading Data Honestly — Check the axis, the sample and the choice of summary before you believe a graph or a statistic, because each one can be bent to mislead.
mth|22|l52-1|9-10|Reading a Scatter Plot — A scatter plot puts one dot per pair of measurements, and its overall drift up or down shows whether the two variables move together.
mth|22|l52-2|9-10|Drawing a Line of Fit — A line of fit runs through the middle of the cloud of dots, and its equation lets you predict the response from the explanatory variable.
mth|22|l52-3|9-10|Slope and Intercept in Context — In a line of fit, the slope is the predicted change in the response for each one-unit increase in the explanatory variable, and the intercept is the prediction at zero.
mth|22|l52-4|9-10|Residuals Measure the Miss — A residual is the actual value minus the predicted value; positive means the dot is above the line and negative means below.
mth|22|l52-5|9-10|Residual Plots and Patterns — A residual plot with no pattern says a line fits well; a residual plot with a curve or a fan says the line is the wrong model.
mth|22|l52-6|9-10|The Correlation Coefficient — The correlation coefficient r, between −1 and 1, gives the direction and strength of a straight-line relationship in a single number.
mth|22|l52-7|9-10|What r Cannot See — The correlation coefficient only measures straight-line pattern, so a curve, an outlier or a cluster can make r badly misleading.
mth|22|l52-8|9-10|Correlation Is Not Causation — A strong correlation shows that two variables move together; it does not show that one causes the other, because a third variable may drive both.
mth|22|l52-9|9-10|When a Line Fails: Exponential Growth — When each step multiplies the response by the same factor instead of adding the same amount, the data follow an exponential model, not a line.
mth|22|l52-10|9-10|Quadratic Models and Second Differences — When first differences change but second differences are constant, the data follow a quadratic model with an x-squared term.
mth|22|l52-11|9-10|How Far a Prediction Can Reach — Predicting inside the range of the data is interpolation and is usually safe; predicting outside it is extrapolation and can fail badly.
mth|23|l53-1|11-12|The Number i — The imaginary unit i is defined by i^2 = −1, so every square root of a negative number can be written as a real number times i.
mth|23|l53-2|11-12|Arithmetic with Complex Numbers — A complex number a + bi is added like binomials, multiplied with i^2 = −1, and divided by multiplying top and bottom by the conjugate.
mth|23|l53-3|11-12|Quadratics with Complex Solutions — When the discriminant b^2 − 4ac is negative, the quadratic formula gives two complex solutions that are conjugates of each other.
mth|23|l53-4|11-12|Long Division of Polynomials — Polynomial long division repeats one cycle: divide the leading terms, multiply, subtract, bring down, until the remainder has smaller degree than the divisor.
mth|23|l53-5|11-12|Synthetic Division and Remainders — Synthetic division is a shortcut for dividing by x − c, and the remainder theorem says the remainder always equals p(c).
mth|23|l53-6|11-12|The Factor Theorem — x − c is a factor of p(x) exactly when p(c) = 0, so testing a few candidate values can crack a polynomial into linear factors.
mth|23|l53-7|11-12|Counting Every Zero — The fundamental theorem of algebra says a degree-n polynomial has exactly n zeros when complex zeros and repeats are counted.
mth|23|l53-8|11-12|Multiplicity and the Graph — At a zero of odd multiplicity the graph crosses the x-axis; at a zero of even multiplicity it touches the axis and turns back.
mth|23|l53-9|11-12|End Behavior — Far from the origin, a polynomial's graph follows its leading term: the degree and the sign of the leading coefficient decide which way each end points.
mth|23|l53-10|11-12|Building the Box Model — A real situation becomes a polynomial model by writing each quantity in terms of one variable, then a table or graph shows where the model peaks.
mth|23|l53-11|11-12|Systems with a Quadratic — To solve a system that mixes a line with a parabola or circle, substitute one equation into the other and solve the resulting quadratic.
mth|23|l54-1|11-12|Domains and Simplifying — A rational expression is undefined where its denominator is zero, and it simplifies by canceling common factors, never common terms.
mth|23|l54-2|11-12|Multiplying and Dividing — Multiply rational expressions by factoring everything and canceling across the fractions; divide by multiplying by the reciprocal of the second fraction.
mth|23|l54-3|11-12|Adding and Subtracting — To add or subtract rational expressions, rewrite each over the least common denominator, then combine the numerators and simplify.
mth|23|l54-4|11-12|Vertical Asymptotes and Holes — Where a denominator is zero, the graph has a vertical asymptote if the factor does not cancel and a hole if it does.
mth|23|l54-5|11-12|Horizontal Asymptotes — Compare the degrees of numerator and denominator: lower on top gives y = 0, equal degrees give the ratio of leading coefficients, higher on top gives no horizontal asymptote.
mth|23|l54-6|11-12|Inverse Variation — Two quantities vary inversely when their product is a constant, so y = k/x and doubling one halves the other.
mth|23|l54-7|11-12|Solving and Extraneous Solutions — Clear the denominators by multiplying every term by the least common denominator, solve, then reject any answer that makes an original denominator zero.
mth|23|l54-8|11-12|Work and Round-Trip Problems — Rates add: two workers together complete 1/a + 1/b of a job per hour, and a round trip's average speed is total distance over total time.
mth|23|l54-9|11-12|Radical Functions and Equations — A square root function needs a nonnegative input under the root; to solve a radical equation, isolate the root, square both sides, and check for extraneous answers.
mth|23|l54-10|11-12|Inverse Functions — An inverse function undoes the original: swap x and y and solve for y, and a function has an inverse only if it passes the horizontal line test.
mth|23|l54-11|11-12|Composition of Functions — Composing functions feeds one function's output into another, written f(g(x)); the order matters, and inverses compose to give back x.
mth|24|l55-1|11-12|Growth by a Constant Factor — An exponential function multiplies by the same factor each step, so y = a × b^x with starting value a and growth factor b.
mth|24|l55-2|11-12|Compound Interest and the Number e — Compounding more often raises the return only a little, and the limit of that process is the number e ≈ 2.71828, the base of continuous growth.
mth|24|l55-3|11-12|Decay and Half-Life — A half-life is the time for an amount to drop to half, and A = A0 × (1/2)^(t/h) counts how many halvings have happened.
mth|24|l55-4|11-12|What a Logarithm Asks — A logarithm is an exponent: log_b(y) = x means b^x = y, so log_2(32) asks what power of 2 gives 32.
mth|24|l55-5|11-12|Common Logs, Natural Logs and Calculators — Calculators give base-10 logs (log) and base-e logs (ln); any other base comes from the change-of-base rule log_b(y) = ln(y) ÷ ln(b).
mth|24|l55-6|11-12|The Three Rules for Logs — Because logs are exponents, log(ab) = log a + log b, log(a ÷ b) = log a − log b, and log(a^n) = n × log a.
mth|24|l55-7|11-12|Solving Exponential Equations — To find an unknown exponent, match bases if you can; otherwise take the log of both sides and use the power rule to bring the exponent down.
mth|24|l55-8|11-12|Solving Logarithmic Equations — Rewrite a log equation as an exponential one, solve, and then reject any solution that makes a log's input zero or negative.
mth|24|l55-9|11-12|Doubling Time From a Rate — Set the growth factor's power equal to 2 (or 1/2) and solve with logs; for continuous growth the doubling time is ln(2) ÷ r, roughly 70 divided by the percent rate.
mth|24|l55-10|11-12|Scales Built on Logs — The Richter, decibel and pH scales report the exponent, so each step of 1 means multiplying the real quantity by 10 (or, for earthquake energy, by about 31.6).
mth|24|l55-11|11-12|Growth That Hits a Ceiling — Logistic growth starts like exponential growth, then slows as it nears a limit L, following P = L ÷ (1 + C × e^(−kt)).
mth|24|l56-1|11-12|Measuring Angles in Radians — A radian measures an angle by arc length divided by radius, so a full turn is 2π radians and 180° = π radians.
mth|24|l56-2|11-12|Sine and Cosine as Coordinates — On the circle of radius 1, an angle θ measured from the positive x-axis lands at the point (cos θ, sin θ).
mth|24|l56-3|11-12|Tangent and the Pythagorean Identity — tan θ = sin θ ÷ cos θ, and because every unit-circle point satisfies x^2 + y^2 = 1, sin^2 θ + cos^2 θ = 1 for every angle.
mth|24|l56-4|11-12|Graphing Sine and Cosine — Unwrap the unit circle and the y-coordinates draw the sine wave: period 2π, highest at 1, lowest at −1, crossing the midline at 0, π and 2π.
mth|24|l56-5|11-12|Stretching the Wave — In y = A sin(Bx) + D, |A| is the amplitude, 2π ÷ B is the period, and y = D is the midline.
mth|24|l56-6|11-12|Phase Shift and the Tangent Graph — y = A sin(B(x − C)) + D shifts the wave right by C; tangent has period π and vertical asymptotes wherever cosine is zero.
mth|24|l56-7|11-12|Daylight and Tides — From a maximum, a minimum, the period and the time of a peak or midline crossing, you can write a sine or cosine model for any regular cycle.
mth|24|l56-8|11-12|Sound and the Power Grid — A pure tone is a sine wave in time; its frequency in hertz is cycles per second, and its period is 1 divided by the frequency.
