mth|24|l56-9|11-12|Solving Trigonometric Equations — Isolate the trig function, find the reference angle, use the quadrants where the sign fits, then add multiples of the period for all solutions.
mth|24|l56-10|11-12|The Law of Sines — In any triangle, a ÷ sin A = b ÷ sin B = c ÷ sin C, so two angles and one side determine the rest.
mth|24|l56-11|11-12|The Law of Cosines — c^2 = a^2 + b^2 − 2ab cos C handles two sides with the included angle, or three sides, and turns into the Pythagorean theorem when C = 90°.
mth|25|l57-1|11-12|Terms, Positions and Rules — A sequence is an ordered list of numbers, and a rule tells you how to find any term from its position or from the term before it.
mth|25|l57-2|11-12|Arithmetic Sequences — In an arithmetic sequence each term is the one before plus a fixed common difference d, so a_n = a_1 + (n − 1)d.
mth|25|l57-3|11-12|Geometric Sequences — In a geometric sequence each term is the one before times a fixed common ratio r, so a_n = a_1 × r^(n − 1).
mth|25|l57-4|11-12|Sigma Notation — Sigma notation is a compact way to write a sum: it names the formula for each term and the range of index values to add.
mth|25|l57-5|11-12|Arithmetic Series — The sum of the first n terms of an arithmetic sequence is S_n = n(a_1 + a_n)/2: the number of terms times the average of the first and last.
mth|25|l57-6|11-12|Geometric Series — The sum of the first n terms of a geometric sequence is S_n = a_1(r^n − 1)/(r − 1), which comes from subtracting the sum from r times itself.
mth|25|l57-7|11-12|Infinite Geometric Series — An infinite geometric series has a finite sum, a_1/(1 − r), exactly when the common ratio is between −1 and 1; otherwise the partial sums never settle.
mth|25|l57-8|11-12|Repeating Decimals and Steady States — A repeating decimal is an infinite geometric series in disguise, and so is a dose that builds up in the body, so the same formula gives an exact answer for both.
mth|25|l57-9|11-12|Fibonacci and the Golden Ratio — The Fibonacci sequence adds its two previous terms to get the next, and the ratio of neighboring terms settles toward the golden ratio, about 1.618.
mth|25|l57-10|11-12|Loans and Populations — Many real processes are recursive: next month's balance or next year's population comes from this one by a fixed rule, and stepping the rule forward shows where it goes.
mth|25|l57-11|11-12|Fractals and Self-Similarity — A fractal is built by repeating one rule at ever smaller scales, and geometric sequences describe how its pieces multiply and shrink.
mth|25|l58-1|11-12|From Question to Model — A model starts with a sharp question and a short list of assumptions, and the assumptions decide which math you can use.
mth|25|l58-2|11-12|Checking and Revising — A model is checked against reality or common sense, and when it misses, you change an assumption and go around the cycle again.
mth|25|l58-3|11-12|Reading Change in a Table — Constant first differences point to a linear model, constant second differences to a quadratic, and constant ratios to an exponential.
mth|25|l58-4|11-12|Clues in the Situation — Words in a situation signal a family: a fixed amount per unit means linear, a percent per unit means exponential, thrown objects and areas mean quadratic, and repeating cycles mean periodic.
mth|25|l58-5|11-12|Fitting a Model to Data — Technology finds the line or curve of best fit, and residuals, actual minus predicted, tell you how well it fits and whether the family is right.
mth|25|l58-6|11-12|Combining Functions — Real models are often built from parts: subtracting cost from revenue, multiplying price by quantity, or feeding one function's output into another.
mth|25|l58-7|11-12|Transforming a Model — Shifting, stretching or flipping a parent function adjusts a model to a new starting time, starting value or scale without changing its family.
mth|25|l58-8|11-12|Piecewise Models — When the rule changes at a threshold, like a parking rate, a tax bracket or a shipping tier, a piecewise function models each piece on its own interval.
mth|25|l58-9|11-12|Error and Sensitivity — Every model's answer carries error from its inputs and assumptions, so test how much the answer moves when an input moves, and report a range rather than one number.
mth|25|l58-10|11-12|The Limits of a Model — A model is only as good as its assumptions and its data range, so know where it stops applying and say so.
mth|25|l58-11|11-12|Communicating a Result — A finished model is reported with the question, the assumptions, the method, the answer with its range, and what would change it.
mth|26|l59-1|11-12|Center, Spread and Shape — To describe a set of numbers, report its center, how far the numbers spread from that center, and the shape of the pile.
mth|26|l59-2|11-12|The Normal Curve and the 68-95-99.7 Rule — When data follow a normal curve, about 68% fall within 1 standard deviation of the mean, 95% within 2, and 99.7% within 3.
mth|26|l59-3|11-12|Z-Scores Measure Distance in Standard Deviations — A z-score tells how many standard deviations a value sits above or below the mean, so scores from different scales can be compared.
mth|26|l59-4|11-12|From a Z-Score to a Percentile — A percentile is the percent of values below a given value; on a normal curve, the z-score tells you the percentile through a standard table.
mth|26|l59-5|11-12|When the Normal Model Fits — Use the normal model only when the data are roughly symmetric and bell-shaped; skewed, two-peaked or bounded data break it.
mth|26|l59-6|11-12|Independence and the Multiplication Rule — When two events are independent, the probability that both happen is the product of their separate probabilities.
mth|26|l59-7|11-12|Conditional Probability and Two-Way Tables — A conditional probability restricts attention to one row or column of a table; if it matches the overall probability, the events are independent.
mth|26|l59-8|11-12|The Addition Rule — To find the chance that A or B happens, add their probabilities and subtract the overlap so nothing is counted twice.
mth|26|l59-9|11-12|Expected Value and Decisions — Expected value is the long-run average result per trial: multiply each outcome by its probability and add.
mth|26|l59-10|11-12|The Binomial Setting — When you repeat the same independent yes-or-no trial a fixed number of times, the count of successes follows a binomial distribution.
mth|26|l59-11|11-12|Mean and Spread of a Binomial Count — In a binomial setting the expected count is n × p and the standard deviation is √(n × p × (1 − p)), which tells you which counts are unusual.
mth|26|l60-1|11-12|Populations, Samples and Statistics — A population is the whole group you want to know about; a sample is the part you actually measure, and a statistic from it estimates a parameter of the population.
mth|26|l60-2|11-12|Surveys, Observational Studies and Experiments — A survey asks, an observational study watches, and an experiment assigns treatments; only an experiment can show that one thing causes another.
mth|26|l60-3|11-12|Random Samples and Sources of Bias — A simple random sample gives every group of the same size the same chance of being chosen; anything that tilts who is asked or who answers is bias.
mth|26|l60-4|11-12|Why Samples Differ From Each Other — Even perfect random samples give different results each time; the pattern of those results, the sampling distribution, is predictable.
mth|26|l60-5|11-12|Simulating a Sampling Distribution — Simulate many random samples with coins, dice or a computer to see how far a statistic typically strays; the spread shrinks like 1 divided by the square root of n.
mth|26|l60-6|11-12|Margin of Error and Confidence Intervals — A confidence interval reports a sample estimate plus and minus a margin of error, a range that captures the true value in about 95% of samples.
mth|26|l60-7|11-12|The Logic of a Hypothesis Test — A hypothesis test assumes the claim is true, asks how surprising the data would then be, and rejects the claim only if the data are very unlikely under it.
mth|26|l60-8|11-12|How Surprising Is Surprising? The P-Value — The p-value is the chance of a result at least as extreme as yours if the null hypothesis were true; a small p-value is evidence against the null.
mth|26|l60-9|11-12|Statistical Versus Practical Significance — With a huge sample, even a tiny, unimportant difference becomes statistically significant; always ask how big the effect is, not just whether p is small.
mth|26|l60-10|11-12|Headlines Built on Numbers — Before believing a data claim, ask who was sampled, how big the sample was, whether the effect is relative or absolute, and who paid for the study.
mth|26|l60-11|11-12|Experiments That Earn a Cause — Random assignment, a control group, blinding and replication are what let an experiment say 'this caused that.'
mth|27|l61-1|11-12|Simple Interest — Simple interest is charged on the original amount only: interest = principal × rate × time.
mth|27|l61-2|11-12|Compound Interest — Compound interest is paid on the principal and on the interest already earned, so a balance grows by a constant factor each period.
mth|27|l61-3|11-12|The Effective Annual Rate — The effective annual rate (APY) is the true percent a balance grows in one year once compounding is counted: APY = (1 + r/n)^n − 1.
mth|27|l61-4|11-12|Car Loans and Amortization — An amortized loan is repaid with equal monthly payments; each payment covers that month's interest first, and the rest lowers the balance.
mth|27|l61-5|11-12|Credit Cards and the Minimum Payment — A credit card charges monthly interest on whatever you have not paid, so paying only the minimum keeps you in debt for years.
mth|27|l61-6|11-12|Mortgages and Student Loans — Long loans use the same payment formula as car loans, but over many years the interest can exceed the amount borrowed.
mth|27|l61-7|11-12|The Time Value of Money — A dollar today is worth more than a dollar later because it can earn interest; present value tells what a future amount is worth now.
mth|27|l61-8|11-12|Paychecks, Taxes and Budgets — Take-home pay is gross pay minus taxes and other deductions, and a budget assigns that smaller number before it is spent.
mth|27|l61-9|11-12|Inflation — Inflation makes prices rise by a percent each year, so money that does not grow at least as fast quietly loses buying power.
mth|27|l61-10|11-12|Comparing Two Offers — To compare offers, put them over the same time span, add every cost, and compare the totals, not the headline numbers.
mth|27|l61-11|11-12|Big Choices Over Time — For a decision that lasts years, compare total cost over the same span, include what you would give up, and test how the answer changes.
mth|27|l62-1|11-12|A Question You Can Measure — A good modeling question is specific, measurable and bounded: it names the quantity, the place and the time, and a way to decide the answer.
mth|27|l62-2|11-12|Assumptions and Variables — Every model leaves things out on purpose; write down each assumption and each variable so you know what the model can and cannot say.
mth|27|l62-3|11-12|Gathering Data — Plan the data before collecting it: decide what to record, in what unit, how often, and by whom, and write it in a table the same way every time.
mth|27|l62-4|11-12|Cleaning the Data — Before modeling, check the data for typos, mixed units, missing values and outliers, and record every change you make.
mth|27|l62-5|11-12|A First Model — Start with the simplest model that could work, fit it to the data, and check its predictions against values it has not seen.
mth|27|l62-6|11-12|Comparing Models — Compare candidate models by how far their predictions miss the data, and prefer the simpler model unless a more complex one clearly does better.
mth|27|l62-7|11-12|Wiggling the Inputs — Sensitivity analysis changes one input at a time to see how much the conclusion moves; the inputs that move it most are the ones to measure best.
mth|27|l62-8|11-12|Honest Pictures — A graph should let a reader see the true size of an effect: start bar axes at zero, label units, show the data points, and never hide the scale.
mth|27|l62-9|11-12|Writing the Argument — A mathematical argument states a claim, gives the evidence, explains the reasoning that links them, and names its limits.
mth|27|l62-10|11-12|Presenting and Defending — Present the claim, the key number and the graph in the first minute, then answer challenges by returning to the data and the assumptions.
mth|27|l62-11|11-12|The Project Plan — A capstone runs on a written plan with dated checkpoints: question, data, model, test, report, so that problems show up early enough to fix.
mth|3|l5-1|k-2|Hundreds, Tens and Ones — A three-digit number is made of hundreds, tens and ones.
mth|3|l5-2|k-2|Expanded Form — Expanded form breaks a number into hundreds plus tens plus ones.
mth|3|l5-3|k-2|Reading and Writing Numbers — Say the hundreds, then the tens and ones, to read a number up to 1,000.
mth|3|l5-4|k-2|Which Number Is Bigger? — Compare the hundreds first, then the tens, then the ones.
mth|3|l5-5|k-2|The Signs >, < and = — Use > for greater than, < for less than and = for equal to.
mth|3|l5-6|k-2|Counting by 5s, 10s and 100s — Skip counting adds the same amount each time: 5, 10 or 100.
mth|3|l5-7|k-2|100 More and 100 Less — Adding or taking away 100 changes only the hundreds digit.
mth|3|l5-8|k-2|Even and Odd Numbers — Even numbers make pairs with none left over; odd numbers have one left over.
mth|3|l6-1|k-2|Tens With Tens, Ones With Ones — To add two-digit numbers, add the tens together and the ones together.
mth|3|l6-2|k-2|Trading Ten Ones for a Ten — When the ones add up to 10 or more, trade ten ones for one ten.
mth|3|l6-3|k-2|Adding Three or Four Numbers — Add several numbers by finding friendly pairs and keeping tens and ones straight.
mth|3|l6-4|k-2|Subtracting Tens and Ones — To subtract, take tens from tens and ones from ones.
mth|3|l6-5|k-2|Trading a Ten for Ten Ones — When there are not enough ones to subtract, break one ten into ten ones.
mth|3|l6-6|k-2|Adding and Subtracting to 1,000 — With hundreds, add or subtract hundreds, tens and ones each in their own place.
mth|3|l6-7|k-2|Mental Math with 10s and 100s — You can add or subtract 10 or 100 in your head by changing one digit.
mth|3|l6-8|k-2|Why the Strategy Works — A good strategy works because it keeps the tens and ones in their places.
mth|4|l7-1|k-2|Longer and Shorter — To compare two lengths, line up one end, then look at the other end.
mth|4|l7-2|k-2|Putting Three in Order — Order lengths from shortest to longest by comparing two at a time.
mth|4|l7-3|k-2|Units End to End — Measure by laying same-size units end to end with no gaps and no overlaps.
mth|4|l7-4|k-2|Inches and Feet — An inch is a small unit. A foot is 12 inches. Use a ruler to measure.
mth|4|l7-5|k-2|Centimeters and Meters — A centimeter is smaller than an inch. A meter is 100 centimeters.
mth|4|l7-6|k-2|Estimate, Then Measure — An estimate is a careful guess. Use something you know as a guide.
mth|4|l7-7|k-2|Picture Graphs and Bar Graphs — A graph shows counts with pictures or bars so you can compare fast.
mth|4|l7-8|k-2|Line Plots — A line plot puts an X above each measurement on a number line.
mth|4|l8-1|k-2|Hour and Half Hour — The short hand tells the hour. Long hand on 12 means o'clock; long hand on 6 means half past.
mth|4|l8-2|k-2|Five Minutes at a Time — Each number on the clock is 5 minutes. Count by fives from the 12.
mth|4|l8-3|k-2|Morning, Noon and Night — A.M. is morning, before noon. P.M. is afternoon and evening, after noon.
mth|4|l8-4|k-2|What Each Coin Is Worth — A penny is 1 cent, a nickel 5 cents, a dime 10 cents, a quarter 25 cents.
mth|4|l8-5|k-2|Counting Mixed Coins — Start with the biggest coins. Count on by 25, 10, 5, then 1.
mth|4|l8-6|k-2|Money Word Problems — 100 cents make 1 dollar. Add or subtract cents to solve money problems.
mth|4|l8-7|k-2|Making Change by Counting Up — To make change, count up from the price to the money paid.
mth|5|l9-1|k-2|Sides and Corners — We name a flat shape by counting its sides and corners.
mth|5|l9-2|k-2|Hexagons and Circles — A hexagon has six sides; a circle is round with no sides at all.
mth|5|l9-3|k-2|Cubes and Spheres — Solid shapes take up space; a cube has flat square faces and a sphere is round all over.
mth|5|l9-4|k-2|Cones and Cylinders — A cylinder has two flat circle faces; a cone has one circle face and a point.
mth|5|l9-5|k-2|Putting Shapes Together — Small shapes can join to make a bigger shape.
mth|5|l9-6|k-2|Drawing a Shape — To draw a shape, decide the number of sides and corners first.
mth|5|l9-7|k-2|Shapes Around Us — Shapes are everywhere: in signs, buildings and floors.
mth|5|l10-1|k-2|What Half Means — A half is one of two equal parts of a whole.
mth|5|l10-2|k-2|Halves Can Look Different — Two halves of the same whole are equal even if they are different shapes.
mth|5|l10-3|k-2|Thirds — A third is one of three equal parts of a whole.
mth|5|l10-4|k-2|Fourths and Quarters — A fourth, also called a quarter, is one of four equal parts.
mth|5|l10-5|k-2|Squares in Rows and Columns — A rectangle can be cut into rows and columns of same-size squares.
mth|5|l10-6|k-2|Sharing Fairly — A fair share means every person gets an equal part of the whole.
mth|6|l11-1|3-5|Counting Equal Groups — Multiplication counts equal groups: 4 groups of 6 is 4 × 6 = 24.
mth|6|l11-2|3-5|Adding the Same Number Again — Adding the same number over and over is multiplying: 5 + 5 + 5 is the same as 3 × 5.
mth|6|l11-3|3-5|Rows and Columns — An array lines things up in rows and columns, and rows × how many in a row gives the total.
mth|6|l11-4|3-5|Turn the Array Around — Turning an array shows that 3 × 5 and 5 × 3 have the same product; the order of the factors does not change it.
mth|6|l11-5|3-5|Break a Factor Apart — To multiply a hard fact, break one factor into two easy parts, multiply each part, and add the two products.
mth|6|l11-6|3-5|Rectangles and Area — A rectangle's area, counted in squares, is one side times the other, so a rectangle is a picture of a product.
mth|6|l11-7|3-5|Twos, Fours and Eights — Multiplying by 2 is doubling; double again for the 4s and once more for the 8s.
mth|6|l11-8|3-5|Fives and Tens — Products of 10 end in 0, products of 5 end in 5 or 0, and a fives fact is half of the matching tens fact.
mth|6|l11-9|3-5|The Nines Pattern — For a nines fact, the tens digit is one less than the other factor and the two digits add to 9; or take 10 × the number and subtract the number.
mth|6|l11-10|3-5|Multiples and the Table — A multiple of a number is that number times a whole number, and the multiplication table is full of patterns.
mth|6|l11-11|3-5|Solving Word Problems — Find the groups and the size of each group, write the multiplication, solve it, then check that the answer fits the story.
mth|6|l12-1|3-5|Sharing Fairly — Division as sharing splits a total into a set number of equal groups and asks how many each group gets.
mth|6|l12-2|3-5|Making Groups — Division as grouping asks how many groups of a given size fit in the total.
mth|6|l12-3|3-5|The Words of Division — In 28 ÷ 4 = 7, the dividend is 28, the divisor is 4 and the quotient is 7.
mth|6|l12-4|3-5|The Missing Factor — Every division is a missing-factor problem: 28 ÷ 4 asks what number times 4 makes 28.
mth|6|l12-5|3-5|Fact Families — Three numbers like 4, 7 and 28 make a fact family of two multiplications and two divisions.
mth|6|l12-6|3-5|Dividing With the Table — To divide with the multiplication table, find the divisor's row, slide across to the dividend, and read the quotient at the top of that column.
mth|6|l12-7|3-5|Dividing With Remainders — When groups do not come out even, the leftover is the remainder, and it is always smaller than the divisor.
mth|6|l12-8|3-5|What the Leftover Means — The story decides what to do with the remainder: round up, drop it, or make it the answer.
mth|6|l12-9|3-5|Two-Step Problems — Some problems take two steps; solve the first step, then use its answer in the second.
mth|6|l12-10|3-5|Which Operation Goes First — In a number sentence with mixed operations, do what is in parentheses first, then multiply and divide, then add and subtract.
mth|6|l12-11|3-5|Estimate to Check — Round the numbers, find a quick estimate, and use it to catch answers that cannot be right.
mth|7|l13-1|3-5|Each Place Is Ten Times More — Every place in a number is worth ten times the place to its right.
mth|7|l13-2|3-5|Reading Numbers in Groups of Three — Commas split a number into periods of three digits, and each period is read like a small number followed by its name.
mth|7|l13-3|3-5|Three Ways to Write a Number — Standard form, expanded form, and word form are three ways to write the same number.
mth|7|l13-4|3-5|Comparing Place by Place — To compare two whole numbers, line up the places and compare from the left until the digits differ.
mth|7|l13-5|3-5|Putting Numbers in Order — To order a set of numbers, compare them in pairs and list them from least to greatest or greatest to least.
mth|7|l13-6|3-5|Rounding to Any Place — To round, look at the digit just to the right of the place you want; 5 or more rounds up, less than 5 keeps the digit.
mth|7|l13-7|3-5|When Rounding Helps — Round to estimate quickly and to check answers, but use exact numbers when every unit counts.
mth|7|l13-8|3-5|Adding Big Numbers — Add place by place from the right, and regroup ten of one place into one of the next place when a column reaches 10 or more.
mth|7|l13-9|3-5|Subtracting with Regrouping — Subtract place by place from the right, and when a digit is too small, regroup one from the next place to the left.
mth|7|l13-10|3-5|Checking Your Answer — An estimate tells you whether an answer is reasonable, and the inverse operation tells you whether it is exactly right.
mth|7|l14-1|3-5|Multiplying by Tens and Hundreds — To multiply by a multiple of ten or a hundred, multiply the basic fact, then attach the zeros.
mth|7|l14-2|3-5|The Area Model — Split each factor by place value, multiply every part by every other part, and add the pieces.
mth|7|l14-3|3-5|Partial Products — Partial products are the pieces of an area model written in a column, then added.
mth|7|l14-4|3-5|The Standard Algorithm — The standard algorithm multiplies by the ones digit, then by the tens digit with a placeholder zero, and adds the two lines.
mth|7|l14-5|3-5|Partial Quotients — Divide by taking away easy groups of the divisor, then add up how many groups you took.
mth|7|l14-6|3-5|Long Division — Long division works one place at a time from the left: divide, multiply, subtract, bring down, repeat.
mth|7|l14-7|3-5|What the Remainder Means — A remainder is what is left after dividing, and the situation tells you whether to round up, drop it, or report it.
mth|7|l14-8|3-5|Estimating Products and Quotients — Round the factors, or pick compatible numbers, to get a quick estimate before you compute.
mth|7|l14-9|3-5|Multi-Step Problems — Break a multi-step problem into a plan, do one step at a time, and check that the last step answers the question.
mth|7|l14-10|3-5|Multiply to Check Division — Multiplication undoes division, so multiply the quotient by the divisor and add any remainder to check.
mth|8|l15-1|3-5|Equal Parts Make Fractions — A fraction names equal parts of a whole: the bottom number counts the equal parts, the top number counts how many you mean.
mth|8|l15-2|3-5|Unit Fractions Build the Rest — A unit fraction has 1 on top, and every other fraction is a stack of unit fractions counted up.
mth|8|l15-3|3-5|Fractions on a Number Line — A fraction is a number with its own place on the number line, found by cutting the space between whole numbers into equal jumps.
mth|8|l15-4|3-5|Same Amount, Different Names — Equivalent fractions name the same amount using different-size parts, so they sit at the same point on the number line.
mth|8|l15-5|3-5|Multiply to Find Equivalents — Multiplying the numerator and the denominator by the same number makes an equivalent fraction, because you are multiplying by 1.
mth|8|l15-6|3-5|Whole Numbers as Fractions — Any whole number can be written as a fraction, like 3 = 3/1 = 6/2 = 12/4, because a whole holds a full set of equal parts.
mth|8|l15-7|3-5|Same Bottom or Same Top — With the same denominator, more parts means a bigger fraction; with the same numerator, a bigger denominator means a smaller fraction.
mth|8|l15-8|3-5|Benchmarks: 0, One Half and 1 — Compare a fraction to 0, 1/2 or 1 first; that quick check often settles which fraction is bigger.
mth|8|l15-9|3-5|Fractions Greater Than One — When the numerator is bigger than the denominator, the fraction is more than one whole.
mth|8|l15-10|3-5|Mixed Numbers — A mixed number is a whole number plus a fraction, and you can rewrite it as a single fraction and back again.
mth|8|l16-1|3-5|Adding With Like Denominators — When denominators match, add the numerators and keep the denominator, because you are counting pieces of the same size.
mth|8|l16-2|3-5|Subtracting With Like Denominators — When denominators match, subtract the numerators and keep the denominator, then check by adding back.
mth|8|l16-3|3-5|Mixed Numbers With Like Denominators — Add or subtract the whole parts and the fraction parts separately, then regroup any fraction that is more than one.
mth|8|l16-4|3-5|Finding a Common Denominator — To add or subtract fractions with different denominators, first rename them so they share the same denominator.
mth|8|l16-5|3-5|Adding Unlike Fractions — Rename the fractions with a common denominator, then add the numerators and keep that denominator.
mth|8|l16-6|3-5|Subtracting Unlike Fractions — Rename with a common denominator, subtract the numerators, and check that the answer is reasonable.
mth|8|l16-7|3-5|A Fraction Times a Whole Number — Multiplying a fraction by a whole number is repeated addition: 3 × 2/5 means three jumps of 2/5, which is 6/5.
mth|8|l16-8|3-5|A Fraction Times a Fraction — To multiply two fractions, multiply the numerators and multiply the denominators; an area model shows why.
mth|8|l16-9|3-5|Bigger or Smaller? Scaling — Multiplying by a fraction less than 1 makes a number smaller; multiplying by a number greater than 1 makes it bigger.
mth|8|l16-10|3-5|Recipes and Measurements — Read the problem, decide whether to add, subtract or multiply, compute carefully, and then ask whether the answer makes sense.
mth|8|l17-1|3-5|How Many Fourths in Three? — Dividing a whole number by a unit fraction asks how many of those small pieces fit inside the whole number.
mth|8|l17-2|3-5|Pictures on the Number Line — A number line shows division by a unit fraction as counting equal jumps from 0 to the whole number.
mth|8|l17-3|3-5|Check With Multiplication — Division and multiplication undo each other: if 3 ÷ 1/4 = 12, then 12 × 1/4 must equal 3.
mth|8|l17-4|3-5|A Unit Fraction Split Among Friends — Dividing a unit fraction by a whole number splits a small piece into even smaller equal pieces.
mth|8|l17-5|3-5|Which Kind of Problem Is It? — Decide whether a story counts how many small pieces fit in a whole (whole ÷ fraction) or splits a small piece (fraction ÷ whole).
mth|8|l17-6|3-5|Three Divided by Four — A fraction is a division in disguise: 3 ÷ 4 = 3/4, because 3 wholes shared 4 ways gives each person 3/4.
mth|8|l17-7|3-5|When the Answer Is More Than One — When the dividend is bigger than the divisor, the fraction answer is greater than one, and you can write it as a mixed number.
mth|8|l17-8|3-5|Is the Answer Reasonable? — Before you compute, predict whether the answer should be bigger or smaller than the number you started with.
mth|8|l17-9|3-5|Line Plots With Fractions — A line plot stacks an X above each measurement, so you can see fractional data at a glance and compare values.
mth|8|l17-10|3-5|Sharing the Total Evenly — Add all the measurements on a line plot, then divide by the number of items to find what each would get if shared equally.
mth|9|l18-1|3-5|Tenths on a Grid — A decimal is a fraction whose bottom number is 10, 100 or 1,000, written with a decimal point instead of a fraction bar.
mth|9|l18-2|3-5|Hundredths and Money — Splitting a whole into 100 equal parts gives hundredths, the second place after the decimal point, just like cents in a dollar.
mth|9|l18-3|3-5|Thousandths and the Stopwatch — Thousandths are the third place after the decimal point; one thousandth, 1/1,000, is written 0.001.
mth|9|l18-4|3-5|Reading and Writing Decimals — Read the whole number, say 'and' for the point, then read the digits after the point with the name of the last place.
mth|9|l18-5|3-5|Expanded Form — Expanded form writes a decimal as a sum of each digit's value, like 4.36 = 4 + 0.3 + 0.06.
mth|9|l18-6|3-5|Zeros That Matter — A zero added to the right end of a decimal does not change its value, but a zero between the point and a digit does.
mth|9|l18-7|3-5|Comparing Decimals — To compare decimals, line up the decimal points and compare place by place, starting from the left.
mth|9|l18-8|3-5|Rounding Decimals — To round a decimal, look at the digit one place right of the rounding place: 5 or more rounds up, less than 5 keeps it.
mth|9|l18-9|3-5|Decimals on the Number Line — Between any two whole numbers, ten equal jumps mark the tenths, and each tenth splits into ten hundredths.
mth|9|l18-10|3-5|Metric Units as Decimals — Metric units go by tens, so 1 centimeter is 0.01 meter and 1 meter is 0.001 kilometer.
mth|9|l19-1|3-5|Lining Up the Point — To add decimals, line up the decimal points so each place sits under the same place, then add like whole numbers.
mth|9|l19-2|3-5|Subtracting With Regrouping — Subtract decimals the way you add them: line up the points, fill empty places with zeros, and regroup when a digit is too small.
mth|9|l19-3|3-5|Estimating Sums and Differences — Round each decimal to a friendly number first; the estimate tells you whether your exact answer makes sense.
mth|9|l19-4|3-5|Multiplying by 10, 100 and 1,000 — Multiplying by 10 makes every digit worth 10 times more, so the decimal point shifts one place to the right; by 100, two places; by 1,000, three.
mth|9|l19-5|3-5|Multiplying a Decimal by a Whole Number — Multiply as if there were no decimal point, then place the point so the product has as many decimal places as the decimal factor.
mth|9|l19-6|3-5|Multiplying Two Decimals — When both factors are decimals, count the decimal places in both, add the counts, and give the product that many places.
mth|9|l19-7|3-5|Dividing by 10, 100 and 1,000 — Dividing by 10 makes every digit worth 10 times less, so the decimal point shifts one place to the left; by 100, two; by 1,000, three.
mth|9|l19-8|3-5|Dividing a Decimal by a Whole Number — Divide as with whole numbers, and put the decimal point in the quotient straight above the point in the number being divided.
mth|9|l19-9|3-5|Money Word Problems — Read the story, decide which operation it asks for, line up the dollars and cents, and check the answer with an estimate.
mth|9|l19-10|3-5|Measurement Word Problems — Measurement problems with decimals work like money problems: match the units, line up the places, then add, subtract, multiply or divide.
fcs|1|l1-1|k-2|What a Germ Is — Germs are tiny living things that can make you sick, and they are too small to see.
fcs|1|l1-2|k-2|Germs Ride on Hands — Germs travel on hands, in coughs and sneezes, and on food.
fcs|1|l1-3|k-2|Before You Eat — Always wash your hands before you eat or touch food.
fcs|1|l1-4|k-2|After the Bathroom and Outside — Wash after using the bathroom and after playing outside.
fcs|1|l1-5|k-2|After Pets and Sneezes — Wash after you touch a pet and after you cough or sneeze.
fcs|1|l1-6|k-2|Soap Lifts, Water Carries — Soap lifts germs off your skin, and running water carries them down the drain.
fcs|1|l1-7|k-2|Cough Into Your Elbow — Cover a cough or sneeze with your elbow, not your hand.
fcs|1|l2-1|k-2|Wet and Lather — Step one is wet your hands, and step two is lather with soap.
fcs|1|l2-2|k-2|Scrub Every Part — Step three is scrub for twenty seconds: backs, between fingers and under nails.
fcs|1|l2-3|k-2|Rinse and Dry — Step four is rinse under running water, and step five is dry with a clean towel.
fcs|1|l2-4|k-2|Sing It Twice — Twenty seconds is about two rounds of a short song like the birthday song.
fcs|1|l2-5|k-2|A Clean Towel — Dry your hands on something clean, or the germs come right back.
fcs|1|l2-6|k-2|Hand Sanitizer Helps — Hand sanitizer is a backup when there is no sink, but soap and water are better.
fcs|1|l2-7|k-2|Brush Twice a Day — Brush your teeth two times a day, morning and night, for two minutes.
fcs|1|l2-8|k-2|Watch Clean Hands in 3D — Watch a cook wash hands. Then copy each step at your sink.
fcs|10|l19-1|6-8|Parts of a Chef's Knife — A chef's knife has six named parts, and each one has a job in a cut.
fcs|10|l19-2|6-8|Sharp Is Safer — A sharp knife cuts where you aim it; a dull knife slips, and slips are how people get cut.
fcs|10|l19-3|6-8|The Right Knife for the Job — Match the knife to the food: chef's knife for most work, paring for small jobs, serrated for bread and tomatoes.
fcs|10|l19-4|6-8|The Pinch Grip — Pinch the blade in front of the handle with thumb and first finger; the other three fingers hold the handle.
fcs|10|l19-5|6-8|The Claw Hand — Curl your fingertips under and lead with your knuckles; the flat of the blade rides against the knuckles, never the tips.
fcs|10|l19-6|6-8|The Rocking Cut — Keep the tip on the board and rock the handle down and forward; the knife slices instead of chopping.
fcs|10|l19-7|6-8|Board and Damp Towel — Put a damp towel under the board so it cannot slide, and give the board room and light.
fcs|10|l19-8|6-8|Carrying and Passing a Knife — Carry a knife pointed down at your side, say what you are doing, and set it down for the next person instead of handing it over.
fcs|10|l19-9|6-8|Washing a Knife — Wash a knife by hand right away, edge away from your fingers, and dry it before it goes anywhere.
fcs|10|l19-10|6-8|Storing Knives — Store a knife where the edge is protected and no hand can find it by accident: block, magnetic strip or a guard in a drawer.
fcs|10|l19-11|6-8|Honing and Sharpening — Honing straightens the edge often; sharpening grinds a new edge rarely, and an adult does it.
fcs|10|l20-1|6-8|Slice and Rough Chop — A slice is a flat, even piece; a rough chop is quick, uneven pieces for food that will be blended or strained.
fcs|10|l20-2|6-8|Dice, Three Sizes — Dice means cubes: small is 1/4 inch, medium 1/2 inch, large 3/4 inch, and the recipe tells you which.
fcs|10|l20-3|6-8|Even Pieces Cook Evenly — Same-size pieces finish at the same time; mixed sizes give you burnt bits and raw bits in one pan.
fcs|10|l20-4|6-8|Mince — Mince means cut as small as you can, so the food melts into the dish and spreads its flavor everywhere.
fcs|10|l20-5|6-8|Julienne — Julienne is matchsticks: 1/8 inch by 1/8 inch, about 2 inches long, made from thin slabs stacked and cut into strips.
fcs|10|l20-6|6-8|Chiffonade — Chiffonade is thin ribbons of leafy herbs or greens, made by stacking, rolling tight and slicing across the roll.
fcs|10|l20-7|6-8|Dicing an Onion — Halve through the root, keep the root on, cut toward it twice, then across; the root holds the onion together.
fcs|10|l20-8|6-8|Mincing Garlic — Smash the clove under the flat of the blade to loosen the peel, slice, then rock into a fine mince.
fcs|10|l20-9|6-8|Chiffonade of Basil — Stack six to eight leaves, roll them tight, slice thin across the roll, and fluff the ribbons just before serving.
fcs|10|l20-10|6-8|Salsa Prep, Start to Finish — A practical is one recipe done in order: station set up, cuts in the right sizes, board and knife clean at the end.
fcs|10|l20-11|6-8|How the Practical Is Graded — The grade comes from safety, correct sizes, evenness, and cleanup; speed is last on the list.
fcs|10|l20-12|6-8|Watch the Cuts in 3D — Watching chop, slice, dice and mince in 3D, from any side, shows where both hands go on every cut.
fcs|11|l21-1|6-8|Dry Cups and the Liquid Cup — Dry ingredients go in nested cups you can level; liquids go in a clear cup with a spout you read at eye level.
fcs|11|l21-2|6-8|Measuring Spoons — Measuring spoons come in a set of exact sizes; a kitchen spoon from the drawer is not a measure.
fcs|11|l21-3|6-8|Spoon and Level — Spoon flour lightly into the cup and level it; scooping and shaking packs in extra flour and dries out the bake.
fcs|11|l21-4|6-8|Reading a Liquid at Eye Level — Set the liquid cup on a flat surface and read the line with your eye level to it; the curved top of the liquid is read at its bottom.
fcs|11|l21-5|6-8|Teaspoons, Tablespoons, Cups — Three teaspoons make a tablespoon, four tablespoons make a quarter cup, and sixteen tablespoons make a cup.
fcs|11|l21-6|6-8|Cups, Pints, Quarts, Gallons — Two cups make a pint, two pints make a quart, and four quarts make a gallon; a cup is 8 fluid ounces.
fcs|11|l21-7|6-8|Weight, Volume and the Scale — Volume measures space and weight measures heaviness; a kitchen scale is the most exact tool for dry ingredients.
fcs|11|l21-8|6-8|Measuring Butter and Shortening — A stick of butter is 1/2 cup with tablespoons marked on the wrapper; solid fats are pressed into a dry cup and leveled.
fcs|11|l21-9|6-8|Sticky Sugar and Sticky Syrup — Brown sugar is the one ingredient you pack; honey and peanut butter come out clean from a cup wiped with oil.
fcs|11|l21-10|6-8|Common Measuring Mistakes — Most measuring mistakes come from the wrong tool, the wrong fill, or the wrong reading, and each one shows up in the finished food.
fcs|11|l22-1|6-8|Parts of a Recipe — A written recipe has a yield, an ingredient list in order of use, and numbered steps; each part answers a different question before you cook.
fcs|11|l22-2|6-8|Read It Twice Before You Start — Read the whole recipe before touching anything, once for the picture and once for the numbers, tools and waiting times.
fcs|11|l22-3|6-8|Mixing Words — Cream, beat, whisk, stir and fold are different motions with different results; a recipe picks one on purpose.
fcs|11|l22-4|6-8|Heat Words — Simmer, boil, sauté and sear name how hot the pan is and how fast the food moves; each has a look you can recognize.
fcs|11|l22-5|6-8|Finding the Factor — Divide the servings you want by the servings the recipe makes; that number is the factor you multiply every ingredient by.
fcs|11|l22-6|6-8|Scaling Up and Changing Units — After multiplying, convert awkward amounts to bigger units with the equivalents chart, and check that your pot is big enough.
fcs|11|l22-7|6-8|Scaling Down — Cutting a recipe uses a fraction as the factor; the tricky lines are eggs and small amounts of leavening.
fcs|11|l22-8|6-8|Everything in Its Place — Mise en place means every ingredient measured and every tool set out before the heat goes on, so cooking is only cooking.
fcs|11|l22-9|6-8|A Time Plan for a Lab — Plan a lab backward from the minute it must be done, giving each step a clock time, and start the longest wait first.
fcs|11|l22-10|6-8|When an Ingredient Is Missing — A substitution swaps a missing ingredient for one that does the same job in the recipe; know the standard swaps and what changes.
fcs|11|l22-11|6-8|Plug In and Scale a Recipe — Typing a recipe into the 3D kitchen and scaling it lets you check your factor and watch the steps before you cook.
fcs|12|l23-1|6-8|Tools in the Kit — A basic hand-sewing kit has a small set of tools, and each one has a job that no other tool does as well.
fcs|12|l23-2|6-8|Needle Sizes and Thread — Hand needles are numbered so that a bigger number means a finer needle, and the needle and thread should match the fabric.
fcs|12|l23-3|6-8|Thread the Needle, Tie the Knot — Cut the thread at an angle, pass it through the eye, and roll a knot on the tail so the first stitch cannot pull through.
fcs|12|l23-4|6-8|Pins, Needles and Safe Hands — Sharp tools stay in the pincushion, get counted, and are passed handle-first so that nobody gets hurt.
fcs|12|l23-5|6-8|The Running Stitch — The running stitch is the simplest stitch, a dashed line of even stitches used for gathering, light seams and hand quilting.
fcs|12|l23-6|6-8|Basting: Stitches Meant to Leave — Basting is a long, loose running stitch that holds fabric in place temporarily and is pulled out after the real seam is sewn.
fcs|12|l23-7|6-8|The Back Stitch — The back stitch overlaps each stitch with the one before it, making the strongest hand seam and the closest thing to a machine stitch.
fcs|12|l23-8|6-8|The Whip Stitch — The whip stitch wraps thread over the edge of two pieces of fabric to join them quickly, and it shows on the outside.
fcs|12|l23-9|6-8|The Slip Stitch — The slip stitch hides inside a fold so a hem or an opening is closed with almost nothing showing on the outside.
fcs|12|l23-10|6-8|The Blanket Stitch — The blanket stitch finishes an edge with a row of upright stitches linked by a line of thread along the edge, and it can also be decorative.
fcs|12|l23-11|6-8|The Overcast Stitch — The overcast stitch wraps thread over a raw edge to stop woven fabric from fraying.
fcs|12|l23-12|6-8|Stitch Length and Tension — Good hand sewing has stitches of even length and thread pulled just enough to lie flat, and both come from slowing down.
fcs|12|l23-13|6-8|Pressing as You Go — Pressing each seam with an iron before the next step makes sewing flatter, straighter and more accurate, and the iron is handled with care.
fcs|12|l23-14|6-8|Watch the Stitches in 3D — In the 3D sewing room each stitch stays in the cloth, so you can compare how every hand stitch is built.
fcs|12|l24-1|6-8|A Button with a Shank — A button sewn with a thread shank has room underneath for the fabric it holds, so it buttons easily and does not pull loose.
fcs|12|l24-2|6-8|Hooks, Eyes and Snaps — Hooks and eyes hold edges that meet under strain, snaps hold edges that overlap, and both are sewn with many small stitches through their holes.
fcs|12|l24-3|6-8|Measuring and Marking a Hem — A good hem starts with the garment on the body, a fold pinned to the right length, and a line marked evenly all the way around.
fcs|12|l24-4|6-8|Hemming with a Slip Stitch — Sew the hem from the inside with a slip stitch that travels in the fold and catches one thread of the outer fabric so nothing shows.
fcs|12|l24-5|6-8|Mending a Split Seam — A split seam is mended from the inside with a back stitch that overlaps the sound stitches on both ends of the gap.
fcs|12|l24-6|6-8|Patching a Hole — A hole is patched with a piece of fabric larger than the hole, stitched down on all sides, either hidden underneath or shown on top.
fcs|12|l24-7|6-8|Reading a Simple Pattern — A pattern is a set of paper pieces and instructions that tells you what to cut, how much fabric to buy and what order to sew in.
fcs|12|l24-8|6-8|Measure, Mark, Cut, Sew — A pouch is made by measuring and marking on grain, cutting cleanly, back-stitching the seams, and sewing a casing for the cord.
fcs|12|l24-9|6-8|Mend or Replace? — Compare the cost of mending, in money and time, with the cost of replacing, and most small repairs win easily.
fcs|12|l24-10|6-8|Where Clothes End Up — Most discarded textiles in the United States are landfilled, so every repair that keeps a garment in use keeps it out of the trash.
fcs|13|l25-1|9-10|Touring the Machine — A sewing machine has about a dozen named parts, and you cannot thread it, fix it or read its manual until you know where each one is.
fcs|13|l25-2|9-10|Needle Types and Sizes — The needle must match the fabric: a ballpoint for knits, a universal for most wovens, a heavy needle for denim, and a fresh one for every project.
fcs|13|l25-3|9-10|Reading the Manual — Every machine is a little different, and the manual is where the threading diagram, the bobbin type and the needle position for your machine actually live.
fcs|13|l25-4|9-10|The Top Thread Path — The top thread follows one fixed path from spool to needle, and it only works if the presser foot is up when you thread it.
fcs|13|l25-5|9-10|Winding a Bobbin — A bobbin must be wound evenly, at a steady speed, with the same thread as the top, or the stitch underneath will never be right.
fcs|13|l25-6|9-10|Inserting the Bobbin — The bobbin must sit in its case or well with the thread coming off in the direction the manual shows, then be pulled through its own tension spring and up through the plate.
fcs|13|l25-7|9-10|The Lockstitch, Step by Step — A lockstitch forms when the needle carries the top thread down, a hook loops it around the bobbin thread, and the take-up lever pulls the two threads tight so they lock inside the fabric.
fcs|13|l25-8|9-10|Stitch Length and Width — Stitch length sets how far the fabric moves per stitch and width sets how far the needle swings sideways; 2.5 mm straight is the everyday setting.
fcs|13|l25-9|9-10|Reading the Tension — A balanced stitch locks in the middle of the fabric; loops on the bottom mean the top is too loose, and bobbin thread showing on top means the top is too tight.
fcs|13|l25-10|9-10|Rules at the Machine — Fingers stay behind the needle, pins come out before the foot reaches them, and the machine is off whenever hands are near the needle.
fcs|13|l25-11|9-10|Cleaning and Oiling — Lint builds up under the throat plate with every seam, and a machine brushed out and, if the manual says so, oiled regularly keeps stitching evenly for decades.
fcs|13|l26-1|9-10|The 5/8-Inch Guide — Seam allowance is the strip between the stitching and the raw edge; keep the fabric edge on the 5/8-inch line of the throat plate and the seam comes out straight and the right width.
fcs|13|l26-2|9-10|Start, Backstitch, End — Every seam starts and ends with three or four backstitches, sewn on the fabric with the tails held behind the foot, so it cannot pull open.
fcs|13|l26-3|9-10|Pivoting a Corner — To turn a sharp corner, stop with the needle down in the fabric, lift the presser foot, turn the fabric, lower the foot and go on.
fcs|13|l26-4|9-10|Finishing the Raw Edge — Woven fabric frays at a cut edge, so seam allowances get a finish: zigzagged, pinked, serged or turned under.
fcs|13|l26-5|9-10|Pressing Seams Open — Pressing means lifting and setting the iron, not sliding it; press every seam flat, then open, before sewing anything across it.
fcs|13|l26-6|9-10|Plan, Measure and Cut — A drawstring bag starts as two rectangles cut on grain, with a plan that already accounts for seam allowance and the casing fold.
fcs|13|l26-7|9-10|Sewing the Sides and Bottom — Right sides together, pinned across the seam, sewn at 5/8 inch with backstitches and pivots, and a gap left at the top of each side for the cord to come out.
fcs|13|l26-8|9-10|The Casing and the Cord — The casing is a double fold at the top, stitched close to the lower fold, and the cords go through it with a safety pin from opposite sides.
fcs|13|l26-9|9-10|Skipped Stitches — Skipped stitches almost always come from the needle: dull, bent, the wrong type for the fabric, or not pushed all the way up when inserted.
fcs|13|l26-10|9-10|Nests, Jams and Puckers — A thread nest under the fabric comes from the top thread, a jam from the bobbin area or lint, and a pucker from tension or stitch length; each has its own short checklist.
fcs|13|l26-11|9-10|Watch a Seam in 3D — Watching the machine sew in 3D, with the needle, presser foot and feed dogs moving together, shows what your hands should and should not do.
fcs|14|l27-1|9-10|Cotton and Linen — Cotton and linen are plant fibers that absorb water, breathe well and wrinkle easily, which makes them comfortable in heat and slow to dry.
fcs|14|l27-2|9-10|Wool and Silk — Wool and silk are protein fibers from animals; wool traps warm air and resists wrinkles, while silk is smooth, strong and delicate to care for.
fcs|14|l27-3|9-10|Polyester and Nylon — Polyester and nylon are made from petroleum; they dry fast, resist wrinkles and hold their shape, but they melt under high heat and do not breathe like plant fibers.
fcs|14|l27-4|9-10|Rayon and Spandex — Rayon is made from wood pulp and drapes like silk but is weak when wet; spandex stretches several times its length and returns, so a little of it changes how a garment fits.
fcs|14|l27-5|9-10|Reading the Fiber Tag — U.S. law requires most clothing to carry a tag listing every fiber by percentage, and reading it tells you how the garment will feel, wear and wash before you buy.
fcs|14|l27-6|9-10|Woven or Knit — Woven fabric is made of yarns crossing at right angles and barely stretches; knit fabric is made of loops and stretches, which decides how each is sewn, worn and washed.
fcs|14|l27-7|9-10|Plain, Twill and Satin — The three basic weaves, plain, twill and satin, change how a woven fabric looks, wears and drapes: plain is even and crisp, twill is diagonal and tough, satin is smooth and shiny.
fcs|14|l27-8|9-10|Choosing by Property — Every fabric trades off breathability, stretch, warmth and durability, and the right choice matches those properties to the job the garment has to do.
fcs|14|l27-9|9-10|Care Label Symbols — Five care symbols, washtub, triangle, square, iron and circle, plus dots for heat and an X for never, tell you how to wash any garment without a single word.
fcs|14|l27-10|9-10|The Burn Test, Described — Textile labs identify an unknown fiber by how a snip of it burns; you will read the results here, not perform them, because open flame near fabric is a fire hazard.
fcs|14|l27-11|9-10|Fast Fashion and Its Cost — Cheap, quickly made clothing costs water, energy and labor to produce and mostly ends up in landfills, so buying less, choosing durable fibers and caring for clothes is a real choice with real effects.
fcs|14|l28-1|9-10|The Envelope, Front and Back — The pattern envelope tells you what the garment is, what fabric and how much to buy, and what else you need, so read all of it before you spend a dollar.
fcs|14|l28-2|9-10|Taking Body Measurements — Pattern sizes are chosen by tape-measure numbers, not store sizes, so measure bust, waist, hip and back waist length carefully and pick the size the chart gives.
fcs|14|l28-3|9-10|Selvage and Grain — The lengthwise grain runs parallel to the selvage and is the strongest, most stable direction of a woven, so pattern pieces are placed along it unless the design says otherwise.
fcs|14|l28-4|9-10|Bias and Stretch — The bias runs at 45 degrees to the grain and stretches the most, which makes it perfect for drape and binding and a trap for anyone who cuts a piece on it by accident.
fcs|14|l28-5|9-10|Pattern Symbols — Grainline arrows, fold brackets, notches, dots and lengthen-shorten lines are a shared language that tells you exactly how to place, cut and match every piece.
fcs|14|l28-6|9-10|Laying Out the Pieces — Follow the cutting layout in the guide sheet, place every piece on grain, and pin the whole layout before cutting anything.
fcs|14|l28-7|9-10|Cutting and Marking — Cut with long strokes on the cutting line, notches outward, and transfer every dot, dart and line to the fabric before you unpin, because after that the map is gone.
fcs|14|l28-8|9-10|Ease, the Room to Move — A pattern is bigger than your body on purpose: wearing ease lets you move and breathe, design ease sets the style, and the finished-garment measurements on the tissue show how much of each you are getting.
fcs|14|l28-9|9-10|Lengthen or Shorten — Use the printed lengthen-or-shorten line to add or remove length in the middle of a piece, keep the edges straight, and make the same change on every piece that joins it.
fcs|14|l28-10|9-10|The Test Garment — Sew a quick test version in cheap fabric, mark every fit problem on the body, and move those changes to the paper pattern before cutting the real fabric.
fcs|14|l28-11|9-10|Watch the Layout in 3D — Watching a pattern being pinned, marked and cut in 3D shows why each step happens before the next.
fcs|15|l29-1|9-10|Conduction: Pan to Food — Conduction is heat moving by direct touch, and it is why the side of the food that touches the pan is the side that browns.
fcs|15|l29-2|9-10|Convection: Moving Air and Water — Convection carries heat through moving air, water or oil, which is why a convection oven cooks faster and why boiling water cooks evenly on every side.
fcs|15|l29-3|9-10|Radiation: Heat Across Space — Radiant heat travels in straight lines from a glowing element or coals to whatever faces it, which is why the broiler cooks only the top and the grill cooks only the bottom.
fcs|15|l29-4|9-10|Preheating Pays Off — Preheating the oven, the pan and the grill puts full heat on the surface of the food from the first second, which is what browning and good texture depend on.
fcs|15|l29-5|9-10|Roasting and Baking — Roasting and baking surround food with hot, dry air in an oven, and high heat with space around the food is what makes the surface brown and crisp.
fcs|15|l29-6|9-10|Sautéing and Pan-Frying — Sautéing cooks small pieces fast in a little fat in a hot pan, and it only works if the pan is hot, the food is dry, and there is room for steam to escape.
fcs|15|l29-7|9-10|Grilling and Broiling — Grilling and broiling cook with intense radiant heat from one side, which suits thin, tender foods and demands close attention and a flip.
fcs|15|l29-8|9-10|Boiling, Simmering, Poaching, Steaming — Moist-heat methods cook food in or over water at or below 212 °F, and the difference between boiling, simmering and poaching is how much the water is moving.
fcs|15|l29-9|9-10|Braising and Stewing — Braising and stewing brown the food first in dry heat, then finish it slowly in a little liquid with a lid, which makes tough cuts tender.
fcs|15|l29-10|9-10|The Crowded Pan — Food is mostly water, and if the water it gives off cannot escape, the pan never gets hotter than 212 °F on the food's surface and nothing browns.
fcs|15|l29-11|9-10|Maillard and Caramelization — Browning is two chemical changes, the Maillard reaction between proteins and sugars and the caramelization of sugar alone, and both need dry heat well above the boiling point of water.
fcs|15|l29-12|9-10|Watch Heat at Work in 3D — Watching sauté, simmer and bake in 3D lets you name the kind of heat transfer doing most of the work in each.
fcs|15|l30-1|9-10|Tender Cuts and Tough Cuts — Muscles that worked hard are tough and need slow moist heat; muscles that did little are tender and need fast dry heat.
fcs|15|l30-2|9-10|Reading the Cut at the Counter — The label and the look of a cut tell you what it is and how to cook it, and a tough cut on sale is a bargain only if you cook it the slow way.
fcs|15|l30-3|9-10|Oven Temperatures and What They Do — A low oven cooks gently and evenly, a moderate oven cooks most things, and a hot oven browns and crisps, so the temperature you choose is a choice about what the surface and center should look like.
fcs|15|l30-4|9-10|Braising a Tough Cut — A braise is brown, soften, deglaze, submerge halfway, cover, and simmer low for hours, and each step is there for a reason.
fcs|15|l30-5|9-10|Stir-Frying — Stir-frying is a very hot pan, everything cut and ready before the heat goes on, and ingredients added in order from slowest-cooking to fastest.
fcs|15|l30-6|9-10|Making a Pan Sauce — A pan sauce turns the browned bits left after searing into a sauce in about three minutes: pour off fat, deglaze, reduce, and finish with butter off the heat.
fcs|15|l30-7|9-10|The Thermometer Decides — Color, time and touch are guesses; a food thermometer in the thickest part, away from bone, is the only way to know meat is safe and not overcooked.
fcs|15|l30-8|9-10|Resting Meat — Meat rests for a few minutes after cooking so the temperature finishes rising and the juices settle, and cutting too soon leaves those juices on the board.
fcs|15|l30-9|9-10|Cooking Rice and Other Grains — Grains cook by soaking up a measured amount of simmering water under a lid, so the ratio, the low heat and the closed lid matter more than anything else.
fcs|15|l30-10|9-10|Cooking Dried Beans — Dried beans need a soak, a long gentle simmer with salt, and, for kidney beans, a hard ten-minute boil first; done right they cost a fraction of canned.
fcs|16|l31-1|9-10|Carbohydrates and Fiber — Carbohydrates are the body's main fuel, and the fiber in whole grains, fruits, vegetables and beans slows that fuel down and keeps digestion moving.
fcs|16|l31-2|9-10|Proteins and Fats — Proteins build and repair the body, fats carry energy and certain vitamins, and both are needed every day in the right amounts.
fcs|16|l31-3|9-10|Vitamins, Minerals and Water — Vitamins and minerals give no calories but run the body's chemistry, and water, the sixth nutrient, is needed in larger amounts than any of the others.
fcs|16|l31-4|9-10|Five Groups on One Plate — MyPlate turns nutrition science into a picture: fruits and vegetables on half the plate, grains and protein foods on the other half, and dairy on the side.
fcs|16|l31-5|9-10|Portions and Servings — A serving is a standard amount printed on a label; a portion is what you actually put on the plate, and the two are often very different.
fcs|16|l31-6|9-10|Serving Size and Calories — Read a Nutrition Facts label from the top: the serving size and servings per container set the scale for every number below them.
fcs|16|l31-7|9-10|Percent Daily Value — The percent Daily Value column tells you whether a serving is high or low in a nutrient: 5% or less is low, 20% or more is high.
fcs|16|l31-8|9-10|Sodium, Added Sugars and Ingredients — The sodium and added sugars lines show the two things most packaged foods have too much of, and the ingredient list shows where they came from.
fcs|16|l31-9|9-10|Allergies and the Label — A food allergy can be life-threatening, and the law requires the nine major allergens to be named plainly on every packaged food label.
fcs|16|l31-10|9-10|Diabetes and Vegetarian Diets — Some people plan around a medical need and some around a belief or a choice; either way, the same six nutrients still have to show up.
fcs|16|l31-11|9-10|Water and What You Drink — Water is the everyday drink; sugary drinks are the largest single source of added sugar for teenagers, and the label tells you exactly how much.
fcs|16|l32-1|9-10|Start From What You Have — A meal plan starts in the cupboard, not in a cookbook: inventory first, then a calendar, then a menu that uses what is already paid for.
fcs|16|l32-2|9-10|Balance Across the Day — Check the whole day, not just dinner: across breakfast, lunch, dinner and snacks, all five food groups should show up in roughly MyPlate shares.
fcs|16|l32-3|9-10|Cook Once, Eat Twice — Cooking a double batch or a big base ingredient on a free day turns one hour at the stove into two or three meals.
fcs|16|l32-4|9-10|A List by Store Section — A grocery list written in the order of the store's sections is faster to shop, harder to forget things from, and easier to stick to.
fcs|16|l32-5|9-10|Unit Price and Store Brands — Compare foods by price per ounce or per pound, not by the price on the package, and try the store brand where the ingredients match.
fcs|16|l32-6|9-10|Sales, Coupons and Seasons — Sales, coupons and seasonal produce lower the bill only when they are for foods already in the plan, or foods the plan can be bent to use.
fcs|16|l32-7|9-10|A Sample Weekly Food Budget — A food budget divides a fixed amount across the week's meals in advance, so the receipt is a check on the plan instead of a surprise.
fcs|16|l32-8|9-10|Stretching the Dollar — The cheapest nutritious foods in the store are dried beans, rice, oats, eggs, frozen vegetables, bananas and whole chickens; build around them and the budget stretches.
fcs|16|l32-9|9-10|Storing Food Safely — Where and how food is stored decides whether the money spent on it is eaten or thrown away: cold food at or below 40 °F, frozen at 0 °F, and raw meat on the bottom shelf.
fcs|16|l32-10|9-10|Eating Well With Little Time — On a night with fifteen minutes, a stocked pantry, a few frozen staples and three fast patterns beat takeout on cost, nutrition and often on time.
fcs|16|l32-11|9-10|Cutting Food Waste — Most household food waste comes from buying too much, storing it wrong and forgetting it; a plan, a first-in-first-out fridge and a Friday clean-out soup fix most of it.
fcs|16|l32-12|9-10|Watch a Weeknight Meal in 3D — Watching a stir-fry and a pasta dinner in 3D shows how prep, allergens and cooking once to eat twice fit into a week's plan.
fcs|17|l33-1|11-12|Why an Egg Sets — Heat unfolds an egg's coiled proteins and then links them into a solid web, which is why a clear runny white turns firm and opaque.
fcs|17|l33-2|11-12|Custards, Scrambles and Gentle Heat — Egg dishes set over gentle heat and keep cooking after they leave the pan, so the cook pulls them early and never lets a custard boil.
fcs|17|l33-3|11-12|Whipping Air into Whites — Beating unfolds egg white proteins without heat, and the unfolded proteins wrap around air bubbles to make a foam that fat will collapse.
fcs|17|l33-4|11-12|What Gluten Is — Gluten is a stretchy protein web that forms when two wheat proteins meet water and get worked, and different flours build different amounts of it.
fcs|17|l33-5|11-12|The Muffin Method — Quick breads are mixed wet into dry with as few strokes as possible, because every extra stroke builds gluten and turns a tender crumb into tunnels and peaks.
fcs|17|l33-6|11-12|When You Want Gluten — Bread dough is kneaded on purpose to line up a strong gluten web that can trap yeast gas, and the windowpane test shows when the web is ready.
fcs|17|l33-7|11-12|Thickening a Sauce — Starch granules swell and burst in hot liquid and their released strands tangle into a thickened sauce, which is why a roux or slurry must be handled in a set order.
fcs|17|l33-8|11-12|Soda, Powder and Acid — Baking soda needs an acid in the recipe to make gas, while baking powder carries its own acid, so swapping one for the other changes the rise and the taste.
fcs|17|l33-9|11-12|Yeast and Steam — Yeast is a living leavener that needs warmth, food and time, and steam is the leavener hiding in every oven that lifts popovers and puff pastry.
fcs|17|l33-10|11-12|Maillard and Caramelization — Browning is two different reactions, proteins plus sugars in the Maillard reaction and sugar alone in caramelization, and both need a dry, hot surface.
fcs|17|l33-11|11-12|Acids and Bases in the Kitchen — Acids and bases change color, texture and browning in food, and the pH scale is the cook's way of knowing which is which.
fcs|17|l33-12|11-12|Watch Gluten and Eggs in 3D — Watching kneading, muffin mixing and scrambled eggs in 3D shows protein chemistry decide the texture of real food.
fcs|17|l34-1|11-12|Oil and Water Will Not Mix — An emulsion is oil and water forced into tiny droplets and held together by an emulsifier, and without one the two separate again in minutes.
fcs|17|l34-2|11-12|A Vinaigrette by Ratio — A vinaigrette is three parts oil to one part acid whisked together with a little emulsifier, and the ratio matters more than the recipe.
fcs|17|l34-3|11-12|Mayonnaise and Why It Breaks — Mayonnaise is a permanent emulsion built by adding oil to yolk drop by drop, and it breaks when the oil comes too fast, too cold or too much for the yolk to coat.
fcs|17|l34-4|11-12|How Fat Carries Flavor — Fat dissolves and carries many flavor compounds, coats the tongue, and can reach browning temperatures that water cannot, which is why it changes both taste and texture.
fcs|17|l34-5|11-12|Solid and Liquid Fats in Baking — Whether a fat is solid or liquid at room temperature decides how it behaves in a dough, so butter that is creamed, cut in or melted gives three different results.
fcs|17|l34-6|11-12|Sugar's Many Jobs — Sugar sweetens, but it also browns, holds moisture, tenderizes, feeds yeast, stabilizes foams and preserves, so cutting it changes far more than taste.
fcs|17|l34-7|11-12|One Variable at a Time — A fair kitchen experiment changes exactly one thing, keeps everything else the same, and compares the result to a control batch made by the original recipe.
fcs|17|l34-8|11-12|Measuring and Recording — An experiment is only as good as its measurements, so weigh the dough, use a ruler and a thermometer, record every number in a table, and repeat.
fcs|17|l34-9|11-12|Writing It Up — A write-up states the question, the hypothesis, the exact method, the results as numbers, and an honest conclusion about what the data can and cannot say.
fcs|17|l34-10|11-12|Freezing and Drying — Freezing at 0 °F stops microbes by locking up water, drying removes the water entirely, and both depend on airtight packaging, labels and safe thawing.
fcs|17|l34-11|11-12|Canning Safely — High-acid foods can be canned in a boiling-water bath, but low-acid foods must go in a pressure canner because only that heat kills botulism spores.
fcs|17|l34-12|11-12|Watch an Emulsion Form — Watching a vinaigrette whisked together in 3D shows how droplets, an emulsifier and time decide whether an emulsion holds.
fcs|18|l35-1|11-12|Physical Development — Physical development runs from head to toe and from the center of the body outward, on a range that is wide but predictable.
fcs|18|l35-2|11-12|Cognitive Development — Cognitive development is how a child learns to think, remember, solve problems and understand that the world keeps existing when they cannot see it.
fcs|18|l35-3|11-12|Social and Emotional Growth — Social and emotional development begins with attachment to a caregiver who responds, and grows into the ability to name feelings, wait, share and make friends.
fcs|18|l35-4|11-12|The First Year — In the first year a baby goes from reflexes to walking and from crying to first words, and a caregiver's job is to respond, protect and talk.
fcs|18|l35-5|11-12|Toddlers, One to Three — Toddlers are driven to do it themselves, and the caregiver's job is to make the home safe enough to let them, and to set a few firm, calm limits.
fcs|18|l35-6|11-12|Preschool, Three to Five — Preschoolers learn through play and questions, and they need adults who answer honestly, set up turns and let them do real jobs.
fcs|18|l35-7|11-12|School Age, Six to Twelve — School-age children build competence and friendships, and they need adults who notice effort, keep rules fair and stay available as the peer world grows.
fcs|18|l35-8|11-12|Play Is the Work — Play is how children practice every domain at once, and the best play materials are open-ended, safe for the age and matched to the stage.
fcs|18|l35-9|11-12|How Language Grows — Language grows from being talked to, and its milestones, from cooing to sentences, are the ones most worth watching because they are easiest to help early.
fcs|18|l35-10|11-12|Every Child, Their Own Path — Every child develops differently, including children with disabilities; the caregiver's job is to know the ranges, watch for the real warning signs and get help early without shame.
fcs|18|l36-1|11-12|Guidance, Not Punishment — Guidance teaches a child what to do; punishment only teaches what to avoid when an adult is watching, and hitting a child teaches that hitting works.
fcs|18|l36-2|11-12|Routines and Choices — A predictable routine prevents most battles, and a choice between two acceptable options gives a child real power inside the adult's limit.
fcs|18|l36-3|11-12|Redirection and Consequences — Redirect a young child toward what is allowed, and let older children learn from consequences that follow naturally or logically from what they did.
fcs|18|l36-4|11-12|Safe Sleep — Every sleep, every time: on the back, on a firm flat surface, alone in the sleep space with nothing else in it.
fcs|18|l36-5|11-12|Childproofing Room by Room — Get down to a toddler's eye level and remove or lock what can burn, poison, shock, drown, cut or crush; the home does the supervising you cannot do every second.
fcs|18|l36-6|11-12|Car Seats by Stage — A child rides rear-facing as long as the seat allows, then forward-facing with a harness, then in a booster until the seat belt fits, and always in the back seat until at least 13.
fcs|18|l36-7|11-12|Choking: Prevent It, Then Act — Cut round foods, keep small objects away from children under 3, and know the back blows and thrusts for a choking child who cannot breathe or cough.
fcs|18|l36-8|11-12|Before the Parents Leave — The information a sitter gathers in the first ten minutes, numbers, allergies, routines and rules, is what makes the next four hours safe.
fcs|18|l36-9|11-12|Activities by Age — A good sitter matches the activity to the child's stage, keeps it simple, and puts the phone away.
fcs|18|l36-10|11-12|Recognizing and Reporting Abuse — Anyone who cares for children learns the signs of abuse and neglect and knows that reporting a reasonable concern is the way a child gets help.
fcs|19|l37-1|11-12|Gross Pay and Net Pay — Gross pay is what you earned; net pay is what reaches your account after withholding, and every plan has to start from net.
fcs|19|l37-2|11-12|Where the Withholding Goes — Federal and state income tax are prepayments on a yearly bill, while Social Security and Medicare withholding, together called FICA, are fixed shares of every paycheck.
fcs|19|l37-3|11-12|Checking the Stub for Errors — Payroll makes mistakes, so check hours, rate and deductions against your own records every pay period and raise a problem the same week.
fcs|19|l37-4|11-12|Start From Take-Home Pay — A budget is a monthly plan that assigns every dollar of net pay to a category before the month starts, so spending follows a decision instead of a mood.
fcs|19|l37-5|11-12|Needs, Wants and Savings — Sorting every expense into needs, wants and savings, with 50/30/20 as one starting split, shows where a budget can bend and where it cannot.
fcs|19|l37-6|11-12|The Emergency Fund — An emergency fund is cash set aside for surprises, built to a starter goal first and then toward a few months of expenses, and kept where it is easy to reach but not easy to spend.
fcs|19|l37-7|11-12|Debit Against Credit — A debit card spends money you already have; a credit card borrows money you must pay back, and the two cards behave differently when things go wrong.
fcs|19|l37-8|11-12|APR and the Minimum Payment — APR is the yearly cost of a balance, charged month by month, and paying only the minimum keeps most of the balance and its interest alive for years.
fcs|19|l37-9|11-12|Compound Interest, Both Directions — Compound interest pays interest on interest, which makes savings grow faster over time and makes unpaid debt grow the same way against you.
fcs|19|l37-10|11-12|Credit Scores in Plain Terms — A credit score is a number built from your borrowing record, driven mostly by paying on time and keeping balances low, and it sets the price and the possibility of future borrowing and renting.
fcs|19|l38-1|11-12|Reading a Lease — A lease is a binding contract that names the term, the rent, the deposit and who pays for what, and you read every line before you sign because signing means you agreed to all of it.
fcs|19|l38-2|11-12|Deposits, Walk-Throughs and Repairs — Document the unit's condition with dated photos at move-in and move-out, put repair requests in writing, and know that landlord and tenant each owe the other specific things.
fcs|19|l38-3|11-12|Setting Up the Household — Utilities, renters insurance and first-month costs are part of an apartment's real price, so list every one with its number before you sign.
fcs|19|l38-4|11-12|Unit Price and the Shelf Label — The unit price, cost divided by amount, is the only fair way to compare sizes and brands, and the small shelf label already does the division for you.
fcs|19|l38-5|11-12|Warranties, Receipts and Returns — Before buying anything that costs real money, know the return window, keep the receipt, and read what the warranty actually covers and for how long.
fcs|19|l38-6|11-12|The Big Purchase — For anything expensive, compare at least three options on total cost including fees, financing and running costs, and never decide on the day you first see it.
fcs|19|l38-7|11-12|Four Signs of a Scam — Nearly every scam relies on urgency, secrecy, an untraceable payment like gift cards or wire transfer, and an offer too good to be true; any one of them is a reason to stop.
fcs|19|l38-8|11-12|Identity Theft and Passwords — Identity theft starts with a few pieces of personal information, so guard your Social Security number, use a unique password with two-factor login on every money account, and check your credit report yearly.
fcs|19|l38-9|11-12|Your Rights and Where to Complain — Consumers have written rights to accurate information, safe products, fair billing and a place to complain, and a calm written record is what turns a complaint into a refund.
fcs|2|l3-1|k-2|Ask Before You Touch — Hot has no color, so ask a grown-up before you touch anything in the kitchen.
fcs|2|l3-2|k-2|Steam Is Hot Too — Steam is hot water in the air, and it can burn just like a hot pan.
fcs|2|l3-3|k-2|Mitts and Pot Holders — An oven mitt or pot holder goes between your hand and anything hot.
fcs|2|l3-4|k-2|Handles Turned In — Pot handles point in toward the back of the stove so nobody can bump them.
fcs|2|l3-5|k-2|Cold Can Hurt Too — Very cold things like ice and freezer shelves can hurt your skin, so hold them briefly.
fcs|2|l3-6|k-2|A Small Burn: Cool Water — For a small burn, run cool water on it and tell a grown-up right away.
fcs|2|l4-1|k-2|A Tool Has a Job — Knives, scissors and forks are tools with a job, and tools are never toys.
fcs|2|l4-2|k-2|Closed and Pointed Down — Carry scissors closed, in your fist, with the point down, and walk.
fcs|2|l4-3|k-2|Handle First — Hand a sharp tool to someone handle-first so their hand never touches the sharp part.
fcs|2|l4-4|k-2|Let It Fall — Never try to catch a falling knife; step back and let it fall.
fcs|2|l4-5|k-2|Butter Knife and Board — Kids can cut soft food with a butter knife on a cutting board, with a grown-up nearby.
fcs|2|l4-6|k-2|Grown-Up Jobs — Broken glass, matches and lighters are for grown-ups only; you tell, you do not touch.

fcs|20|l39-1|11-12|Pick a Project You Can Finish — A good capstone project is one you can plan, afford, complete on time and evaluate against a clear standard.
fcs|20|l39-2|11-12|Writing a Specification — A specification describes the finished product in measurable terms so anyone can tell whether the result meets it.
fcs|20|l39-3|11-12|Know Your Audience — The people you serve or sew for shape the spec: their number, ages, allergies, food rules, sizes and tastes come before your own preferences.
fcs|20|l39-4|11-12|Every Ingredient, Every Material — A cost sheet lists every single item the project uses, with its unit price and the amount needed, so the total is real and not a guess.
fcs|20|l39-5|11-12|Scaling Recipes to the Head Count — To scale a recipe, divide the servings you need by the servings it makes to get a factor, then multiply every ingredient by that factor and convert to sensible units.
fcs|20|l39-6|11-12|Hidden Costs and the Budget Line — Beyond ingredients or fabric, a real cost sheet includes supplies, equipment you must buy or rent, a waste allowance and a cushion, compared against the budget you were given.
fcs|20|l39-7|11-12|Working Backwards From the Deadline — Build the timeline from the finish time backwards, giving each task its real duration, so that the longest and earliest jobs start first.
fcs|20|l39-8|11-12|The Materials and Equipment List — List every tool and piece of equipment for every step, check that you have it or can borrow it, and stage it before you start.
fcs|20|l39-9|11-12|The Safety Plan — A safety plan writes down the standard temperatures, times and rules for the project before the day, so that safety decisions are already made.
fcs|20|l39-10|11-12|Writing the Rubric First — A rubric written before the work names what a good result looks like at each level, so the finished project can be judged fairly against the spec.
fcs|20|l40-1|11-12|Running the Timeline — On the production day you follow the timeline you wrote, check the clock at every checkpoint and adjust the plan, not the safety rules, when you fall behind.
fcs|20|l40-2|11-12|Documenting as You Go — Photos, times, temperatures and short notes taken during the work are the evidence you will need to evaluate the project and present it.
fcs|20|l40-3|11-12|Serving and Finishing — The final steps of plating and serving, or pressing and finishing, decide how the work is received, and they follow rules of temperature, timing and care like every earlier step.
fcs|20|l40-4|11-12|Scoring Against the Spec — Evaluation means putting the finished result beside the spec and the rubric line by line and recording what met, what did not and by how much.
fcs|20|l40-5|11-12|Cost and Time, Plan Against Actual — Compare the planned cost sheet and timeline with what really happened, find every difference and name its cause.
fcs|20|l40-6|11-12|What I Would Change — The most valuable page of the evaluation is a short, specific list of what you would do differently, each tied to a cause you found in the evidence.
fcs|20|l40-7|11-12|Presenting to an Audience — A project presentation walks the audience from the spec to the result to the changes in a set order, with evidence on every slide or page and the product itself in the room.
fcs|20|l40-8|11-12|Careers in Food and Nutrition — Culinary and dietetics careers use exactly the planning, costing, safety and evaluation skills of the capstone, at every level of education.
fcs|20|l40-9|11-12|Careers in Apparel, Children and Families — Apparel, early childhood, family services and consumer affairs careers each grow from one strand of the FCS course and use its planning and evaluation habits.
fcs|20|l40-10|11-12|Rehearse Your Project in 3D — A rehearsal in the 3D kitchen or sewing room lets you test your timeline and catch a missing step before production day.
