fcs|5|l9-1|3-5|Space, Not Weight — A measuring cup measures how much space food takes up, not how heavy it is.
fcs|5|l9-2|3-5|Dry Cups and the Liquid Cup — Dry ingredients go in the nested cups; liquids go in the clear cup with a spout.
fcs|5|l9-3|3-5|Why the Shapes Differ — Each cup is shaped for the job it does: a flat rim for leveling, or a spout and lines for pouring.
fcs|5|l9-4|3-5|Spoon In and Level — Spoon flour into the cup, then level it with the back of a knife; never scoop or pack.
fcs|5|l9-5|3-5|Packing Brown Sugar — Brown sugar is the one ingredient you press firmly into the cup, unless the recipe says otherwise.
fcs|5|l9-6|3-5|Teaspoon and Tablespoon — Measuring spoons come in a set; fill each one and level it, and never use a spoon from the drawer.
fcs|5|l9-7|3-5|Read It at Eye Level — Set the liquid cup on the counter and bend down to read the line at eye level.
fcs|5|l9-8|3-5|Three Spoons Make One — Three teaspoons equal one tablespoon, so you can measure either way when one spoon is missing.
fcs|5|l9-9|3-5|Sixteen Tablespoons — Sixteen tablespoons fill one cup, and four tablespoons fill a quarter cup.
fcs|5|l9-10|3-5|Find the Measuring Mistake — When a dish comes out wrong, walk back through each measurement to find the step that slipped.
fcs|5|l10-1|3-5|Title, Yield and Time — The top of a recipe tells you what you are making, how much it makes and how long it takes.
fcs|5|l10-2|3-5|The Ingredient List — The ingredient list names each food and its exact amount, in the order you will use it.
fcs|5|l10-3|3-5|The Steps in Order — Follow the numbered steps in order, because each one sets up the next.
fcs|5|l10-4|3-5|Read It All First — Read the whole recipe from top to bottom before you touch a single ingredient.
fcs|5|l10-5|3-5|Short Words for Long Ones — Recipes use short abbreviations for units, and mixing up tsp and Tbsp changes the dish.
fcs|5|l10-6|3-5|Set a Timer — Set a timer for every cooking time, and check the food when it rings instead of guessing.
fcs|5|l10-7|3-5|Doubling a Recipe — To double a recipe, multiply every ingredient by 2 and write the new amounts before you start.
fcs|5|l10-8|3-5|Halving a Recipe — To halve a recipe, divide every ingredient by 2, and use spoon equivalents for the tricky ones.
fcs|5|l10-9|3-5|Trail Mix, Times Four — To multiply a recipe by 4, multiply every line by 4 and trade up to the biggest tool that fits.
fcs|5|l10-10|3-5|No-Bake Bites, Step by Step — A no-bake recipe still needs every step in order, real measuring and a timer for chilling.

fcs|6|l11-1|3-5|Seven Tools in a Tin — A sewing kit holds a few small tools, and each one has one clear job.
fcs|6|l11-2|3-5|Sharp Rules for Needles and Pins — Needles and pins are sharp tools, so you count them, cover them and never put them in your mouth.
fcs|6|l11-3|3-5|Choosing the Thread — Pick thread that matches the color of the cloth and is strong enough for the job.
fcs|6|l11-4|3-5|Through the Eye — Cut the thread at a slant, hold the needle still, and push the thread through the eye in good light.
fcs|6|l11-5|3-5|The Anchor Knot — A knot at the end of the thread stops it from pulling through the cloth on the first stitch.
fcs|6|l11-6|3-5|Two Sides of Cloth — Cloth has a right side and a wrong side, and knots and messy ends belong on the wrong side.
fcs|6|l11-7|3-5|The Running Stitch — The running stitch goes in and out in a straight line and is the fastest stitch to learn.
fcs|6|l11-8|3-5|The Back Stitch — The back stitch goes one step back and two steps forward, which makes a seam that will not pull open.
fcs|6|l11-9|3-5|Tie It Off — End every line of sewing with a small knot on the wrong side, then snip the tail.
fcs|6|l12-1|3-5|Two Holes and Four — Buttons come with two holes or four holes, and you sew through the holes in a pattern that matches the other buttons.
fcs|6|l12-2|3-5|The Thread Shank — A shank is a short stem of thread under the button that leaves room for the cloth of the buttonhole.
fcs|6|l12-3|3-5|Sewing the Button On — Mark the spot, anchor the thread, stitch through the holes six times, wrap the shank, and tie off on the back.
fcs|6|l12-4|3-5|A Split Seam — A split seam is a seam where the old thread broke, and you fix it from the inside with a back stitch over the old stitch line.
fcs|6|l12-5|3-5|Pin It So It Matches — Before you mend, pin the two edges so they line up exactly, or the seam will pucker or twist.
fcs|6|l12-6|3-5|Make Clothes Last — Small habits, such as hanging, folding, washing gently and fixing early, make clothes last for years.
fcs|6|l12-7|3-5|A Felt Bookmark — A felt bookmark is a first project that uses cutting, a running stitch and a finishing knot.
fcs|6|l12-8|3-5|A Felt Pouch — Fold a rectangle of felt, back stitch the two sides, and you have a pouch that holds coins or pencils.
fcs|6|l12-9|3-5|Fix It or Toss It? — Mending saves money and keeps cloth out of the trash, so fixing usually beats throwing away.
fcs|6|l12-10|3-5|Watch a Button Go On — Watching a button being sewn in 3D shows where the needle goes and how the shank is made.
fcs|7|l13-1|3-5|Price and Amount — A price only makes sense when you also know how much you get for it.
fcs|7|l13-2|3-5|Divide to Find the Unit Price — Unit price is the price divided by the amount, and it lets you compare any two sizes.
fcs|7|l13-3|3-5|The Small Tag on the Shelf — Most stores print the unit price on the shelf tag, so you can compare without doing the division yourself.
fcs|7|l13-4|3-5|Is Bigger Always Cheaper? — Bigger packages are often, but not always, cheaper per unit, so check the tag every time.
fcs|7|l13-5|3-5|Brand Name or Store Brand — A store brand often has the same ingredients as a name brand at a lower unit price, so read both labels.
fcs|7|l13-6|3-5|Sales and Coupons — A sale or coupon only helps if you needed the item, and the sale price still has to beat the unit price of the other choices.
fcs|7|l13-7|3-5|Needs and Wants — A need is something you must have to live and stay healthy; a want is something nice to have, and telling them apart guides every purchase.
fcs|7|l13-8|3-5|Writing the List — A good shopping list starts at home, in the cupboard and the fridge, and is written in the order of the store.
fcs|7|l13-9|3-5|Sticking to the List — Stores are built to make you buy extra, and the list is your plan for walking past it.

fcs|7|l14-2|3-5|Counting What You Earned — Write down every job and every dollar, and subtract what the job cost you, so you know what you really made.
fcs|7|l14-3|3-5|A Want Now or a Goal Later — Every dollar spent on a small want today is a dollar that cannot go toward a bigger goal later.
fcs|7|l14-4|3-5|The Jar With a Picture — Saving works best when the money has one place to go and you can see the goal every day.
fcs|7|l14-5|3-5|How Long Until the Goal — Divide the goal by what you save each week to see how many weeks it will take.
fcs|7|l14-6|3-5|What a Bank Does — A bank keeps money safe, keeps a record of it, and lets you take it out when you need it.
fcs|7|l14-7|3-5|Interest, Money for Saving — A bank pays interest, a small amount added to your savings for leaving the money there.
fcs|7|l14-8|3-5|Spend, Save, Share — Splitting every dollar that comes in into spend, save and share gives each part of your money a job.
fcs|7|l14-9|3-5|A Budget From an Allowance — A budget is a written plan that gives every dollar coming in a place to go, and the parts must add up to the total.
fcs|7|l14-10|3-5|Giving to Others — Sharing money, things or time with others is part of a healthy money plan, and even a small gift counts.

fcs|8|l15-2|3-5|Check the Cupboard First — Look at what you already have before you write a shopping list.
fcs|8|l15-3|3-5|Write the List by Section — A shopping list grouped by store section is faster to shop and easier to stick to.

fcs|8|l15-5|3-5|Everything in Its Place — Before you turn on any heat, get out and prepare every ingredient and tool you will need.

fcs|8|l15-8|3-5|Washing Fruits and Vegetables — Rinse produce under cool running water and rub it with your hands, even if you will peel it.
fcs|8|l15-9|3-5|Read It All the Way Through — Read the whole recipe before you start so you know every tool, ingredient, time and step.

fcs|8|l16-2|3-5|Set the Timer — A timer remembers the minutes so you can do other work without burning anything.
fcs|8|l16-3|3-5|Scrambled Eggs — Scrambled eggs are whisked, cooked over medium-low heat and stirred until firm, with a grown-up at the stove.
fcs|8|l16-4|3-5|A Quesadilla in a Pan — A quesadilla is a tortilla with cheese, cooked on medium heat until the cheese melts and the outside is golden.
fcs|8|l16-5|3-5|Pasta in a Big Pot — Pasta cooks in a large pot of boiling salted water, timed by the box, and is drained in a colander by a grown-up.
fcs|8|l16-6|3-5|Clear While It Cooks — Use every waiting minute to put things away, so cleanup after the meal is small.
fcs|8|l16-7|3-5|Glasses, Plates, Then Pots — Wash dishes from cleanest to dirtiest: glasses, then plates and silverware, then pots and pans.
fcs|8|l16-8|3-5|Wipe the Counter and the Stove — Wipe counters, stovetop and table with a clean cloth and warm soapy water once the dishes are done and the stove is cool.
fcs|8|l16-9|3-5|Leftovers Within Two Hours — Put leftovers in the refrigerator within two hours of cooking, in shallow containers with the date on them.

fcs|9|l17-1|6-8|Germs That Grow in Food — Bacteria you cannot see, taste or smell grow in food and cause most foodborne illness.
fcs|9|l17-2|6-8|The Danger Zone — Bacteria grow fastest between 40 and 140 °F, so keep cold food cold and hot food hot.
fcs|9|l17-3|6-8|The Two-Hour Rule — Perishable food may sit out two hours at most, or one hour when the air is above 90 °F.
fcs|9|l17-4|6-8|Hands and Surfaces — Wash hands for twenty seconds with soap and water before, during and after handling food.
fcs|9|l17-5|6-8|Produce and Dishes — Rinse produce under running water and wash dishes hot, soapy and in order.
fcs|9|l17-6|6-8|Cross-Contamination — Cross-contamination moves bacteria from raw food to ready-to-eat food; separate keeps them apart.
fcs|9|l17-7|6-8|Boards, Plates and Fridge Order — Use separate boards and plates for raw meat, and store raw meat on the bottom shelf.
fcs|9|l17-8|6-8|The Food Thermometer — Only a thermometer in the thickest part of the food can tell you it has reached a safe temperature.
fcs|9|l17-9|6-8|Safe Minimum Temperatures — Every food has a safe minimum internal temperature; poultry is 165 °F, ground meat 160 °F, whole cuts and fish 145 °F.
fcs|9|l17-10|6-8|Thawing Safely — Thaw frozen food in the fridge, in cold water changed every 30 minutes, or in the microwave, never on the counter.
fcs|9|l17-11|6-8|Cooling and Storing Leftovers — Get leftovers into the fridge within two hours, in shallow containers, and use them within about three to four days.
fcs|9|l18-1|6-8|Burns and Scalds — Most kitchen burns come from steam, hot handles and splashing oil, and every one of them is preventable.
fcs|9|l18-2|6-8|Cuts and Falls — A sharp knife, a steady board and a dry floor prevent most cuts and falls.
fcs|9|l18-3|6-8|Electric Shock and Cords — Water and electricity never mix; keep cords dry, short and unplugged before you reach in.
fcs|9|l18-4|6-8|The Grease Fire — For a grease fire: turn off the heat, slide a lid over the pan, and never use water.
fcs|9|l18-5|6-8|PASS and the Extinguisher — Use a fire extinguisher with PASS: pull, aim at the base, squeeze, sweep, with your back to the exit.
fcs|9|l18-6|6-8|Oven, Range and Appliances — Never leave the stove unattended, keep the area clear, and know how to shut off every appliance.
fcs|9|l18-7|6-8|Treating a Cut — For a cut: wash your hands, press with a clean cloth, rinse, bandage, and get help if it will not stop bleeding.
fcs|9|l18-8|6-8|Treating a Burn — Cool a burn under cool running water, cover it loosely, and never put ice, butter or ointment on it.
fcs|9|l18-9|6-8|When to Call 911 — Call 911 for fire, trouble breathing, heavy bleeding, a large burn, or anyone unconscious; know the address before you dial.
