spt|6|l12-9|3-5|Women Shut Out, Then Welcomed — The FA banned women's matches from its members' grounds in 1921, lifted the ban in 1971, and FIFA held the first Women's World Cup in 1991.
spt|6|l12-10|3-5|IHSA Soccer, 1972 and 1988 — The IHSA crowned its first boys soccer state champion in 1972 and its first girls soccer state champion in 1988.
spt|6|l12-11|3-5|The World Comes to Chicago — The 1994 World Cup opened at Soldier Field in Chicago, and the Chicago Fire club was founded in 1997.
spt|7|l13-1|3-5|A Straight Race at Olympia — The oldest known Olympic race was one length of a straight track called a stade.
spt|7|l13-2|3-5|Who Could Run at Olympia — Only free Greek men and boys could compete at the ancient Olympic Games.
spt|7|l13-3|3-5|Yards, Miles and Meters — Older American and British tracks used a 440-yard lap; the world later settled on 400 meters.
spt|7|l13-4|3-5|The World's Track Rules — A world body, now called World Athletics, writes the rules that make every standard track the same.
spt|7|l13-5|3-5|Why Starts Are Staggered — Staggered starting marks make every lane the same distance in a race that goes around a curve.
spt|7|l13-6|3-5|From Cinders to Rubber — Tracks changed from dirt and cinders to all-weather rubber surfaces, first used at an Olympics in 1968.
spt|7|l13-7|3-5|The Hand-Held Stopwatch — Stopwatches let officials write down a race as a time, so meets on different days could be compared.
spt|7|l13-8|3-5|The Photo Finish — Cameras and electronic clocks now decide close races and record times to the hundredth of a second.
spt|7|l13-9|3-5|Illinois Track Since 1893 — IHSA records list state track champions from 1893, before the IHSA was founded in 1900.
spt|7|l14-1|3-5|Races in Open Water — The first modern Olympic swimming races were held in open water; a built pool came in 1908.
spt|7|l14-2|3-5|Short Course and Long Course — Swimming records are kept separately for short-course pools and 50-meter long-course pools.
spt|7|l14-3|3-5|Lane Lines and Walls — Floating lane lines and painted lines on the pool floor give each swimmer a fair, marked path.
spt|7|l14-4|3-5|Real Tennis Indoors — Long before lawn tennis, people played an older game, now called real tennis, in walled indoor courts.
spt|7|l14-5|3-5|Wingfield's Boxed Game — Walter Clopton Wingfield introduced a lawn tennis game in 1873 and patented it in 1874.
spt|7|l14-6|3-5|Wimbledon, 1877 — The first Wimbledon tournament in 1877 used a rectangle court that became the standard.
spt|7|l14-7|3-5|IHSA Swimming Titles — The IHSA crowned its first boys swimming champions in 1932 and its first girls swimming champions in 1975.
spt|7|l14-8|3-5|IHSA Tennis Titles — The IHSA crowned its first boys tennis champion in 1912 and its first girls tennis champion in 1972.
spt|8|l15-1|6-8|The Pythians and the 1867 Convention — In 1867 the national baseball association voted to keep out clubs with Black players, the first written step toward the color line.
spt|8|l15-2|6-8|The Walker Brothers in 1884 — In 1884 Moses Fleetwood Walker played in a major league for Toledo, decades before 1947.
spt|8|l15-3|6-8|The International League Vote — In July 1887 a leading minor league voted to approve no new contracts with Black players, and other leagues soon followed.
spt|8|l15-4|6-8|An Unwritten Rule Held for Decades — No major league rule book barred Black players, yet owners kept every roster white from the 1890s to 1947.
spt|8|l15-5|6-8|Traveling Teams and Barnstorming — Shut out of organized leagues, Black professionals formed independent teams that traveled and played wherever they could book a game.
spt|8|l15-6|6-8|Rube Foster Founds a League — In February 1920 Rube Foster founded the Negro National League, giving Black baseball a constitution, a schedule and standings.
spt|8|l15-7|6-8|A Business and a Showcase — Negro Leagues clubs were businesses run largely by Black owners, and the East-West All-Star Game in Chicago became their biggest event.
spt|8|l15-8|6-8|Effa Manley and the Front Office — Newark Eagles co-owner Effa Manley ran a winning club and in 2006 became the first woman elected to the Baseball Hall of Fame.
spt|8|l15-9|6-8|1947: Robinson and Doby — Jackie Robinson crossed the National League color line on April 15, 1947, and Larry Doby crossed the American League line that July.
spt|8|l15-10|6-8|Twelve Years to Reach Every Team — Integration came team by team, the last major league club integrated in 1959, and the Negro Leagues slowly lost their players and fans.
spt|8|l15-11|6-8|Putting the Records Back — In 2020 Major League Baseball recognized seven Negro Leagues from 1920 to 1948 as major leagues and later added their statistics to its records.
spt|8|l16-1|6-8|Gentlemen and Mechanics in England — Nineteenth-century English sports clubs wrote amateur rules that shut out people who worked with their hands for wages.
spt|8|l16-2|6-8|Amateur Bodies in America — In the United States, the AAU (1888) and the NCAA (1906) took charge of amateur sport and wrote rules about who could compete.
spt|8|l16-3|6-8|Coubertin's Congress of 1894 — The 1894 congress in Paris that founded the International Olympic Committee made amateurism a rule of the modern Games.
spt|8|l16-4|6-8|Jim Thorpe's Medals — Jim Thorpe won two Olympic gold medals in 1912, lost them in 1913 over minor-league baseball pay, and was fully restored as sole champion in 2022.
spt|8|l16-5|6-8|The End of the Amateur Rule — The IOC removed the word amateur from its eligibility rule in 1974 and let professionals in sport by sport from the late 1980s.
spt|8|l16-6|6-8|Pro Football Closes and Reopens — Black players were in the early NFL, were shut out from 1934 to 1945, and returned in 1946 when a public stadium lease forced the question.
spt|8|l16-7|6-8|Basketball, Hockey and Golf — Black players entered the NBA in 1950 and the NHL in 1958, while the PGA kept a written whites-only clause from 1934 to 1961.
spt|8|l16-8|6-8|Althea Gibson and Tennis — Althea Gibson broke tennis's color line at the U.S. Nationals in 1950 and won Wimbledon in 1957.
spt|8|l16-9|6-8|Women Enter the Olympics Slowly — Women first competed at the Olympics in 1900, but many events stayed closed to them for decades.
spt|8|l16-10|6-8|Boston 1967 and Little League 1974 — Women ran the Boston Marathon before it allowed them in 1972, and a New Jersey case led Little League to admit girls in 1974.
spt|9|l17-1|6-8|Bare Knuckles and the London Rules — In the 1800s prizefighting was fought with bare fists under the London Prize Ring Rules and was illegal in most places.
spt|9|l17-2|6-8|Sullivan, Kilrain and the Last Bare-Knuckle Title — The 1889 Sullivan–Kilrain fight in Mississippi was the last heavyweight title fight with bare knuckles.
spt|9|l17-3|6-8|Gloves and the 1892 Title Fight — In 1892 the heavyweight title changed hands in a gloved fight under the Queensberry rules in New Orleans.
spt|9|l17-4|6-8|The Sports Page and the Police Gazette — In the late 1800s newspapers and the Police Gazette used boxing to sell papers and even gave out championship belts.
spt|9|l17-5|6-8|Jack Johnson and the Color Line — Jack Johnson became the first Black heavyweight champion in 1908, and his victories set off a public backlash.
spt|9|l17-6|6-8|A Law Against Fight Films — In 1912 Congress banned carrying prizefight films across state lines, a law that stood until 1940.
spt|9|l17-7|6-8|New York's Athletic Commission — The Walker Law of 1920 made boxing legal in New York under a state athletic commission that licensed fighters and set rules.
spt|9|l17-8|6-8|Boxing Goes on the Air — Radio carried the 1921 Dempsey–Carpentier fight to a huge audience and made heavyweight fights national events heard live.
spt|9|l17-9|6-8|The Long Count at Soldier Field — The 1927 Dempsey–Tunney rematch in Chicago drew more than 100,000 people and set off a long public argument over one count.
spt|9|l17-10|6-8|Golden Gloves and Chicago Youth Boxing — In Chicago, newspapers and the Catholic Youth Organization ran amateur boxing for young people outside the school leagues.
spt|9|l17-11|6-8|Joe Louis and Two Famous Nights — Joe Louis won the heavyweight title in Chicago in 1937, and his 1938 win over Max Schmeling was heard on radio around the world.
spt|9|l17-12|6-8|Why School Leagues Kept Boxing Out — Illinois school sport under the IHSA never took boxing in, and the NCAA dropped college boxing after a death in 1960.
spt|9|l18-1|6-8|Hull House on Halsted Street — Jane Addams and Ellen Gates Starr opened Hull House in Chicago in 1889, and its gym gave immigrant neighbors indoor space to play.
spt|9|l18-2|6-8|A Movement from London — The settlement idea began at Toynbee Hall in London in 1884 and spread to American cities such as Chicago and New York.
spt|9|l18-3|6-8|Jane Addams on Youth and Play — In 1909 Jane Addams argued that cities must give young people safe places for play instead of leaving them to the streets.
spt|9|l18-4|6-8|Chicago's Park Field Houses — In 1905 Chicago's South Park Commission opened neighborhood parks with field houses that held gyms, baths and meeting rooms.
spt|9|l18-5|6-8|The Playground Movement — The Playground Association of America, founded in 1906, pushed cities to build public playgrounds and hire people to run them.
spt|9|l18-6|6-8|New York's School League — In 1903 Luther Gulick helped found New York's Public Schools Athletic League, which brought basketball into city school sport.
spt|9|l18-7|6-8|Black Ys and E. B. Henderson — Shut out of many white Y branches, Black communities built their own Ys, and Edwin B. Henderson brought organized basketball to Black students in Washington.
spt|9|l18-8|6-8|The Rens and the Ballroom Court — The New York Renaissance, founded in 1923 by Bob Douglas, played in a Harlem ballroom and won the first world pro tournament in 1939.
spt|9|l18-9|6-8|Neighborhood Teams Go Pro — Community teams like the SPHAS in Philadelphia and the Globetrotters from Chicago grew out of neighborhood clubs and halls into pro teams.
spt|9|l18-10|6-8|College Nights at the Garden — In the 1930s promoter Ned Irish filled Madison Square Garden with college doubleheaders, and the NIT began there in 1938.
spt|9|l18-11|6-8|Outdoor Courts and the Chain Net — Outdoor city courts with metal backboards and chain nets became free public rooms where players built their own leagues and reputations.
skt|1|l1-1|k-2|Surfers in California — Surfers in California wanted to surf even when the ocean had no waves.
skt|1|l1-2|k-2|Wheels Under a Board — People made the first skateboards by putting roller-skate wheels under a wooden board.
skt|1|l1-3|k-2|Sidewalk Surfing — People called the new ride sidewalk surfing because it felt like surfing on land.
skt|1|l1-4|k-2|Bumpy Old Wheels — Early skateboard wheels were hard and bumpy, so small stones could stop them.
skt|1|l1-5|k-2|Boards in Stores — Around 1959, stores began to sell skateboards that were already made.
skt|1|l1-6|k-2|Around the World — Today people skateboard in countries all over the world.
skt|1|l1-7|k-2|Then and Now — Skateboards changed a lot, but riders still lean, roll and balance.
skt|1|l2-1|k-2|The Deck — The deck is the board you stand on.
skt|1|l2-2|k-2|Nose and Tail — The nose is the front end of the deck, and the tail is the back end.
skt|1|l2-3|k-2|Rough Grip Tape — Grip tape is rough like sandpaper so your shoes do not slip.
skt|1|l2-4|k-2|The Trucks — Trucks are the metal parts that hold the wheels and help the board turn.
skt|1|l2-5|k-2|Four Wheels — A skateboard rolls on four wheels, two on each truck.
skt|1|l2-6|k-2|Bearings Help Wheels Spin — Bearings are small rings inside the wheels that help them spin.
skt|1|l2-7|k-2|Counting the Parts — Each part has a job, and a board needs all of them to ride safely.
skt|10|l19-1|9-10|From Photos to Moving Pictures — In the 1980s, home video let skaters watch a whole trick from start to finish, so new tricks spread much faster than through photos alone.
skt|10|l19-2|9-10|Video Days, 1991 — Blind's Video Days (1991) showed loose, creative street skating and became one of the most copied skate videos ever made.
skt|10|l19-3|9-10|Questionable and the Video Race — After Questionable (1992), companies raced to make videos with harder tricks, and new video magazines spread the newest tricks even faster.
skt|10|l19-4|9-10|Small Wheels — Street skaters in the early 1990s chose small, hard wheels because they made boards lighter and lower for flip tricks, even though they rolled worse over cracks.
skt|10|l19-5|9-10|The Double-Kick Deck — In the early 1990s, decks became narrower with a nose and a tail of almost the same shape, so skaters could do tricks rolling either direction.
skt|10|l19-6|9-10|Riding Fakie and Switch — Fakie means rolling backward in your normal stance, and switch means riding with your other foot in front; street skaters of the 1990s used both.
skt|10|l19-7|9-10|Ledges, Curbs and Plazas — In the 1990s, concrete ledges, curbs and open plazas became the main spots for street skating, and plazas like the Embarcadero in San Francisco became famous on film.
skt|10|l19-8|9-10|Rails and Stairs on Film — Handrails and stairs became symbols of 1990s street skating on film, but they are pro-level and dangerous; learners practice on low flat bars and curbs at a skatepark.
skt|10|l19-9|9-10|The Skate Shop as Home Base — In the 1990s, local skate shops were community centers where skaters bought gear, watched new videos, found local spots and joined shop teams.
skt|10|l19-10|9-10|Bans and a Slogan — As street skating grew, many cities banned skateboarding in downtowns and plazas, and skaters answered with the slogan Skateboarding Is Not a Crime.
skt|10|l19-11|9-10|Gonzales and Kaupas — Mark Gonzales and Natas Kaupas helped invent modern street skating in the 1980s by treating walls, benches, rails and even fire hydrants as things to skate.
skt|10|l20-1|9-10|The Extreme Games, 1995 — In 1995, ESPN held the Extreme Games in Rhode Island; renamed the X Games in 1996, the event put skateboarding on television around the world.
skt|10|l20-2|9-10|How Contests Are Scored — In most skateboarding contests, judges score short timed runs on difficulty, variety, height and how cleanly tricks land; a Best Trick contest rewards one landed trick.
skt|10|l20-3|9-10|Tony Hawk's 900 — At the 1999 X Games in San Francisco, Tony Hawk landed the first 900, two and a half spins in the air on a vert ramp, after many tries past the time limit.
skt|10|l20-4|9-10|The Physics of a Big Spin — Higher airs give more time to spin: a 1.5 m air lasts about 1.1 s, so a 900 needs an average of about 810 degrees per second.
skt|10|l20-5|9-10|After the 900 — After 1999, skaters kept raising the limit, with giant mega ramps in the 2000s and the first 1080 by a 12-year-old in 2012; these are pro-level feats, not things to try.
skt|10|l20-6|9-10|Tony Hawk's Pro Skater — The video game Tony Hawk's Pro Skater (1999) taught millions of players skate trick names and spots, and Elissa Steamer was its first woman skater.
skt|10|l20-7|9-10|The Boom of the Late 1990s — By around 2000, skateboarding was a large business, with skate shoes and clothing sold worldwide, and new foundations helped build public skateparks.
skt|10|l20-8|9-10|Women Who Led the Way — Women skated from the start, from Patti McGee in 1965 to Peggy Oki and Cindy Whitehead in the 1970s, even when magazines and contests gave them little space.
skt|10|l20-9|9-10|Steamer and Equal Prize Money — Elissa Steamer became a leading pro street skater in the 1990s, and in the 2000s women skaters organized and won equal prize money at the X Games.
skt|10|l20-10|9-10|Skaters of Color — Skaters of color shaped skateboarding from the 1970s on, from Tony Alva on the Zephyr team to Ray Barbee, Stevie Williams and Daewon Song in the street era.
skt|10|l20-11|9-10|New Doors Around the World — In the 2000s, wheelchair skating and programs like Skateistan in Kabul showed skateboarding could welcome people of every body and every country.
skt|11|l21-1|9-10|Grinds, Slides and a Safe Start — A grind rides on the trucks and a slide rides on the deck, and both start on something low with your gear on.
skt|11|l21-2|9-10|Wax on the Edge — Skate wax makes an edge slicker so the board slides farther, and a thin coat is enough.
skt|11|l21-3|9-10|Practice Without Rolling — Setting the board on the edge while standing still lets you feel the balance point before you add speed.
skt|11|l21-4|9-10|The 50-50 — In a 50-50, both trucks grind the edge at once with your weight centered between them.
skt|11|l21-5|9-10|The 5-0 — A 5-0 grinds on the back truck only, balanced like a manual with the nose up.
skt|11|l21-6|9-10|Angle and Speed — Come in at a shallow angle and a steady, comfortable speed so the trucks lock on and carry to the end.
skt|11|l21-7|9-10|The Boardslide — In a boardslide the board turns across the rail and the middle of the deck slides along it, between the trucks.
skt|11|l21-8|9-10|The Noseslide — In a noseslide, the board turns a quarter turn and only the nose slides on the ledge.
skt|11|l21-9|9-10|The Tailslide — A tailslide sets the tail on the ledge with the board turned a quarter turn and the weight over the back foot.
skt|11|l21-10|9-10|Look Where You Want to Go — Keeping your eyes on the end of the ledge keeps your shoulders, hips and weight lined up for the whole grind.
skt|11|l21-11|9-10|Bailing Safely — A good bail means stepping off and running it out when you can, and falling low and rolling when you cannot.
skt|11|l21-12|9-10|A Step-by-Step Practice Plan — Grinds and slides come in a sensible order, each one building on the skills of the one before.
skt|11|l22-1|9-10|What Friction Does — Friction is a force between two touching surfaces that pushes against the sliding, so it slows a grind.
skt|11|l22-2|9-10|The Normal Force — The normal force is the edge pushing up on the board; on a level ledge it equals the skater's weight.
skt|11|l22-3|9-10|Friction Equals Mu Times N — Kinetic friction force equals the coefficient of friction times the normal force: f = μN.
skt|11|l22-4|9-10|Slowing Equals Mu Times g — On a level rail, a grinding board slows at a = μg, so the skater's mass cancels out.
skt|11|l22-5|9-10|How Far a Grind Goes — A grind that starts at speed v slides a distance d = v² divided by (2μg) before it stops.
skt|11|l22-6|9-10|Why Speed Carries a Grind — Slide distance grows with the square of speed, so doubling your speed makes a grind go four times as far.
skt|11|l22-7|9-10|Wax Halves the Mu — Lowering the coefficient of friction makes a grind go farther; halving μ doubles the slide distance.
skt|11|l22-8|9-10|Metal, Concrete and Paint — Different edges have different friction: smooth metal is usually slick, bare concrete grabs, and paint changes as it wears.
skt|11|l22-9|9-10|Heat and Wear — Friction turns the board's motion energy into heat and wears away the metal, wood and wax it rubs.
skt|11|l22-10|9-10|Will It Reach the End? — Comparing the slide distance to the ledge's length tells you whether a grind will make it to the end.
skt|11|l22-11|9-10|Where the Model Stops — The formula is a good first model, but real grinds also depend on landing, slope, balance and changing friction.
skt|12|l23-1|9-10|Where Your Weight Is Centered — The center of mass is the single point where an object's weight acts as if it were all gathered together.
skt|12|l23-2|9-10|The Base of Support — An object stays balanced while its center of mass is above its base of support.
skt|12|l23-3|9-10|Staying Up on a Rolling Board — Bent knees lower the center of mass and let a rider make the small corrections that keep it over the board.
skt|12|l23-4|9-10|The Parts of a Truck — A truck is a set of metal parts held together by a kingpin, with rubbery bushings that let it tilt and spring back.
skt|12|l23-5|9-10|Leaning Turns the Wheels — Because the hanger swings on a slanted line, tilting the deck turns the axles, and the board curves toward the side you lean.
skt|12|l23-6|9-10|Tight Trucks and Loose Trucks — Tightening the kingpin nut or using harder bushings makes a board steadier but slower to turn; loosening does the opposite.
skt|12|l23-7|9-10|Why a Turn Needs a Force — A moving object goes straight unless a force pushes it sideways; in a turn, friction from the ground pushes the board toward the center of the curve.
skt|12|l23-8|9-10|Leaning Into the Carve — In a turn the rider leans toward the center so the ground's push stays in line with the center of mass; faster or tighter turns need more lean.
skt|12|l23-9|9-10|Speed, Radius and Grip — Friction can only push so hard; if a turn needs more inward force than the wheels can grip, the board slides.
skt|12|l23-10|9-10|What Speed Wobble Is — Speed wobble is a fast side-to-side shaking that feeds on itself, most often at higher speeds with loose trucks or stiff legs.
skt|12|l23-11|9-10|Calming a Wobble — To calm speed wobble, crouch lower, press gently on the front foot, relax and look ahead; prevent it by riding within your skill and setting trucks a little tighter.
skt|12|l24-1|9-10|Spinning Things Keep Spinning — Angular momentum, the amount of spin an object carries, stays the same unless an outside torque changes it.
skt|12|l24-2|9-10|Torque Starts and Stops a Spin — A torque is a twist; it equals the force times the lever arm, and it is the only thing that changes angular momentum.
skt|12|l24-3|9-10|Winding Up for a 180 — Skaters wind their shoulders the opposite way, then unwind as they pop, so the ground gives them spin before they leave it.
skt|12|l24-4|9-10|The Stool Experiment — Pulling the arms in lowers the moment of inertia, so the spin speeds up to keep angular momentum the same.
skt|12|l24-5|9-10|How Mass Spread Out Matters — Moment of inertia grows with the square of distance from the axis, so mass far out counts much more than mass close in.
skt|12|l24-6|9-10|Where the Extra Spin Energy Comes From — A faster spin has more motion energy, and that energy comes from the muscles doing work to pull the arms in.
skt|12|l24-7|9-10|How to Pump — To pump, crouch as you ride into each curve of the bowl, then push up to stand tall as you pass through the curve.
skt|12|l24-8|9-10|Why Pumping Adds Speed — Standing up where the curve pushes hardest lets the legs do more work than crouching gives back, and the extra becomes speed.
skt|12|l24-9|9-10|What Frontside and Backside Mean — Frontside means your chest and toes face the wall or the direction of a spin; backside means your back and heels face it.
skt|12|l24-10|9-10|The Frontside 180 Ollie — A frontside 180 ollie is an ollie with a wind-up: shoulders turn first, the hips follow, and the board lands rolling fakie.
skt|12|l24-11|9-10|Practicing Spins Safely — Spins are learned in small steps: quarter turns, then half turns, low and slow, with a plan for falling.
skt|13|l25-1|11-12|Knobs on the Ledge — Skate stoppers are metal or stone pieces fixed to ledges, rails and benches to block grinding and sliding.
skt|13|l25-2|11-12|Hostile Design in the City — Hostile design shapes public objects to stop certain uses, and skate stoppers are one example among many.
skt|13|l25-3|11-12|Why a Stopper Throws a Rider — When a stopper halts the board, the rider's body keeps moving forward, and wax works by lowering friction.
skt|13|l25-4|11-12|A Concrete Space in London — Skaters have used the undercroft beneath London's Southbank Centre since the 1970s, making it one of the oldest skate spots still in use.
skt|13|l25-5|11-12|Long Live Southbank — When a redevelopment plan threatened the undercroft, the Long Live Southbank campaign won an agreement in 2014 to keep it.
skt|13|l25-6|11-12|Burnside Under the Bridge — In 1990 skaters in Portland, Oregon, began building a concrete park under the Burnside Bridge without permission, and the city later accepted it.
skt|13|l25-7|11-12|The Do-It-Yourself Spirit — DIY skate spots grow from skaters building what their city lacks, and safe building takes skill, planning and permission.
skt|13|l25-8|11-12|Paying for Public Parks — The Tony Hawk Foundation, founded in 2002 and renamed The Skatepark Project in 2020, helps communities build public skateparks.
skt|13|l25-9|11-12|Reasons for Skateparks — Supporters say skateparks give young people a safe, free place to be active and build community, away from traffic and private property.
skt|13|l25-10|11-12|Concerns About Parks and Streets — Opponents raise cost, noise, injury and property damage, and skaters debate whether parks should replace street skating.
skt|13|l25-11|11-12|Bringing a Plan to the City — A strong skatepark proposal names a site, shows who will use it, answers stakeholders' concerns and keeps showing up.
skt|13|l26-1|11-12|Skateistan Begins in Kabul — Skateistan, founded by Oliver Percovich in Kabul in 2007, used skateboarding to draw children, including many girls, into education.
skt|13|l26-2|11-12|Growing Beyond Kabul — Skateistan spread to other countries, won wide attention, and had to change its work in Afghanistan after 2021.
skt|13|l26-3|11-12|Joining the Olympic Program — The International Olympic Committee added skateboarding in 2016 for the Tokyo Games, which were held in 2021.
skt|13|l26-4|11-12|Street and Park — Olympic skateboarding has two kinds of events: street, on a course like a city plaza, and park, in a deep, curved bowl.
skt|13|l26-5|11-12|How Judges Score — Judges score difficulty, clean landings, height and speed, and variety, and height in the air follows simple physics.
skt|13|l26-6|11-12|Street Gold in Tokyo — At Tokyo 2020, Yuto Horigome and Momiji Nishiya of Japan won the first Olympic street gold medals, and Rayssa Leal of Brazil took silver at thirteen.
skt|13|l26-7|11-12|Park Gold in Tokyo — In the Tokyo park events, Sakura Yosozumi of Japan and Keegan Palmer of Australia won gold, and Sky Brown won bronze for Great Britain at thirteen.
skt|13|l26-8|11-12|Paris 2024 and Beyond — At Paris 2024, Horigome and Palmer won gold again, Arisa Trew and Coco Yoshizawa won the women's titles, and skating is set for Los Angeles 2028.
skt|13|l26-9|11-12|Contests Before the Olympics — Skateboarding contests began long before the Olympics, from Anaheim in 1965 to Del Mar in 1975 to the X Games.
skt|13|l26-10|11-12|The Olympic Debate — Skaters disagree about the Olympics: some see lost freedom and culture, others see money, parks and new doors for girls and young skaters.
skt|13|l26-11|11-12|Young Champions, Long Careers — Young Olympic skaters train with protection and step-by-step progression, and their careers depend on recovering well from falls.
skt|14|l27-1|11-12|Seven Thin Layers of Maple — Most decks are seven thin plies of hard maple glued together and pressed in a mold, which makes them strong, light and springy.
skt|14|l27-2|11-12|Concave and Stiffness — Bending a deck into a side-to-side curve called concave makes it much stiffer and gives the feet edges to press against.
skt|14|l27-3|11-12|Nose, Tail and Wheelbase — The kicked-up tail and nose make levers for tricks, and the wheelbase sets how stable or quick a board feels.
skt|14|l27-4|11-12|Measuring Wheel Hardness — Wheel hardness is measured on the durometer A scale, from about 78A for soft wheels to about 101A for hard ones.
skt|14|l27-5|11-12|Soft Wheels, Hard Wheels — Soft wheels squash to grip and smooth rough ground; hard wheels keep their shape to roll fast and slide on smooth surfaces.
skt|14|l27-6|11-12|Wheel Size — Bigger wheels roll over cracks and hold speed; smaller wheels are lighter and lower, which helps flip tricks.
skt|14|l27-7|11-12|What Urethane Is — Urethane wheels are cast from two liquids that react into a tough, elastic plastic, and changing the recipe changes the hardness.
skt|14|l27-8|11-12|How a Ball Bearing Works — Bearings replace sliding with rolling, so a wheel spins on its axle with very little friction.
skt|14|l27-9|11-12|ABEC Is Precision, Not Speed — The ABEC scale rates how precisely a bearing is made, not how fast a skateboard will roll.
skt|14|l27-10|11-12|Keeping Bearings Rolling — Bearings roll best when they are clean, lightly lubricated and not squeezed by an overtightened axle nut.
skt|14|l27-11|11-12|No Part Is Best at Everything — Every skateboard part trades one strength for another, so engineers start from where and how the board will be ridden.
skt|14|l27-12|11-12|Matching a Setup to the Ground — A good setup matches deck, wheels and bearings to the ground and the kind of riding the skater plans to do.
skt|14|l28-1|11-12|A Curve From a Circle — Most ramp transitions are arcs cut from a circle, and the circle's radius sets how tight or mellow the curve feels.
skt|14|l28-2|11-12|How Steep Is the Top? — If a ramp stops before vertical, geometry tells you how steep its top edge is and how long the curve runs along the ground.
skt|14|l28-3|11-12|Coping, Platform and Flat Bottom — The coping, the platform and the flat bottom each give riders something they need: a grinding edge, a safe place to start and time between walls.
skt|14|l28-4|11-12|Height Becomes Speed — As a rider drops down a transition, potential energy turns into kinetic energy, so the drop height predicts the speed at the bottom.
skt|14|l28-5|11-12|Losses and Pumping — Friction and air drain energy on every pass, and riders replace it by pumping: crouching high and pressing up through the bottom of the curve.
skt|14|l28-6|11-12|Dropping In With Energy in Mind — Dropping in turns a standing rider's height into speed, and committing the front foot keeps the board under the body.
skt|14|l28-7|11-12|Why Riders Feel Heavier — At the bottom of a curve, the ramp must push up extra hard to bend the rider's path, so the rider feels heavier.
skt|14|l28-8|11-12|Why Tight Curves Feel Harder — A tighter curve gives a bigger and more sudden jump in force and less time to react, so it demands more strength and skill.
skt|14|l28-9|11-12|Ribs, Stringers and Plywood — A wooden ramp is a frame of curved ribs joined by stringers and covered with layers of thin plywood bent to the curve.
skt|14|l28-10|11-12|Building Safely — Safe ramp building means protecting the builders, choosing a good site and checking the finished ramp before every session.
skt|14|l28-11|11-12|A Worked Design Problem — Good ramp design puts the numbers together: height, radius, top angle, footprint, speed and felt weight, then checks them against the riders.
skt|15|l29-1|11-12|Frontside and Backside — Frontside means you turn so your chest faces where you are going first; backside means your back leads.
skt|15|l29-2|11-12|The Frontside 180 Ollie — In a frontside 180 ollie, the head and shoulders turn first and the board follows them halfway around.
skt|15|l29-3|11-12|The Backside 180 Ollie — In a backside 180 ollie, your back turns toward the direction you were rolling, and you land rolling fakie.
skt|15|l29-4|11-12|Why the Spin Starts on the Ground — Once you leave the ground, your total spin cannot change, so the turn must start before your feet leave.
skt|15|l29-5|11-12|Three Axes of a Board — A board can spin flat around an up-and-down axis, roll around its length, or tumble end over end.
skt|15|l29-6|11-12|Why Flips Spin Faster — A deck is much easier to roll around its length than to spin flat, so a small flick makes a fast flip.
skt|15|l29-7|11-12|The 360 Flip — A 360 flip, or tre flip, is a backside 360 shove-it and a kickflip done at the same time.
skt|15|l29-8|11-12|Varial Flips and Their Cousins — Combining a half shove-it with a kickflip or heelflip makes a family of four related flip tricks.
skt|15|l29-9|11-12|Rock to Fakie — In a rock to fakie, the board rests on the coping between the trucks, then the rider lifts the front wheels back over and rolls down fakie.
skt|15|l29-10|11-12|Airs Above the Coping — An air is a trick where the rider and board fly above the coping and land back on the ramp.
skt|15|l29-11|11-12|Backside Airs and Grabs — A grab holds the board to the feet in the air, and each grab is named by which hand holds which edge.
skt|15|l29-12|11-12|Mapping a Progression — Every advanced trick sits on a chain of simpler ones, so a skater learns them in an order where each piece is steady first.
skt|15|l30-1|11-12|Three Stages of Learning — A motor skill moves from careful thinking, to smoother practice, to doing it without thinking.
skt|15|l30-2|11-12|What Practice Changes Inside — Practice changes the brain so a movement uses less effort and attention, and errors are corrected faster.
skt|15|l30-3|11-12|Rest and Sleep Help Skills Settle — Skills keep strengthening after practice ends, especially during sleep, and tired practice can make form worse.
skt|15|l30-4|11-12|Spaced Practice — Practice spread over several shorter sessions usually builds a longer-lasting skill than the same time in one long session.
skt|15|l30-5|11-12|Variable Practice — Practicing a trick in different spots, speeds and mixes builds a skill that works in new places.
skt|15|l30-6|11-12|Filming Yourself for Feedback — Video shows what the body actually did, and counting frames can even measure how high a pop went.
skt|15|l30-7|11-12|One Cue at a Time — Focusing on one simple cue about the effect of a movement often works better than thinking about many body parts.
skt|15|l30-8|11-12|Fear Is a Signal — Fear is a normal warning that a step is too big, and breaking a trick into smaller steps makes it manageable.
skt|15|l30-9|11-12|Falling Small — Crouching, rolling and keeping the arms bent spread a fall over more time and more body, which lowers the force.
skt|15|l30-10|11-12|Knee Slides on a Ramp — On a ramp, a fall forward becomes a slide down the transition on hard-capped knee pads.
skt|15|l30-11|11-12|Skaters Teaching Skaters — For most of its history skateboarding has been learned from other skaters, in person and through magazines and videos.
skt|15|l30-12|11-12|Building Your Own Practice Plan — A good practice plan combines a warm-up, a short spaced block on one trick, variety, one cue, and a planned stop.
skt|16|l31-1|adult|From Plain Planks to Painted Decks — Early decks were mostly plain, and graphics grew into a major art form once companies, teams and pro models gave artists a reason to draw.
skt|16|l31-2|adult|Jim Phillips at Santa Cruz — Jim Phillips, an artist for Santa Cruz, drew bold cartoon graphics such as the Screaming Hand that became some of the best-known images in skating.
skt|16|l31-3|adult|Vernon Courtland Johnson and the Skulls — Vernon Courtland Johnson drew Powell Peralta's skulls, skeletons and the Bones Brigade images that defined how 1980s skating looked.
skt|16|l31-4|adult|SkateBoarder and the Dogtown Stories — SkateBoarder magazine spread skating's look across the country, and its 1970s Dogtown articles and photos made the Zephyr skaters famous.
skt|16|l31-5|adult|Thrasher and TransWorld — Thrasher (1981) and TransWorld SKATEboarding (1983) carried skating through the 1980s, each with its own voice and look.
skt|16|l31-6|adult|How a Skate Photo Is Made — Skate photographers use wide lenses, flashes and careful timing to freeze a trick at the exact moment that shows it best.
skt|16|l31-7|adult|The Bones Brigade on VHS — The Bones Brigade Video Show (1984) used home video tape to show skating in motion, and videos soon became how skaters learned and shared tricks.
skt|16|l31-8|adult|Street Videos of the Early 1990s — Videos such as Blind's Video Days (1991) and Plan B's Questionable (1992) made street skating and the filmmaker's style central to skate culture.
skt|16|l31-9|adult|From Camcorders to the Internet — Better cameras in the 1990s and the internet in the 2000s moved skate video from mailed tapes to clips anyone can post.
skt|16|l31-10|adult|Punk, Hip-Hop and the Soundtrack — Punk in the 1980s and hip-hop in the 1990s shaped skate culture, and the songs in videos and games introduced many people to that music.
skt|16|l31-11|adult|Zines and DIY Culture — Skaters have a long habit of making things themselves: photocopied zines, backyard ramps and skater-built parks like Burnside.
skt|16|l32-1|adult|Patti McGee in 1965 — Patti McGee won the women's title at the first national championships in 1965 and appeared on the cover of Life magazine that year.
skt|16|l32-2|adult|Peggy Oki and the Zephyr Team — Peggy Oki was the only woman on the original Zephyr team, and she skated in the same low, surf-style way as her teammates.
skt|16|l32-3|adult|Women's Pro Careers Grow — Women skaters rode professionally from the 1970s on, but they had fewer contests, pro models and photos until skaters organized for change.
skt|16|l32-4|adult|Teenage Women on the Olympic Podium — At Tokyo 2020 and Paris 2024, young women from Japan, Brazil, Great Britain and Australia won Olympic medals, some at age 12 or 13.
skt|16|l32-5|adult|Being Seen Changes Who Skates — When people see skaters who look like them in photos, videos and contests, more of them try skating, so coverage itself opens doors.
skt|16|l32-6|adult|Ray Barbee and Smooth Street Style — Ray Barbee, a Black skater on the Bones Brigade in the late 1980s, became known for a smooth, flowing flat-ground street style.
skt|16|l32-7|adult|Stevie Williams and Nyjah Huston — Stevie Williams rose from Philadelphia's Love Park to start his own company, and Nyjah Huston became one of the most successful contest skaters ever.
skt|16|l32-8|adult|Skating in Brazil and Japan — Brazil and Japan built strong skate scenes that produced Olympic champions, showing that skateboarding's center is no longer only California.
skt|16|l32-9|adult|WCMX and Aaron Fotheringham — WCMX is riding a skatepark in a wheelchair, and Aaron Fotheringham showed what is possible when he landed the first wheelchair backflip in the mid-2000s.
skt|16|l32-10|adult|Groups That Open the Gate — Groups such as Skateistan and The Skatepark Project, plus local girls' and adaptive sessions, work on purpose to bring new people into skating.
skt|16|l32-11|adult|A Place Where Many Belong — Skateparks work best for everyone when riders share space with simple habits: take turns, watch your line and welcome newcomers.
skt|17|l33-1|adult|Healing Takes Longer — Adult bodies usually heal more slowly than children's, so preventing an injury is worth more than toughing one out.
skt|17|l33-2|adult|A Helmet That Fits — A helmet protects you only if it is made for the job, sits level on your forehead and is buckled snug every time.
skt|17|l33-3|adult|Wrists, Knees and Elbows — Wrist injuries are among the most common skateboarding injuries, and wrist guards help prevent them.
skt|17|l33-4|adult|Ten Minutes Before You Roll — A short, active warm-up gets the legs, hips and ankles ready for the bending and balancing that skating demands.
skt|17|l33-5|adult|A Board That Feels Calm — A wider deck and softer, larger wheels give a beginning adult a steadier, smoother ride.
skt|17|l33-6|adult|Cruiser or Standard Board — A cruiser is built for smooth rolling and getting around; a standard popsicle board is built for tricks and parks.
skt|17|l33-7|adult|Trucks That Fit the Deck — Trucks should be about as wide as the deck, and the riser pads, bolts and bearings must match the setup.
skt|17|l33-8|adult|The Shape of a Small Fall — Crouching low and rolling spreads a fall out, while locked straight arms send all the force into the wrists.
skt|17|l33-9|adult|Falling Drills on Grass — Practicing falls on soft grass, in full gear and at no speed, teaches your body the right move before you need it.
skt|17|l33-10|adult|Finding Your Stance — Regular and goofy are both normal; your front foot goes over the front bolts and your back foot over the back bolts.
skt|17|l33-11|adult|Quiet Times at the Park — Going when a skatepark is less crowded and learning its unwritten rules make the first visits calmer and safer.
skt|17|l33-12|adult|Small Goals, Steady Wins — Small, specific goals for each session keep an adult learner safe, motivated and able to see progress.
skt|17|l34-1|adult|Three Stages of Learning — Learning a movement usually moves from thinking through every step, to refining it, to doing it without thinking.
skt|17|l34-2|adult|Short, Frequent Sessions — Spreading practice across several short sessions usually builds skill better than one long session.
skt|17|l34-3|adult|Breaking a Trick Into Steps — Any trick can be split into small pieces, practiced first on grass and then rolling slowly, like the kickturn.
skt|17|l34-4|adult|Fear Is Information — Fear is a normal signal that a step may be too big; the answer is usually a smaller step, not ignoring the feeling.
skt|17|l34-5|adult|Building Real Confidence — Confidence grows most from actually succeeding at small steps, which psychologists call mastery experiences.
skt|17|l34-6|adult|Plateaus and Bad Days — Slow weeks and bad days are a normal part of motor learning, and changing the practice often gets you moving again.
skt|17|l34-7|adult|Finding a Crew or a Lesson — A lesson, a class or a friendly group of skaters can speed up learning and make it safer and more fun.
skt|17|l34-8|adult|A Beginner Beside Your Child — Learning next to a child models safe habits and shows that making mistakes is part of getting better.
skt|17|l34-9|adult|Skating for Fitness and Balance — Skating works the legs, hips and core and trains balance, which helps adults move with more steadiness in daily life.
skt|17|l34-10|adult|Notes That Show Progress — A simple skate log records what you practiced and how it went, so you can see progress you might otherwise miss.
skt|17|l34-11|adult|Using Video Well — A short phone video, watched frame by frame, shows what your body is doing so you can fix one thing at a time.
skt|18|l35-1|adult|Speed from Distance and Time — Average speed is the distance you cover divided by the time it takes, so two marked cones and a clock are enough to measure it.
skt|18|l35-2|adult|Counting Video Frames — Counting video frames gives a more exact time than a hand stopwatch, because each frame is a fixed slice of a second.
skt|18|l35-3|adult|Repeat Runs and Honest Error — Repeating a test, reporting the mean and the range, and filming from the right spot make a measurement trustworthy.
skt|18|l35-4|adult|Finding the Slope of a Path — A slope's grade is its rise divided by its run, and on a gentle slope gravity pulls along it with about g times the grade.
skt|18|l35-5|adult|The Coast-Down Test — Timing a board's speed and measuring how far it coasts on level ground reveals how strongly rolling losses slow it.
skt|18|l35-6|adult|Waxed and Unwaxed Ledge — Pulling a loaded deck along a ledge with a luggage scale measures the coefficient of friction before and after waxing.
skt|18|l35-7|adult|Predicting a Slide — On level ground a slide lasts a distance of v² ÷ (2μg), so lowering friction or adding speed makes it longer.
skt|18|l35-8|adult|Hang Time from Frames — The time a board spends in the air sets how high it rose, by the rule h = g × t² ÷ 8.
skt|18|l35-9|adult|Checking the Height — Comparing the predicted height with marks on a wall tests the model, as long as you measure the same thing and know your frame error.
skt|18|l35-10|adult|Energy on a Ramp — Height becomes speed on a ramp, and comparing the predicted speed with the measured one shows how much energy friction and air took.
skt|18|l35-11|adult|Writing It Up Like a Scientist — A clear report states the question, the hypothesis, the method, the results and a conclusion, so someone else can repeat the test.
skt|18|l36-1|adult|From Idea to Opening Day — Most public skateparks are built by cities after advocates show a need, the public helps design it and the council approves the money.
skt|18|l36-2|adult|Community Meetings — At community meetings, skaters and neighbors shape a park's design, and worries answered with facts there are less likely to block it later.
skt|18|l36-3|adult|Budgets and Funding — A park's budget adds design and a contingency to construction, and the money usually comes from several sources at once.
skt|18|l36-4|adult|In Front of the City Council — The city council decides because it controls the budget, and short, factual public comments help members say yes.
skt|18|l36-5|adult|Street Plaza or Bowl — A street plaza copies city features, a bowl comes from the pool tradition, and many towns mix both in a hybrid park.
skt|18|l36-6|adult|Room for Beginners — A beginner area placed away from fast lines, with features that step up in size, lets new riders of any age learn safely.
skt|18|l36-7|adult|Concrete, Steel and Wood — Poured concrete lasts longest and is quieter, while prefabricated ramps cost less to start but are louder and wear faster.
skt|18|l36-8|adult|Girls and Women at the Park — Women have skated from the start, Olympic champions have raised their visibility, and simple design and programs help more girls and women ride.
skt|18|l36-9|adult|Adaptive Riders and WCMX — Wheelchair and other adaptive riders use skateparks too, and smooth paths, step-free entries and gentle access ramps let them reach the features.
skt|18|l36-10|adult|Safety, Lighting and Noise — Clear views, even lighting on a timer and distance from homes make a park safer and quieter, and sound drops about 6 decibels per doubling of distance.
skt|18|l36-11|adult|Parks Skaters Built Themselves — Burnside, the Southbank undercroft and Skateistan show how skaters who build and care for a space can change how a city sees them.
skt|18|l36-12|adult|Writing the Proposal — A strong proposal answers the council's questions with evidence: need, site, design, cost, safety and support.
skt|2|l3-1|k-2|A Helmet Guards Your Head — A helmet has soft foam inside that keeps a bump from hurting your head.
skt|2|l3-2|k-2|Snug and Buckled — A helmet works best when it sits low, fits snug and is buckled.
skt|2|l3-3|k-2|Pads and Wrist Guards — Knee pads, elbow pads and wrist guards cover the parts that hit the ground first.
skt|2|l3-4|k-2|Flat Shoes That Grip — Flat sneakers with rubber soles help your feet stay on the board.
skt|2|l3-5|k-2|A Safe Place to Ride — Ride on smooth, dry, flat ground, far away from cars.
skt|2|l3-6|k-2|A Grown-Up Nearby — Young skaters ride with a grown-up close by to help.
skt|2|l3-7|k-2|Get Low, Then Roll — If you start to fall, bend low and roll so the fall is short and soft.
skt|2|l3-8|k-2|Bent Arms, Not Straight — Do not catch yourself with straight, stiff arms; keep them bent and soft.
skt|2|l4-1|k-2|Grass or Carpet First — Stand on the board on grass or carpet first, so it cannot roll away.
skt|2|l4-2|k-2|Feet Over the Bolts — Put your front foot over the front bolts and your back foot over the back bolts.
skt|2|l4-3|k-2|Knees Like a Spring — Bent knees work like a spring and help you stay steady.
skt|2|l4-4|k-2|Arms Out Wide — Holding your arms out helps you catch your balance.
skt|2|l4-5|k-2|Eyes Where You Go — Look up and ahead to where you want to go, not down at your feet.
skt|2|l4-6|k-2|Regular and Goofy — Some riders put the left foot in front and some the right; both are normal.
skt|2|l4-7|k-2|Practice Makes Steady — Balance gets better each time you practice.
skt|3|l5-1|k-2|Safe Before You Roll — Put on a buckled helmet and find a smooth, safe place before you ride.
skt|3|l5-2|k-2|Front Foot, Back Foot — Your front foot goes near the front of the board, and your back foot does the pushing.
skt|3|l5-3|k-2|One Foot Pushes — Your front foot stays on the board while your back foot pushes the ground.
skt|3|l5-4|k-2|A Push Is a Force — A push is a force, and a force can start a board moving.
skt|3|l5-5|k-2|Foot Back On, Roll — After a push, put your back foot on the board and roll with bent knees.
skt|3|l5-6|k-2|Small Pushes Are Fine — Small pushes make a slow, steady ride, which is good for learning.
skt|3|l5-7|k-2|Smooth or Bumpy Ground — Smooth ground lets a board roll far, and bumpy ground slows it down.
skt|3|l6-1|k-2|Drag a Foot Gently — Brush your back foot lightly on the ground to slow down while your front foot stays on.
skt|3|l6-2|k-2|Rubbing Slows You — Friction is the rubbing that slows things down.
skt|3|l6-3|k-2|Lean on Your Toes — Press your toes down and the board turns toward your toe side.
skt|3|l6-4|k-2|Lean on Your Heels — Press your heels down and the board turns toward your heel side.
skt|3|l6-5|k-2|Slopes Make You Faster — Gravity pulls you down a slope, so the board speeds up without a push.
skt|3|l6-6|k-2|Stop Before Too Fast — Start slowing down early, before the board gets too fast.
skt|3|l6-7|k-2|Step Off Safely — Slow down first, then step off with your back foot and walk it out.
skt|4|l7-1|3-5|Surfing on the Sidewalk — In California in the late 1950s, surfers rode boards on sidewalks when the ocean had no good waves.
skt|4|l7-2|3-5|Making a Board at Home — The first skateboards were homemade: a wooden plank with roller-skate wheels fastened underneath.
skt|4|l7-3|3-5|Who Made the First One? — No one person is known to have invented the skateboard; many people built them in many places.
skt|4|l7-4|3-5|Store Boards Arrive — Around 1959 stores began selling skateboards, and by the early 1960s companies like Makaha and Hobie were making them.
skt|4|l7-5|3-5|Clay Wheels — Many 1960s boards rolled on clay wheels, which were quieter than metal but slid out easily.
skt|4|l7-6|3-5|Steel Wheels and Noise — Steel wheels were loud, shaky and slippery, and they jammed at even a tiny stone.
skt|4|l7-7|3-5|Why the Rider Keeps Going — When a board stops suddenly, the rider's body keeps moving forward until something stops it.
skt|4|l7-8|3-5|Anaheim, 1965 — In 1965 the first National Skateboard Championships were held in Anaheim, California, and shown on TV.
skt|4|l7-9|3-5|Patti McGee — Patti McGee was the women's national champion in 1965 and appeared on the cover of Life magazine.
skt|4|l7-10|3-5|The Boom Fades — Around 1965 to 1966 the first boom faded, as many riders got hurt on boards with poor wheels.
skt|4|l8-1|3-5|Frank Nasworthy's Idea — In the early 1970s, Frank Nasworthy put urethane wheels on skateboards and sold them as Cadillac Wheels.
skt|4|l8-2|3-5|Why Urethane Grips — Urethane wheels squish a little and grip the ground, so they roll over bumps and hold on in turns.
skt|4|l8-3|3-5|Soft and Hard Wheels — Wheel hardness is measured on the durometer A scale, from about 78A for soft wheels to about 101A for hard ones.
skt|4|l8-4|3-5|The Kicktail — Larry Stevenson's kicktail, patented in 1971, let riders press the tail to lift the front of the board.
skt|4|l8-5|3-5|Try a Tail Press — Practice pressing the tail on grass or carpet first, with your back foot on the tail and your front foot near the front bolts.
skt|4|l8-6|3-5|Smooth Spinning Bearings — In the mid-1970s, sealed precision bearings let wheels spin smoothly and kept dirt out.
skt|4|l8-7|3-5|Surf Style from Dogtown — The Zephyr team from Santa Monica and Venice, California, skated low and fast, copying surfers.
skt|4|l8-8|3-5|Del Mar, 1975 — At the 1975 Del Mar Nationals, the Zephyr team's low surf style surprised the crowd and changed contest skating.
skt|4|l8-9|3-5|A Dry California — During the California drought of 1976 to 1977, many backyard swimming pools sat empty, and skaters rode them.
skt|4|l8-10|3-5|Riding the Curved Wall — Rolling up a curved wall turns motion energy into stored energy, and rolling back down turns it back into motion.
skt|4|l8-11|3-5|The First Skateparks — In 1976, Carlsbad Skatepark in California and Skateboard City in Florida opened as two of the first skateparks.
skt|5|l9-1|3-5|A Force Is a Push or a Pull — A force is a push or a pull, and every force has a size and a direction.
skt|5|l9-2|3-5|Balanced and Unbalanced Forces — Balanced forces cancel out and nothing changes; an unbalanced force changes how a board moves.
skt|5|l9-3|3-5|What Inertia Means — Inertia means things keep doing what they are doing until a force makes them change.
skt|5|l9-4|3-5|The Pebble Stop — When a wheel hits a pebble, the board stops but your body keeps going forward because of inertia.
skt|5|l9-5|3-5|Friction Slows the Board — Friction is a force between two surfaces that rub, and it slowly stops a rolling board.
skt|5|l9-6|3-5|Friction You Want — Friction is also helpful: it keeps your shoes on the deck and lets your foot slow the board.
skt|5|l9-7|3-5|Gravity Pulls Everything Down — Gravity is a pull toward the center of the Earth that acts on everything, all the time.
skt|5|l9-8|3-5|Newton's First Law — Newton's first law says an object stays still or keeps moving straight at the same speed unless a force changes it.
skt|5|l9-9|3-5|Soft Wheels Roll Over Cracks — Soft urethane wheels squeeze around small bumps and grip the ground, so cracks and pebbles stop them less.
skt|5|l9-10|3-5|Measuring Wheel Hardness — Wheel hardness is measured with a durometer; smaller numbers mean softer wheels.
skt|5|l10-1|3-5|Height Stores Energy — Anything lifted up high has stored energy, called potential energy, that gravity can turn into motion.
skt|5|l10-2|3-5|Lifting Takes Energy — Energy is never made from nothing; a skater's muscles supply the energy that gets stored as height.
skt|5|l10-3|3-5|Motion Energy — Anything that is moving has motion energy, called kinetic energy, and faster things have more.
skt|5|l10-4|3-5|The Big Energy Trade — Rolling down a ramp trades stored energy for motion energy; rolling up trades it back.
skt|5|l10-5|3-5|A Ramp Works Like a Slide — A ramp works like a playground slide: gravity pulls you along the slope, and a curve turns falling into rolling.
skt|5|l10-6|3-5|A Higher Start Gives More Speed — The higher a skater starts, the more stored energy there is, so the faster the skater goes at the bottom.
skt|5|l10-7|3-5|Rolling Up and Back on a Bank — On a low bank, gravity slows you as you roll up and speeds you up as you roll back down.
skt|5|l10-8|3-5|Friction Turns Motion Into Heat — Friction changes some motion energy into heat and sound, so a rolling board loses energy over time.
skt|5|l10-9|3-5|Never Quite as High — Because friction takes a little energy each trip, a rolling thing never climbs back quite as high as it started.
skt|5|l10-10|3-5|The Halfpipe Energy Trade — On a halfpipe, energy trades back and forth between height and speed, and pumping with the legs adds the energy friction takes.
skt|6|l11-1|3-5|Gear That Fits — A buckled helmet that fits, wrist guards, pads and flat shoes protect you while you learn new tricks.
skt|6|l11-2|3-5|A Good Practice Spot — Learn on ground that is smooth, dry and flat, far from cars, with a grown-up nearby.
skt|6|l11-3|3-5|How to Fall Small — Falling is part of learning; crouch low, land on your pads, and roll instead of catching yourself with straight arms.
skt|6|l11-4|3-5|A Board Your Size — A board that fits your feet is easier to balance and control.
skt|6|l11-5|3-5|Tail, Nose and Bolts — Knowing the tail, the nose, the bolts and your front and back foot helps you follow any trick's steps.
skt|6|l11-6|3-5|Kickturn on the Grass — A kickturn means pressing the tail to lift the front wheels, turning, and setting the wheels back down.
skt|6|l11-7|3-5|Kickturns While Rolling — Once kickturns feel easy standing still, try them while rolling slowly, and keep the tail press gentle.
skt|6|l11-8|3-5|Tic-Tac to Move Forward — A tic-tac is a string of small kickturns from side to side that moves the board forward without pushing.
skt|6|l11-9|3-5|Balancing on the Back Wheels — A manual is rolling on only the back wheels, finding the balance point and holding it.
skt|6|l11-10|3-5|The Tail Is a Lever — The board works like a lever: press the tail down, the back wheels act as the pivot, and the nose goes up.
skt|6|l11-11|3-5|A Little Every Day — Short practice on many days builds a trick better than one very long practice.
skt|6|l12-1|3-5|Words for Ramps — A ramp has a flat top called the deck, a metal edge called the coping, a curved wall called the transition, and a flat bottom.
skt|6|l12-2|3-5|Start Small with a Coach — Learn ramp skills on a small, low ramp with a coach or grown-up nearby and all your pads on.
skt|6|l12-3|3-5|Rolling Up and Back Down — Rolling a little way up a ramp and back down backward, called fakie, teaches the feel of the curve.
skt|6|l12-4|3-5|Setting Up on the Coping — To set up a drop-in, the tail sits on the coping and the back foot holds it while the front of the board hangs over the ramp.
skt|6|l12-5|3-5|Lean Forward and Commit — To drop in, put the front foot on the bolts, lean your chest over it, and commit with bent knees.
skt|6|l12-6|3-5|Why Leaning Back Slips Out — Leaning back on a drop-in leaves your weight behind the board, so the board slips out ahead of you.
skt|6|l12-7|3-5|When You Feel Scared — Fear is a normal signal, and breaking a trick into smaller steps helps you get ready.
skt|6|l12-8|3-5|Toe Side and Heel Side — Pressing your toes turns the board toward the toe side; pressing your heels turns it toward the heel side.
skt|6|l12-9|3-5|Long, Smooth Carves — A carve is a long, smooth turn made by leaning on your toes or heels while you roll.
skt|6|l12-10|3-5|How to Pump a Ramp — Pumping means crouching high on the wall and standing up through the curve, which adds speed without pushing.
skt|6|l12-11|3-5|Why Pumping Adds Speed — Friction takes away a little energy on every trip, and pumping puts energy back from your muscles.
skt|7|l13-1|6-8|Backyard Pools in California — In the 1970s, skaters in Southern California rode empty backyard pools because their smooth curved walls felt like waves.
skt|7|l13-2|6-8|The Parts of a Pool — Skaters learned names for each part of a pool: the flat bottom, the curved transition, the vertical wall, the tile and the coping.
skt|7|l13-3|6-8|Wheels and Bearings That Grip — Urethane wheels and sealed precision bearings made it possible to grip a curved wall and roll smoothly up it.
skt|7|l13-4|6-8|Riding the Curve — On a curved transition, the wall pushes the skater around the curve, so the skater feels heavier at the bottom.
skt|7|l13-5|6-8|Speed From Height — Starting higher on a wall gives a skater more stored energy, which becomes more speed at the bottom.
skt|7|l13-6|6-8|The Frontside Air — In the late 1970s, pool skaters learned to fly above the coping by grabbing the board, an early trick called the frontside air.
skt|7|l13-7|6-8|1976: Carlsbad and Port Orange — Two of the first built skateparks, Carlsbad Skatepark in California and Skateboard City in Florida, opened in 1976.
skt|7|l13-8|6-8|The Park Boom — By the late 1970s, dozens of paid skateparks had opened across the United States, often with rules about helmets and pads.
skt|7|l13-9|6-8|SkateBoarder Magazine — SkateBoarder magazine spread pool and park skating across the country through photos and stories in the 1970s.
skt|7|l13-10|6-8|Insurance and Lawsuits — In the early 1980s, high insurance costs and injury lawsuits closed many skateparks.
skt|7|l13-11|6-8|What Skaters Did Next — When parks closed, skaters built wooden ramps and took to the streets, and later public parks returned.
skt|7|l14-1|6-8|Alan Gelfand's No-Hands Air — In the late 1970s, Alan 'Ollie' Gelfand popped his board into the air on a ramp wall with no hands, and the ollie was named for him.
skt|7|l14-2|6-8|How a Board Pops Up — In an ollie, the back foot snaps the tail, the ground pushes back, the board tips up on its back wheels, and the front foot drags it level.
skt|7|l14-3|6-8|How Long an Ollie Lasts — Hang time depends on how high the rider rises; a 30-centimeter ollie keeps a rider in the air about half a second.
skt|7|l14-4|6-8|Freestyle Skating — Freestyle was a style of flat-ground skating done as a routine, and Rodney Mullen became its best-known rider.
skt|7|l14-5|6-8|The Ollie on Flat Ground — In the early 1980s, Rodney Mullen did the ollie on flat ground, so a skater could jump with no ramp at all.
skt|7|l14-6|6-8|Kickflips and Flip Tricks — Rodney Mullen invented the kickflip as it is done today, along with other flip tricks like the heelflip and the 360 flip.
skt|7|l14-7|6-8|The Bones Brigade — Powell Peralta's team, the Bones Brigade, gathered young riders such as Tony Hawk and Rodney Mullen in the 1980s.
skt|7|l14-8|6-8|Skate Videos on Tape — The Bones Brigade Video Show of 1984 showed skaters that tricks could be learned by watching moving pictures at home.
skt|7|l14-9|6-8|Thrasher and TransWorld — Two magazines that started in the early 1980s, Thrasher (1981) and TransWorld SKATEboarding (1983), kept skating connected after parks closed.
skt|7|l14-10|6-8|Backyard Ramps — In the 1980s, skaters built wooden halfpipes in backyards, and small mini ramps became a common place to learn.
skt|7|l14-11|6-8|Curbs, Ledges and the Street — In the 1980s, street skaters used the ollie to ride curbs, ledges and banks, starting with low objects first.
skt|8|l15-1|6-8|What a Lever Does — A lever is a stiff bar that turns around a fixed point, so a push on one side moves the other side.
skt|8|l15-2|6-8|Finding the Pivot on a Board — When the tail is pressed down, a skateboard turns around its rear wheels, so the rear wheels are the fulcrum.
skt|8|l15-3|6-8|Small Push, Big Swing — Points far from the pivot move farther than points close to it, so a short drop of the tail makes a big rise of the nose.
skt|8|l15-4|6-8|What Torque Means — Torque is how strongly a force turns something, and it equals the force times its distance from the pivot.
skt|8|l15-5|6-8|Tail Tip or Bolts? — The same stomp makes far more torque on the tip of the tail than near the rear bolts, because the lever arm is longer.
skt|8|l15-6|6-8|Torques That Fight — The front foot makes a torque the other way, so it must go light at the pop or the nose will not rise.
skt|8|l15-7|6-8|Flat Boards Came First — Early skateboards were mostly flat, and the upturned kicktail later gave skaters a lever they could press.
skt|8|l15-8|6-8|Why the Angle Helps — An upturned tail starts higher, so the board can turn farther before the tail hits the ground, and the foot finds it easily.
skt|8|l15-9|6-8|Practicing the Pop Standing Still — A skater can learn the pop safely on grass or carpet, where the board cannot roll, before trying it while moving.
skt|8|l15-10|6-8|The Ground Pushes Back — When the tail strikes the ground, the ground pushes back up on the tail just as hard; that push is what pops the board.
skt|8|l15-11|6-8|Getting Out of the Way — The skater must jump and pull the knees up so the board has room to rise; the board cannot lift a rider standing on it.
skt|8|l16-1|6-8|What Grip Tape Does — Grip tape is a rough sheet on top of the deck that makes strong friction with a skater's shoes.
skt|8|l16-2|6-8|The Front Foot Drags the Board — As the front shoe slides toward the nose, friction from the grip tape pulls the board up and forward with it.
skt|8|l16-3|6-8|Leveling Out — At the top of the slide, the front foot pushes forward and a little down to bring the board level under both feet.
skt|8|l16-4|6-8|Crouch and Pop — An ollie begins with a low crouch that stores up spring in the legs, then a sharp pop of the tail.
skt|8|l16-5|6-8|Slide and Jump — Right after the pop, the front foot slides toward the nose while the skater jumps and tucks, almost all at once.
skt|8|l16-6|6-8|Level and Land — The front foot levels the board, then both feet come down over the bolts with bent knees.
skt|8|l16-7|6-8|The Center of Mass — A body's center of mass is its balance point, and in the air it follows a path that arms and legs cannot change.
skt|8|l16-8|6-8|A Curve Called a Parabola — In the air the center of mass rises, slows, stops at the top and falls along a curve called a parabola.
skt|8|l16-9|6-8|Why the Board Stays Under You — The skater and board keep the same forward speed in the air, so a rolling ollie lands back on the board.
skt|8|l16-10|6-8|Higher Jumps, Longer Hang Time — Hang time grows with jump height, but slowly: four times the height gives only twice the time.
skt|8|l16-11|6-8|Working Out a 30 cm Ollie — A 30 cm ollie keeps a rider in the air about half a second, which matches the slow-motion frame count.
skt|8|l16-12|6-8|Bend Your Knees — Bending the knees on landing stretches out the stopping time, which makes the force on the body much smaller.
skt|9|l17-1|6-8|Gear for Ollie Practice — A buckled helmet that fits, wrist guards, pads and flat shoes let you practice the ollie and fall without serious harm.
skt|9|l17-2|6-8|A Good Place to Practice — Practice on a flat, smooth, dry surface away from cars, and start on grass or carpet, where the wheels cannot roll.
skt|9|l17-3|6-8|Where Your Feet Go — Put the ball of your back foot on the tail and your front foot just behind the front bolts, across the board.
skt|9|l17-4|6-8|Crouch to Load Your Legs — Bending your knees low before the pop gives your legs a longer push, so you can jump higher.
skt|9|l17-5|6-8|Snap the Tail — The back foot strikes the tail down hard and fast, the ground pushes back, and the board tips nose-up and rises.
skt|9|l17-6|6-8|Slide the Front Foot Up — Right after the pop, the front foot drags up the grip tape toward the nose, pulling the board higher.
skt|9|l17-7|6-8|Jump and Pull Your Knees Up — After the pop, jump straight up and lift both knees toward your chest so the board has room to rise.
skt|9|l17-8|6-8|Level the Board — At the top of the jump, the front foot pushes the nose down while the back knee lifts, so the board flattens out.
skt|9|l17-9|6-8|Land Over the Bolts — Land with both feet over the bolts and bend your knees, which spreads the landing over more time and lowers the force.
skt|9|l17-10|6-8|Standing Still on Grass — Practice the ollie standing still on grass or carpet, one step at a time, until the order of moves feels natural.
skt|9|l17-11|6-8|Rolling Ollies — Once the still ollie works, try it rolling slowly with your shoulders level and your weight centered.
skt|9|l17-12|6-8|Fixing Common Problems — Most ollie problems come from the timing of the pop and slide or from where your weight sits, and each has a fix.
skt|9|l18-1|6-8|A Flat Spin Under Your Feet — In a pop shove-it, the board spins half a turn flat under you, around a vertical axis, while your body jumps straight up.
skt|9|l18-2|6-8|The Scoop — For a backside pop shove-it, the back foot pops and scoops the tail behind you while the front foot lifts out of the way.
skt|9|l18-3|6-8|Practicing the Shove-it — Learn the shove-it on grass first, then rolling slowly, and fix problems one at a time.
skt|9|l18-4|6-8|Kickflip Foot Position — For a kickflip, the back foot sits on the tail as for an ollie, and the front foot sits angled just behind the front bolts.
skt|9|l18-5|6-8|The Flick — The front foot slides up and flicks off the toe-side corner of the nose, giving a torque that rolls the board around its long axis.
skt|9|l18-6|6-8|Kickflip Problems and Fixes — Most kickflip problems come from flicking too hard or too soft, not jumping high enough, or leaning back.
skt|9|l18-7|6-8|Heelflip Foot Position — For a heelflip, the front foot points across the board with the toes slightly over the toe-side edge, so the heel can reach the other edge.
skt|9|l18-8|6-8|The Heel Flick — Pop, then kick the front heel forward and out off the heel-side corner of the nose, and let the board roll.
skt|9|l18-9|6-8|Telling Flips Apart — Flip tricks are named by which part of the foot flicks and which edge gets the push, and they can be mixed with shove-it spins.
skt|9|l18-10|6-8|The Three Axes of a Board — Every board trick turns around one or more of three axes: the long axis, the short axis and the vertical axis.
skt|9|l18-11|6-8|Catching the Board — Catching means stopping the board's turn with your feet as the grip tape comes back up, then landing over the bolts.
skt|9|l18-12|6-8|Practicing Flips Slowly — Practice flip motions slowly and in parts, on grass and holding a support, before trying them rolling.
